\operatorname{Ends}(M)=\operatorname{Ends}_{\infty}(M)
,
\operatorname{Ends}(M^{\prime})=\operatorname{Ends}_{\infty}(M^{\prime})
and both of these spaces are homeomorphic to
C
, and

•

the Veech groups of
M
and
M^{\prime}
are conjugated to
P
and
P^{\prime}
respectively.

Proof.

We only provide a sketch of the proof to illustrate the main ideas.
First, let’s consider the case where
C
is just one point (
i
.
e
., when the surfaces are homeomorphic to a Loch Ness monster) and discuss the key points of the general case.
The construction starts from the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We consider the family
L^{+}
of horizontal segments

\ell^{+}_{i}:=[((4i-1),0),(4i,0)]
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
M_{P}
be the tame translation surface obtained by gluing the segment
l^{+}_{2i-1}
to the segment
l^{+}_{2i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Given that
L^{+}
is a discrete family of parallel segments contained in the real axis, the surface
M_{P}
is a tame Loch Ness monster. Moreover, its Veech group is precisely
P
because the set of all holonomy vectors of
M_{P}
is
\{\pm e_{1}\}
and there are no saddle connections on the half plane
x<0
. To produce a Loch Ness monster with Veech group
P^{\prime}
consider on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
the additional families of parallel segments
L^{-}
made of
\ell_{i}^{-}:=[((1-4i),0),(-4i,0)]
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
and define
M_{P^{\prime}}
as the surface
obtained by gluing
l^{+}_{2i-1}
to
l^{+}_{2i}
and
l^{-}_{2i-1}
to
l^{-}_{2i}
, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The Veech group of
M_{P^{\prime}}
is
P^{\prime}
for reasons that are analogous to the ones presented for the surface
M_{P}
.

The general case follows from
[
RMV17
, construction 1.1]
. This construction takes as input data a closed subset
C
of the Cantor set and a tame Loch Ness monster
M
and produces a tame translation surface
M_{elem}
(called an
elementary piece
) satisfying
\operatorname{Ends}_{\infty}(M_{elem})=\operatorname{Ends}(M)=C
. When

M=M_{P}
(respectively
M_{P^{\prime}}
) it is possible to assure that
P
(respectively
P^{\prime}
) persists through the steps of the construction so that
\Gamma(M_{elem})=P
(respectively
\Gamma(M_{elem})=P^{\prime}
). The surface
M_{elem}
is obtained by a cut-and-paste construction similar to the one presented in the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
, the main difference being that instead of following the Cayley graph of a group as guide for the gluings one emulates the structure of a subtree
T_{C}
of the infinite regular tree of degree 3 which satisfies
Ends(T_{C})=C
.
∎

Question 3.2.14

Let
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
be a nested couple of closed subspaces of the Cantor set. Is it possible to realise the group
P
(respectively
P^{\prime}
) as Veech group of a tame translation surface
M
(respectively
M^{\prime}
) such that
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is homeomorphic to
\operatorname{Ends}_{\infty}(M)\subset\operatorname{Ends}(M)
?

3.3
Affine groups and Veech groups of coverings

We now study affine groups of translation coverings
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
where
M
is a finite-type translation surface (see Section
2.2
, Definition
2.2
). More precisely, we will study which affine elements of
M
can be
lifted
to
\widetilde{M}
; that is whether there exists
\widetilde{f}\in\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
so that
p\circ\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{f}\circ p
.

In Section
3.3.1
we present the general lifting criterion which follows from standard algebraic topology. Next, in Section
3.3.2
we study the case of Abelian coverings and discuss the importance of the affine group action on homology of the base surface. In Section
3.3.3
we focus on parabolic elements of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
that, as we already have seen in the Thurston-Veech construction from Section
1.2.6
, are intimately related to cylinder decompositions of
M
. Then, in Section
3.3.4
we prove a theorem due to Hooper and Weiss that provides a powerful method to show that the Veech group of a covering is non-elementary
4
4

4

Recall that a subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is called
elementary
if its limit set
\Lambda(G)\subset\partial\mathbb{H}
has at most two elements, see also Appendix
A
Finally, in Section
3.3.5

we apply these results to the wind-tree model.

Most statements concern translation surfaces though they
can be applied to half-translation surfaces
(see Section
1.1.4
) after an
appropriate double-covering construction.

3.3.1
The lifting criterion

Let us start with the general lifting criterion. Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering branched over
\Sigma\subset M
. In order to lift affine elements from
M
to
\widetilde{M}
, the branching locus
\Sigma
must be preserved. Hence we only deal with elements of the group
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
, which is the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
preserving the set
\Sigma
. The obstruction for a map
f
in
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
to be lifted to
\widetilde{M}
can be measured by its action on the fundamental group of
M\setminus\Sigma
. Recall that if
x_{0}
is any point in
M
then
f
induces a group morphism
f_{*}:\pi_{1}(M,x_{0})\to\pi_{1}(M,f(x_{0}))
. Given that
f
can change basepoints,
f
does not necessarily induces an automorphism of
\pi_{1}(M,x_{0})
. However, the action of
f
on conjugacy classes is well defined. To lighter notations, we will often write
\pi_{1}(M)
without reference to the base point when the properties we require are conjugacy invariant.

Theorem 3.3.1
(Lifting criterion)

Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering with branching locus
\Sigma\subset M
and define
\widetilde{\Sigma}:=p^{-1}(\Sigma)
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
(i.e.,
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
and
f(\Sigma)=\Sigma
). Then there exists a lift
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{M}\to\widetilde{M}
, (i.e.,
f\circ p=p\circ\widetilde{f}
on
\widetilde{M}
) if and only if the induced outer
5
5

5

Recall that outer automorphism group of a group, G, is the quotient,
\operatorname{Aut}(G)/\operatorname{Inn}(G)
, where
\operatorname{Aut}(G)
is the automorphism group of G and
\operatorname{Inn}(G)
is the subgroup consisting of inner automorphisms.
morphism
f_{*}\in\operatorname{Out}(\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma))
preserves the conjugacy class of
p_{*}(\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}\setminus\widetilde{\Sigma}))
in
\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)
.

Proof.

From Proposition 1.33, p. 61 in
[
Hat02
]
we have the following topological criterion:

Lemma 3.3.2
(Topological lifting criterion)

Let
S
be a topological surface,
p:\widetilde{S}\to S
a covering map and
f:S\to S
a continuous function. Let
x_{0}\in S
,
x_{1}=f(x_{0})
and
\tilde{x}_{0}\in p^{-1}(x_{0})
,
\tilde{x}_{1}\in p^{-1}(x_{1})
. Then there exists a lift
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{S}
satisfying
\widetilde{f}(\tilde{x}_{0})=\tilde{x}_{1}
if and only if the induced morphisms
f_{*}:\pi_{1}(S,x_{0})\to\pi_{1}(S,x_{1})
,
p_{0*}:\pi_{1}(\widetilde{S},\tilde{x}_{0})\to\pi_{1}(S,x_{0})
and
p_{1*}:\pi_{1}(\widetilde{S},\tilde{x}_{1})\to\pi_{1}(S,x_{1})
on the corresponding fundamental groups satisfy

f_{*}p_{0*}\pi_{1}(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{x_{0}})\subset p_{1*}\pi_{1}(\widetilde{S},\widetilde{x_{1}})

In order to apply the above Lemma, we fix a basepoint
x_{0}
in
M^{0}=M\setminus\Sigma
and let
x_{1}
be its image under
f
. Since
f
is an element of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
the image
x_{1}
is not in
\Sigma
. Now, a pair of points
\tilde{x}_{0}
and
\tilde{x}_{1}
in
\widetilde{M}^{0}
exist if and only if
f
preserves the conjugacy class of
(p_{0})_{*}(\pi_{1}(\widetilde{M}^{0},\tilde{x}_{0}))
. If such a lifting exists, it can be uniquely extended to
\widetilde{M}
in a continuous way.
∎

Exercise 3.3.3

Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering. We define the subgroups
G_{1}(p)\subset\operatorname{Aff}(M)
and
G_{2}(p)\subset\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
as follows

\displaystyle G_{1}(p)

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M):\text{there exists $\widetilde{f}\in\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})$ such that $p\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ p$}\}

(3.4)

\displaystyle G_{2}(p)

\displaystyle:=\{\widetilde{f}\in\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M}):\text{there exists $f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)$ such that $p\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ p$}\}

(3.5)

1.

Prove that the composition with
p
provides a surjective map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}G_{2}(p)&\to&G_{1}(p)\\
\tilde{f}&\mapsto&p\circ\tilde{f}\end{array}.

2.

Show that the kernel of the map
G_{2}(p)\to G_{1}(p)
considered above is
\operatorname{Deck}(p)
.

3.

Assume that
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
is a normal cover, that is
\operatorname{Deck}(p)
acts transitively on the fibers of
p
. Then prove that
G_{2}(p)
is the normalizer of
\operatorname{Deck}(p)
in
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
(
i
.
e
.,
\tilde{f}
in
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
belongs to
G_{2}(p)
if and only if
\tilde{f}\operatorname{Deck}(p)\tilde{f}^{-1}=\operatorname{Deck}(p)
).

4.

Prove that if
\tilde{f}\in G_{2}(p)
maps to
f\in G_{1}(p)
then the derivatives
D(\tilde{f})
and
D(f)
are equal.

Definition 3.3.4

Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering. The
relative Veech group

\Gamma(p)
or
\Gamma(\widetilde{M}\to M)

of the covering
p
is the group
D(G_{1}(p))=D(G_{2}(p))
, that is, the image by the derivative homomorphism of the groups
G_{1}(p)
and
G_{2}(p)
defined in (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).

The following direct consequence of Theorem
5
is already a rich source of examples.

Corollary 3.3.5

Let
M
be a translation surface,
\Sigma\subset M
a discrete subset and
M^{0}=M\setminus\Sigma
. Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering defined by a characteristic subgroup
6
6

6

A subgroup
H
of a group
G
is
characteristic
if for any automorphism
\phi:G\to G
we have
\phi(H)=H
.
of
\pi_{1}(M^{0})
. Then every element of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
lifts to
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
.
In particular,
\Gamma(M,\Sigma)\subset\Gamma(\widetilde{M})
.

Example 3.3.6

Let
G
be a group. We consider the following families of subgroups of
G
:

1.

The lower central series:

\gamma_{0}(G)\unrhd\gamma_{1}(G)\unrhd\ldots\unrhd\gamma_{n}(G)\unrhd\ldots

(3.6)

where
\gamma_{0}(G):=G
and
\gamma_{n+1}(G):=[\gamma_{n}(G),G]
for each
n>0
.

2.

The derived series:

\delta_{0}(G)\unrhd\delta_{1}(G)\unrhd\ldots\unrhd\delta_{n}(G)\unrhd\ldots

(3.7)

where
\delta_{0}(G)=G
and
\delta_{n+1}(G):=[\delta_{n}(G),\delta_{n}(G)]
for
n>0
.

3.

The subgroup of
n
-th powers:

G^{n}:=\langle g^{n}:g\in G\rangle

(3.8)

It is not difficult to check that all subgroups of
G
listed above are characteristic. In particular Corollary
3.3.1
applies. Let us consider the case of square-tiled surfaces where everything can be made more explicit.

Corollary 3.3.7

Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
be a square-tiled surface determined by a subgroup of
F_{2}\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
in one of the families (
3.6
), (
3.7
) or (
3.8
) defined above. Then every element of
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\{0\})\simeq\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
lifts to
\widetilde{M}
, in particular
\Gamma(\widetilde{M})
contains
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, if
p
is ramified then
\Gamma(\widetilde{M})=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement directly follows from Corollary
3.3.1
. The Veech group of the torus with a marked point is
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. In particular the Veech group of
\widetilde{M}
contains
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. However, it might not be equal to it: the Euclidean plane admits affine automorphisms that permute the fibers of the covering map
p:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
. When the covering is ramified, the fact that the covering subgroup is characteristic ensures that the corners of each square in
\widetilde{M}
are conical singularities (when the ramification order is finite) or punctures that gives rise to infinite angle singularity in the metric completion (when the order is infinite). All other points of
\widetilde{M}
are regular. Hence, any element of
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
preserves
\widehat{p}^{-1}(\{0\})
, where
\widehat{p}
is the unique extension of
p
to the metric completion (see Definition
2.2
and the paragraph that follows). As a consequence every element of
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
descends to
M
.
∎

To illustrate let us consider the subgroup
\gamma_{1}(F_{2})=\delta_{1}(F_{2})=[F_{2},F_{2}]
of the fundamental group
F_{2}=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
. The associated (non-ramified) covering space
p:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-covering, because
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=F_{2}/\delta_{1}(F_{2})
. Now, recall from Definition
2.2
in Chapter
2
that the corresponding translation covering is obtained by adding the conical singularities and regular points in the metric completion of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to which
p
can be extended continuously. In the case of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the metric completion is obtained by adding
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which are all regular points and
p
is the (unramified) universal cover
\widehat{p}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
An other explicit example is given by the second group in the central series:
F_{2}/\gamma_{2}(F_{2})
is isomorphic to the
Heisenberg group

\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&a&c\\
0&1&b\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\hskip 2.84526pt|\hskip 2.84526pta,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},

More generally, subgroups of the central series gives rise to a nilpotent coverings.

The second group in the derived series
\delta_{2}(F_{2})
is however less explicit. The quotient
F_{2}/\delta_{2}(F_{2})
is called the
free metabelian group of rank 2
.

Exercise 3.3.8

1.

Show that for
n\geq 0
,
[\gamma_{n+1}(F_{2}):\gamma_{n}(F_{2})]=+\infty
and
[\delta_{n+1}(F_{2}):\gamma_{n}(F_{2})]=+\infty
.

2.

Show that the subgroups
\gamma_{n}(F_{2})
and
\delta_{n}(F_{2})
are pairwise distinct except:
\gamma_{0}(F_{2})=\delta_{0}(F_{2})=F_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}(F_{2})=\delta_{1}(F_{2})=[F_{2},F_{2}]
.

(
hint
: use the fact that subgroups of
F_{2}
are free groups)

The study of quotients by the
n
-th power subgroup is much more delicate and has a long history. The quotient
B(m,n):=F_{m}/(F_{m})^{n}
is called the
free
m
-generator Burnside group of exponent
n
(each element in
B(m,n)
has order at most
n
). It has been known for a while that for
n=2,3,4,6
these groups are finite. However,
it is a deep result that there exists
n_{0}
such that
B(2,n)
is infinite for every
n\geq n_{0}
(Adian and Novikov (1968), Ol’shanskiĭ (1982), Ivanov (1994), Lysenok (1996), Delzant and Gromov (2008), and Coulon (2018)). Let us also mention that it is still unknown whether
B(2,5)
is infinite. More recently, a versatile and easy-to-apply tool for constructing examples of finitely generated infinite
n
-periodic groups (such as the Burnside groups) has been developed by Coulon and Gruber, see
[
CG19
]
for details.

We now present two examples of coverings that are not translation coverings but for which the deck group acts by affine transformations. The first example are irrational billiards (for which the deck group acts by isometries) and the second one dilation surfaces (for which the Veech group acts by dilations).

Example 3.3.9

In Section
1.2.1
we discussed how to translate the dynamics of a billiard ball in a polygon
P
into the dynamics of the translation flow on a translation surface
M(P)
. Moreover, Theorem
1.2.1
proven in Example
2.2
shows that if
P
is an irrational polygon (
i
.
e
., none of its interior angles is commensurable with
\pi
) then
M(P)
is homeomorphic to the Loch Ness monster.

Theorem 3.3.10
(
[
Val12
]
)

Let
P
be a simply connected Euclidean polygon with interior angles
\{\lambda_{j}\pi\}_{j=1}^{N}
. If there exists
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
then the Veech group
\Gamma^{+}(M(P))
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, the subgroup of
\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})
generated by the rotations
z\to e^{2\lambda_{j}\pi}z
,
j=1,\ldots,N
is a subgroup of
\Gamma^{+}(M(P))
of finite index.

Proof.

We discuss the case of triangles
P
, for the case of a general polygon is analogous. From Section
1.2.1
and the proof of Theorem
1.2.1
presented in Example
2.2
we can deduce that there exists a non-ramified covering
p:M(P)\to\mathbb{S}^{2}(P)
whose base is a three-punctured sphere made of two copies of
P
(with the vertices removed) and whose deck transformation group is isometric to
\mathbb{Z}\times A
or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times A
(where
A
is a finite abelian group) depending on whether the rank of
P
is equal to 1 or 2, see (
1.8
) in Section
1.2.1
. Let us emphasize that here
\mathbb{S}^{2}(P)
is not a translation surfaces and
p
is not a translation covering.
The surface
\mathbb{S}^{2}(P)
has a
\operatorname{Isom}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-structure (see Appendix
B.2
). Let
\operatorname{hol}:\pi_{1}(M(P))\to\operatorname{Isom}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be its holonomy. Then the covering
p
is defined by the normal subgroup
\operatorname{hol}^{-1}(\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

By construction, the image of
\operatorname{hol}
is contained in the group generated by the reflections with respect to the sides of
P
. Let
R(P)
be the group generated by the linear part of the reflections with respect to the sides of
P
and
R^{+}(P)=R(P)\cap\operatorname{SO}(2)
. The deck group of
p
acts on the translation surface
M(P)
by affine homeomorphisms whose linear parts generate the group
R^{+}(P)
. We claim that actually
\Gamma^{+}(M(P))
is formed only by rotations. Indeed, in
M(P)
there are saddle connections of minimal length, say
L>0
, corresponding to geodesics of minimal length in the Euclidean surface
\mathbb{S}^{2}(P)
. Since one of the
\lambda_{j}
is irrational,
R^{+}(P)
contains an irrational rotation. In particular, the holonomy vectors of length
L
are dense in the circle
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=L\}
. Now, if we had an element
A\in\Gamma^{+}(M(P))
different from a rotation, then the image of
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=L\}
under
A
would be an ellipse containing infinitely many points corresponding to holonomy vectors of norm less than
L
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
L
is the minimal length of a saddle connection. To finish the proof remark that the set of holonomy vectors of minimal length of
M(P)
is of the form
\sqcup_{i=1}^{k}R^{+}(P)v_{i}
, where
v_{i}
are minimal length holonomy vectors and the group
\Gamma^{+}(M(P))/R^{+}(P)
acts freely on the set of
R^{+}(P)
-orbits of holonomy vectors of minimal length.
∎

Veech groups of translation coverings of dilation surfaces
. At the end of Section
2.2
we explained that each dilation surface
S
has a natural normal covering
\pi:\widetilde{S}\to S
defined by the inverse image of
\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
under the holonomy
\operatorname{hol}:\pi_{1}(S)\to\operatorname{Dil}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the
\operatorname{Dil}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-structure of
S
. The pullback of the dilation surface structure of
S
defines thus a translation surface structure on
\widetilde{S}
and the deck transformation group of
\pi
acts on this translation surface by dilations. In particular, if
\Delta
denotes the image of
\pi_{1}(S)\to\operatorname{Dil}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbb{R}^{*}
, where the second arrow is just the derivative map, then the Veech group of
\widetilde{S}
contains an element of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&0\\
0&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, for each
a\in\Delta
.

Exercise 3.3.11

Let
S
be a finite type dilation surface and
\pi:\widetilde{S}\to S
its canonical translation cover. Let
\operatorname{Aff}(S)
be the set of homeomorphisms of
S
which are affine in local coordinates. Show that:

1.

\operatorname{Aff}(S)
is a group.

2.

Every element in
\operatorname{Aff}(S)
lifts to an element of
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{S})
.

Veech groups can also be defined for dilation surfaces and have been studied in
[
DFG19
]
.
As with translation surfaces, the characterization of Fuchsian groups that are Veech groups of dilation surfaces is still unknown.

3.3.2
Abelian coverings and
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
-action on
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})

The lifting criterion, as stated in Theorem
5
, is defined
in terms of the action of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
on the fundamental group. In the case of
Abelian coverings this can naturally be reformulated in terms of the action
of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
on the relative homology space
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
.
The central theme of this section and the next one is to study this action.

First, let’s mention that the properties of the action of the affine group on

H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
are tightly linked to dynamical properties of translation flows or
foliations in
\widetilde{M}
(see for example Theorem
1.2.5

in Chapter
1
). Dynamical properties of Abelian coverings will be investigated
in a (second) forthcoming volume
[
DHV
]

dedicated mostly to dynamical aspects of infinite-type translation surfaces.

Recall from Lemma
2.2
that an Abelian covering of a translation surface
M
at most branched over
\Sigma
is determined by a subspace
V\subset H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
.
On the other hand, the group
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
has an induced action
on
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
. As a consequence of the lifting criterion
(Theorem
5
) we have the following.

Theorem 3.3.12
(Abelian lifting criterion)

Let
M
be a compact translation surface and
\Sigma\subset M
a finite set.
Let
V
be a vector subspace of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
and
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
the
Abelian translation covering it determines. Then an element
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
lifts to
\widetilde{M}
if and only
if
f_{*}V=V
.

In Example
3.3.1
, we already encountered an Abelian covering. Namely, the covering determined by the vector subspace
V=H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})

which corresponds to the derived subgroup
\delta_{1}(\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma))

of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)
. In this situation, all
elements of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
lift to
\widetilde{M}
(see
Corollary
3.3.1
).

In view of Theorem
3.3.2
, studying the affine groups of Abelian translation
covers of a compact surface
M
amounts to studying the stabilizers of vector subspaces of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
under the action of the affine group
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
.
An important subspace that is stabilized by
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
is the kernel of the holonomy
map, which we define as follows:

Definition 3.3.13

Let
M=(X,\omega)
be a finite-type translation surface and
\Sigma
be a finite subset in
M
(possibly empty). We define
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
as the kernel of the holonomy map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\rm hol}:H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})&\to&\mathbb{C}\\
c&\mapsto&\int_{c}\omega\end{array}.

When
\Sigma=\emptyset
, we will simply use the simpler notation
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
for

\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\emptyset)
.

Let us emphasize that in the definition of
\operatorname{kerhol}
we used the homology with
rational coefficients
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
and not
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. By the rank-nullity theorem, the kernel of
\operatorname{hol}:H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{C}
has always codimension 2. On the other hand, as we see
in Theorem
3.3.2
, the codimension of

\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is intimately related to the arithmetic properties of the
holomy vectors of
M
.

Exercise 3.3.14

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
\Sigma\subset M
a finite subset.

1.

Prove that
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
is invariant under the action of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
.

2.

Prove that all elements of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
lift to the Abelian covering
defined by
V=\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
(
hint:
use Theorem
3.3.2
).

Most Abelian coverings that we study in this book are defined by subspaces of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
. This is the case of the
infinite staircase: as it can be seen in Figure
2.3
in Example
2.2
this
\mathbb{Z}
-covering
is defined by the cycle
B-A\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
and
\operatorname{hol}(A)=\operatorname{hol}(B)=1
. It is also the case, for
the wind-tree models
W_{a,b}\to X_{a,b}
for any
(a,b)\in(0,1)\times(0,1)
. Let us consider Figure
1.15

in which the cycles
Z_{ij}
and
Y_{ij}
appear. Let us define the two absolute cycles

Y=Y_{00}+Y_{10}-Y_{01}-Y_{11}\quad\text{and}\quad Z=Z_{00}+Z_{01}-Z_{10}-Z_{11}

(3.9)

in
H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{Z})
. The subspace of
H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{Q})
generated by
Y
and
Z
defines the unramified covering
W_{a,b}\to X_{a,b}
.
To see that it belongs to
\operatorname{kerhol}(X_{a,b})
it is enough to notice that

\forall i,j\in\{0,1\},\quad\operatorname{hol}(Z_{i,j})=1\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{hol}(Y_{i,j})=\sqrt{-1}.

As shown in the following exercise, half-translation surfaces (or quadratic differentials)
provides examples of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-coverings defined by subspaces in
\operatorname{kerhol}
.

Exercise 3.3.16

Let
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}

a finite

.

Show that

with

\Sigma\subset\mathbb{Q}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

then there is a decomposition

Let
p:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
be a finite-type square-tiled surface.
Let

be the

p
of closed curves in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Show that
p_{*}:U_{M}\to H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{Q})
is an isomorphism and
that we have a decomposition

H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})=U_{M}\oplus\operatorname{kerhol}(M)

(3.11)

that is orthogonal with respect to the algebraic intersection form on
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})
.

Show that
U_{M}

\operatorname{Aff}(M)
-invariant.

5.

Let
M
be a finite-type square-tiled surface as before. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset M
be
a finite set contained in the preimage of the rational

M
. Show that

H_{1}(M,\Sigma^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})=U_{M}\oplus\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma^{\prime}).

(3.12)

We now study in full generality a finite-type surface
M
with non-elementary Veech group.
It will clarify the special role that
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
plays when considering the
Veech groups of a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering of
M
. The discussion requires
some technical concepts from compact translation surfaces. A reader that is not familiar
with these notions should not be afraid of not understanding everything as most of
the results developed here will not be used intensively in the following sections.

Recall that a subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is called non-elementary if
it contains two hyperbolic elements with disjoint axes (see Appendix
A
). By extension, we say that
the affine group
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
of a finite-type translation
surface is non-elementary if its image
\Gamma(M,\Sigma)
by the derivative homomorphism

D:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is non-elementary (since we assume that

M
is finite-type, the affine elements necessarily preserve the area and hence
image is necessarily in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
).

An important source of examples of finite-type translation surface
M

with non-elementary Veech groups are the ones obtained from the
Thurston-Veech construction (see Section
1.2.6
).
In this case, the Veech group
\Gamma(M)
contains two parabolic elements
with distinct fixed points in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Among these Thurston-Veech
surfaces, there are some very exceptional ones for which the Veech group
\Gamma(M)

is of finite covolume: these are the so-called Veech surfaces. Finite-type
square-tiled surfaces (see Example
2.2
) are Veech
surfaces and their Veech groups are commensurable with
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
An other example of surface with non-elementary Veech group but which does not
come from a Thurston-Veech construction is the
Arnoux-Yoccoz surface
[
HLM09
]
. We terminate our list with mentioning a general construction from
[
HS04
]
, for which we need some
definitions.

Definition 3.3.17

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
v_{1},v_{2}
two non-parallel vectors in
\operatorname{hol}(H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}))
. The smallest subfield
k
of
\mathbb{R}
for which any element in
\operatorname{hol}(H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}))
can be written as
av_{1}+bv_{2}
,
a,b\in k
is called the
holonomy field
of
M
.

Equivalently, let
T^{id}_{M}:=\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)+\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)\subset H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
be the

tautological plane
. Then
k
as defined above is also the field of definition of
T^{id}_{M}
, that is,
the smallest field such that one can write
T^{id}_{M}
as a kernel of a matrix with
coefficients in
k
(in a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})
). A direct consequFence of these definitions is that the Veech group of a finite type translation surface
M
with holonomy field
k
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(2,k)
.

Theorem 3.3.18
(
[
KS00
]
)

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface with holonomy field
k
and suppose that there exists a hyperbolic element
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
, then
k=\mathbb{Q}[\operatorname{tr}(D(f))]
.

For a more detailed discussion on holonomy fields and its applications we refer the reader also to
[
GJ00
]
,
[
HL06a
]

and
[
McM03
]
. For a discussion about how the concept of holonomy field can be extended to infinite-type surfaces and some new phenomena that appear in this context see Ñ
[
VWS14
]
. Remark that if
M
is a square-tiled surface then its holonomy field is
k=\mathbb{Q}
and
U_{M}
, the subspace of
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})
defined in Exercise
3.3.2
, is the Poincaré dual
8
8

8

If
D:H^{1}(M)\to H_{1}(M)
is the isomorphism given by Poincaré-duality, then we say that a subspace
W\subset H_{1}(M)
is the Poincaré dual of
V\subset H^{1}(M)
if
D^{-1}(W)=V
.
of the tautological plane
T^{id}_{M}
.

Definition 3.3.19

Let
M
be a compact translation surface with holonomy field
k
and
\Sigma
be its
set of conical singularities. Let

\Lambda=\{\int_{\gamma}\omega:\gamma\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})\}
.
A point in
M
is called
k
-rational

if the holonomy of any path from
x
to a point in
\Sigma
belongs to
\Lambda

Definition 3.3.20

Let
M
be a surface with non-elementary Veech group.
A point
x
in
M
is called

periodic
if its stabilizer
in
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
has finite index (or equivalently, if its
orbit is finite).

For square-tiled surfaces, the rational points are the elements of
\mathbb{Q}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}

which are all periodic. This justifies our terminology. For a surface with
non-elementary Veech group the periodic points are rational points, however
the converse is not true: in a Veech surface the set of periodic
points is finite
[
GHS03
,
M0̈6
]
. The fact
that there exist rational points which are not periodic can be used to construct
finite-type translation surfaces whose Veech group do not admit a finite set
of generators, see
[
HS04
]
.

We now discuss how the homology of a translation surface with non-elementary Veech group decomposes, but first let us discuss and recall some useful facts.

For each embedding
\sigma:k\to\mathbb{R}
or a pair of complex conjugate embeddings

\sigma,\overline{\sigma}:k\to\mathbb{C}
we can define a Galois conjugate

T_{M}^{\sigma}\subset H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
of the tautological plane by applying
\sigma

on the defining equations of
T_{M}^{id}
. For real embeddings
T_{M}^{\sigma}

has (real) dimension 2 while for a pair of complex conjugate embeddings it has (real) dimension 4.

Recall also that for any compact translation surface
M
of genus
g
the algebraic intersection number:

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\times H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}

extends uniquely to a symplectic form on
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, if
\eta_{a},\eta_{b}
are the Poincaré duals of
a,b\in H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
then
\langle a,b\rangle=\int_{M}\eta_{a}\wedge\eta_{b}
. On the other hand, the natural action of
\operatorname{MCG}(M)
, the mapping class group of
M
on
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
defines a (surjective)
symplectic
representation:

\operatorname{MCG}(M)\to\operatorname{Sp}(H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}))

We refer the reader to Chapter 6 in
[
FM12
]
for a more detailed dicussion.

Theorem 3.3.21

Let
M
be a compact translation surface whose affine group is
non-elementary and
k
be its holonomy field. Then
each subspace
T_{M}^{\sigma}
is symplectic and
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
-invariant.
There is direct sum decomposition orthogonal with respect to
the intersection form

T_{M}:=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Emb}(k)}T_{M}^{\sigma}

(3.13)

where
\operatorname{Emb}(k)
is the set of real embeddings and pairs of complex conjugate
embeddings of
k
.

The Poincaré dual of
T_{M}
is a subspace of
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and the
anihilator of
T_{M}
is exactly
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
.
As a consequence
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is
symplectic and, if we henceforth identify
T_{M}
to its Poincaré dual in
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})
(and keep the notation
T_{M}
) we have a direct sum decomposition orthogonal with respect
to the algebraic intersection form

H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})=T_{M}\oplus(\operatorname{kerhol}(M)\otimes\mathbb{R})

(3.14)

Any subspace
V\subset H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
that has a non-elementary
stabilizer in
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
decomposes as

V=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in I}T_{M}^{\sigma}\oplus(\operatorname{kerhol}(M)\otimes\mathbb{R}\cap V)

(3.15)

where
I\subset\operatorname{Emb}(k)
.

One of the main ingredients in the proof of the above result is the following Theorem of Thurston:

Theorem 3.3.22

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)

a hyperbolic element. Then the dominant eigenvalue of
D(f)

is also the dominant eigenvalue of the action of
f
in

H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, this eigenvalue is simple.

Proof.

We provide a proof only for the case when
k
is totally real. The
general case introduce some technical details
that would obfuscate the proof.

First of all, the
action of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
on the tautological plane
T_{M}^{id}=\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)\oplus\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
identifies with the derivative on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed, by definition of
the affine action

\begin{pmatrix}f^{*}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)\\
f^{*}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}=D(f)\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)\\
\operatorname{Im}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}.

The restriction of the symplectic form
\langle\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\rangle=\int_{M}\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}

on the tautological plane is non-degenerate since
\int_{M}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)\wedge\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
is the
area of the surface

More generally, for each embedding
\sigma:k\to\mathbb{R}
, the induced action of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
on

T_{M}^{\sigma}
identifies with the action of
\sigma\circ D
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This shows
that each
T_{M}^{\sigma}
is symplectic and
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
-invariant.

We now prove that the sum in (
3.14
) is direct and symplectic orthogonal. Let us consider the fact that the holonomy field is the field
generated by the traces of the matrices
D(f)
for
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
, see Theorem
3.3.2
. Because

\operatorname{Aff}(M)
is non-elementary, it admits a hyperbolic element.
Its trace
t=\operatorname{tr}(D(f))
is a generator of
k
and hence the
embeddings
\sigma(t)
are pairwise distinct. The eigenvalues
of
D(f)
is a pair
\lambda
,
1/\lambda
such that

t=\lambda+1/\lambda
. Moreover
\lambda
is simple as
an eigenvalue of the induced action
f_{*}:H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
, see Theorem
3.3.2
. Hence

T_{M}^{Id}=E_{\lambda}(f_{*})\oplus E_{1/\lambda}(f_{*})
where
E_{\lambda}(f_{*})

and
E_{1/\lambda}(f_{*})
are the eigenspaces of
f_{*}
with eigenvalues

\lambda
and
1/\lambda
.
This is also true for each Galois conjugate:

T_{M}^{\sigma}=E_{\sigma(\lambda)}(f_{*})\oplus E_{1/\sigma(\lambda)}(f_{*})
.
Because the eigenspaces are in direct sums, the sum in (
3.13
)
is direct. Since the matrix
f_{*}
is symplectic, this sum is symplectic orthogonal
9
9

9

If
M
is a symplectic matrix (preserving a symplectiv form
\omega
) and
\lambda,\mu
are eigenvalues such that
\lambda\mu\neq 1
then for every
\lambda
-eigenvector
v
and
\mu
-eigenvector
w
we have that
\omega(v,w)=\omega(Mv,Mw)=\lambda\mu\omega(v,w)
and hence
\omega(v,w)=0
.
.

Since
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subspace of
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
it
is also anihilated by the
T_{M}^{\sigma}
. The direct sum
in equation (
3.14
) follows.

Now we want to prove the last part of the Theorem. Let
V
be a subspace
of
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
with a non-elementary stabilizer
G
under the action of

\operatorname{Aff}(M)
. Because
G
is non-elementary, it contains two hyperbolic elements
f_{1}

and
f_{2}
such that their derivatives
D(f_{1})
and
D(f_{2})
have no common
fixed points on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
(see Theorem
A.1
).
Equivalently, the derivatives
D(f_{1})
and
D(f_{2})
have no common eigenspace.
Hence, the only invariant subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
preserved by both
D(\operatorname{Aff}(M))

are only the trivial subspaces
0
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The same property holds for
any
T_{M}^{\sigma}
.

Now the invariant subspace
V
must decompose along the eigenspaces of any
element in its stabilizer. Since the decompositions in eigenspaces of
f_{1}

and
f_{2}
lead to distinct decompositions of
T_{M}^{\sigma}
, we obtain
the decomposition (
3.15
).
∎

Corollary 3.3.23

Let
M
be finite type translation surface and
\Sigma\subset M
a finite set
such that
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
is non-elementary. Let
T_{M}=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Emb}(k)}T_{M}^{\sigma}

as in Theorem
3.3.2

Let
V
be a vector subspace of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
. If the
stabilizer of
V
in
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
is non-elementary
then either
V
contains
10
10

10

Here we are abusing notation by writing
T_{M}\subset H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Q})
for the Poincaré dual of
T_{M}
as defined in Theorem
3.3.2
.

T_{M}
or
V
is contained in
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
.

In the particular case of
\mathbb{Z}
-coverings, that is
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}V=1
, only the second
possibility occurs.

Proof.

The decomposition (
3.14
)
from Theorem
3.3.2

is also valid for relative homology

H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Emb}(k)}T_{M}^{\sigma}\oplus\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma).

(3.16)

The reason is that the class of
\operatorname{Re}(\omega)
and
\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
are well
defined in
H^{1}(M\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
which by Poincaré duality
can be identified to subspaces of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
.

Now, the second part of Theorem
3.3.2
shows that an
invariant subspace must respect the decomposition (
3.16
).
Since
V
is assumed to be defined over
\mathbb{Q}
it must either contain the whole sum

\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\operatorname{Emb}(k)}T_{M}^{\sigma}
(that is the smallest subspace defined
over
\mathbb{Q}
that contains
T_{M}^{id}
) or be contained in
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)

With the additional hypothesis that
\dim_{Q}V=1
, it can not contain

T_{M}
which has non-zero even dimension.
∎

Let us mention two important elementary results about
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
.
The first one concerns the behavior under finite coverings.

Lemma 3.3.24

Let
M
be a compact translation surface with non-elementary Veech
group,
k
its holonomy field and

\Sigma\subset M
a finite set of
k
-rational points. Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
a finite
covering branched over
\Sigma
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}:=p^{-1}(\Sigma)
. Then

p_{*}:H_{1}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma};\mathbb{Q})\to H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
induces
an isomorphism between
T_{\widetilde{M}}
and
T_{M}
as defined in Theorem
3.3.2
; moreover

\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)=\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma})

(where the codimensions are respectively measured in
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})

and
H_{1}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma};\mathbb{Q})
).

The second lemma states that the surfaces for which
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is maximal
are exactly the square-tiled surfaces.

Lemma 3.3.25

Let
M
be a compact translation surface and
\Sigma\subset M
a (possibly
empty) finite set of points. Then
\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)=2
(where the
codimension is taken inside
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
) if and only if
p:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
is a
square-tiled surface and
\Sigma\subset p^{-1}(0)
.

Proof of Lemma
3.3.2
.

Let
\omega
and
\widetilde{\omega}
be respectively the one forms on
M
and
\widetilde{M}
. By definition of translation covering,
\widetilde{\omega}=p^{*}\omega
. It follows that
T_{\widetilde{M}}
and
T_{M}
are isomorphic.

Let us define

L:=\left\{\int_{\gamma}\omega:\ \gamma\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{L}:=\left\{\int_{\gamma}\widetilde{\omega}:\ \gamma\in H_{1}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma};\mathbb{Z})\right\}.

By paths projection and paths lifting we have that

\widetilde{L}\subset L\subset\frac{1}{\deg(p)}\widetilde{L}.

In particular
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\widetilde{L}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.
Now, by definition of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
and
\operatorname{kerhol}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma})
, we have that

\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)=\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb{Z}}L
and

\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\Sigma})=\operatorname{rk}_{\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{L}
from which the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3.2
.

As in the proof Lemma
3.3.2
, let us consider

L:=\left\{\int_{\gamma}\omega:\ \gamma\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}.

Fix a reference point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
if
\Sigma
is non-empty and any point in
M
otherwise.
Then consider the period mapping

P:\begin{array}[]{ccc}M&\to&\mathbb{C}/L\\
x&\mapsto&\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\omega.\end{array}.

The map is well defined since two paths from
x_{0}
to
x
differ by an
element in absolute homology.

Now
\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)=2
if and only if
L
is a lattice in
\mathbb{C}
.
Hence if
\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)=2
, the map
P
provides a translation covering with
a torus target. And by construction, all points of
\Sigma
are mapped to
0
in the
quotient.

The proof of the converse is a particular case of Lemma
3.3.2
.
∎

A “generic” surface
M
in a given stratum
\mathcal{H}(\kappa)
of Abelian differentials
satisfies
\dim\operatorname{kerhol}(M)=0
. However, Lemmas
3.3.2

and
3.3.2
show that when
M
comes from a covering
then
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is non-trivial.

Exercise 3.3.26

Let
L
be the L-shaped surface made from 3 squares as in Figure
3.2
(after identifying opposite sides using translations)
and let
\alpha=\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}
and
\beta=\gamma_{3}-\gamma_{4}
(as elements
of the absolute homology
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
).

Figure 3.2
:

1.

Show that
\operatorname{kerhol}(L)=\mathbb{Q}\alpha\oplus\mathbb{Q}\beta
,

2.

Show that
L
admits no translation automorphism (so that we can identify
the Veech group and the affine group).

3.

Show that the following three matrices belong to the Veech group of
L

s=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad h_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad v_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
2&1\end{pmatrix}.

(actually these 3 matrices generate the Veech group)

4.

Let
\rho:\operatorname{Aff}(M)\to\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{kerhol}(M))
. Show that in the basis
(\alpha,\beta)

of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
one has

\rho(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\rho(h_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\rho(v_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}.

5.

Conclude that the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(L)
that consists of elements that act
trivially on
\operatorname{kerhol}(L)
is a non-trivial normal subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(L)
of infinite
index. Does it contain a parabolic element?

Affine action on absolute and relative homologies

In this section we study the differences between the action of affine groups on
absolute and relative homology groups.

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
\Sigma\subset M
be a finite
set so that
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
is non-elementary. Recall that:

•

both
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)

and
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
are
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
-invariant subgroups of

H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
(see Exercise
3.3.2
).

•

To any discrete (
i
.
e
., Fuchsian) subgroup
\Gamma
of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
one can associate its limit set
\Lambda(\Gamma)\subset\partial\mathbb{H}
: this is the set of limit points of
\Gamma z
for any
z\in\mathbb{H}
. For more details see Appendix
A
.

Theorem 3.3.27
(
[
HW12
]
)

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and let

\psi:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to GL(\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma))

and

\psi_{0}:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to GL(\operatorname{kerhol}(M))

be the induced actions. Then,
\ker(\psi)
is a normal
subgroup of
\ker(\psi_{0})
and the quotient is virtually Abelian of rank at most
r_{0}(r-r_{0})

where
r_{0}=\dim(\operatorname{kerhol}(M))
and
r=\dim(\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma))
.

In particular, if
\Gamma(M)
is non-elementary and
\ker(\psi)
is
non-trivial then

\Lambda(D\ker\psi)=\Lambda(D\ker\psi_{0})=\Lambda\Gamma(M),

where
D:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
denotes the derivative.

Proof.

Because
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)\subset\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
, in an appropriate basis of

\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
,
\psi
has the form

\psi(f)=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0}(f)&\alpha(f)\\
0&\sigma(f)\end{pmatrix}

(3.17)

where
\sigma:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to\operatorname{GL}(V)
is the action induced by permutations
on
V=\{v\in\mathbb{Q}^{\Sigma}:\sum v_{i}=0\}
and
\alpha(f)
is some
r_{0}\times(r-r_{0})
matrix with integer coefficients.

Hence,
\ker(\sigma)
is of finite index (the image of
\sigma
is a permutation
group) and the image of
\alpha
is an Abelian group whose rank is given by
the dimensions of the matrix:
r_{0}(r-r_{0})
.

The last part of the proof concerning the limit sets is a direct application of
Lemma
A.1
from the Appendix
A
.
∎

Exercise 3.3.28

This exercise is a continuation of Exercise
3.3.2
.
Recall that
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\{0\})\simeq\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Instead of considering
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\Sigma)
(that can admit translations) we consider subgroups of
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\{0\})
that we identify to subgroups of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Let
n
be a positive integer and denote
\Sigma_{n}
the set of points
x
in
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that such that

. A point in
\Sigma_{n}
is called a
n
-torsion point
.

1.

Show that
\Sigma_{n}

(\mathbb{T}^{2},+)
isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.

2.

Show that
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
preserves
\Sigma_{n}
.

The
principal congruence subgroup
of degree
n
is

\Gamma(n):=\{A\in\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z}):A\equiv Id\mod n\}

3.

Show that
\Gamma(n)
is the pointwise stabilizer of
\Sigma_{n}
,
i
.
e
.,
As=s
for each
s
in
\Sigma_{n}
.

Recall that we have a map
\delta:H_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\Sigma_{n};\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{n}}
which to a relative cycle associates its signed boundary. On the other hand
\Sigma_{n}
is a group and we have a map

which to a formal linear combination of points associates its sum in

. We denote

4.

Show that

.

Show that

coincide with the composition of

.

6.

Deduce from the two previous items that
\Gamma(n)
preserves

and that for each element in
\Gamma(n)
its induced action on
\operatorname{kerhol}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\Sigma_{n})

Deduce that if
V\subset\operatorname{kerhol}(\mathbb{T}^{2},\Sigma_{n})

the translation Abelian covering associated to

then the affine group of
