contains
\Gamma(n)
.

In the above exercise, we have seen a family of examples where the affine group

\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
acts via a finite group on
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
. However, as shown
in the following example this is not the case in general.

Example 3.3.29

We now present an example of a finite-type square-tiled surface
Z_{3,1}
made of 4 squares in the stratum
\mathcal{H}(1,1)
of
Abelian differentials. We compute explicitly the action of
\operatorname{Aff}(Z_{3,1})=\operatorname{Aff}(Z_{3,1},\Sigma)
on

H_{1}(Z_{3,1};\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{1}(Z_{3,1},\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
where
\Sigma
is the set made of the two conical
singularities of
O
. We use the notation introduced in the proof of Theorem
3.3.2
.

Figure 3.3
:
The
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
-orbit of the square-tiled surface
Z_{3,1}
(shown here in the middle). The plain arrows correspond to the
action of the horizontal parabolic element
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, while the dotted ones to the rotation by
\pi/2
.

The Veech group (which in that case identifies to the affine group) is
an index 4 subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
generated by the matrices

g_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad g_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\quad g_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-3\\
1&-2\end{pmatrix}.

Then in the basis
11
11

11

Oriented in the Figure from left to right and bottom to top.

\{2\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3},2\alpha_{2}-2\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2}-\beta_{1},\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}\}
of
\operatorname{kerhol}(Z_{3,1},\Sigma)
depicted
in Figure
3.3
the map
\psi_{0}
is given by

\psi_{0}(g_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\psi_{0}(g_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}-2&1&1\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\psi_{0}(g_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}-2&3&1\\
-1&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

One can show that for

g=\begin{pmatrix}37&60\\
8&13\end{pmatrix}=g_{1}g_{3}(g_{2})^{2}g_{3}g_{2}

then
\alpha(g)=(-1,0)
. Which shows that
\ker(\psi)
and
\ker(\psi_{0})

are different. Moreover, taking its conjugate

g^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}13&24\\
20&37\end{pmatrix}=g_{2}g_{3}g_{2}g_{1}g_{3}g_{2}
we have
\alpha(g^{\prime})=(-1,-1)
. Hence, the Abelian group
of Theorem
3.3.2
has rank
2
in this example (the maximal possible).

Let us also mention that this example shows that the
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
-invariant subspace

H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
inside
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
does not necessarily admits a complement that is
also invariant.

3.3.3
Lifting parabolic elements in
G
-coverings

In this section we study parabolic elements in more detail. Because a parabolic element
in the affine group of a finite-type translation surface is associated to a cylinder decomposition
their properties are often much easier to understand than for hyperbolic elements.
As we will see in the next chapters, cylinders are extremely useful to study recurrence
and ergodicity of coverings.

Cylinders and strips in translation surfaces were defined in Definition
1.1.5
. First, let’s consider the general question of preimages of cylinders.
Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a translation covering. Then, the preimages in
\widetilde{M}
of cylinders in
M
are either cylinders or strips. The following statement
gives a precise description.

Lemma 3.3.30

Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be a
G
-covering branched over
\Sigma
and determined
by the kernel of the morphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)\to G
. Let
C
be
a maximal cylinder in
M\setminus\Sigma
with core curve
\gamma
seen as a conjugacy
class in
\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)
.
Let
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,\}\cup\{\infty\}
be the order of
\rho(\gamma)
in
G
. Then,
if
k
is finite, the preimage of
C
in
\widetilde{M}
consists of a disjoint collection of copies of cylinders
with same height and whose circumference is
k
-times longer than the circumference of
C
. If
k

is infinite, the preimage of
C
consists of a disjoint union of strips of the same height as
C
.

Proof.

The argumentation is based on the path-lifting property from the theory
of covering spaces. Namely, let us fix a basepoint
x
on the curve
\gamma
.
Then this curve can be
lifted from any point
\widetilde{x}
in the preimage of
x
. The endpoint
of the lifting is then
\widetilde{x}\cdot\gamma
where
\cdot
represents
the right action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma,x)

on the fiber of
x
. Repeating this lifting from
\widetilde{x}\cdot\gamma

we end up in
\widetilde{x}\cdot(\gamma)^{2}
, etc. This procedure comes back
for the first time to
\widetilde{x}
in
k
steps if and only if the order of

\rho(\gamma)
is
k
. This proves the part of the Lemma concerning the circumferences.
The preimage of a cylinder
C
in
\widetilde{M}
has the same height because
the lift of a saddle connection in the boundary of
C
is a saddle connections (of the same length).
∎

Definition 3.3.31

Let
M
be a translation or half-translation surface. We say that
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
is:

1.

completely periodic
if there exists a collection of maximal cylinders
\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
parallel to
\theta
such that
\cup_{i\in I}C_{i}
is dense in
M
. Such a collection of cylinders is called a
cylinder decomposition
in direction
\theta
.

2.

an
affine multitwist direction
if it is completely
periodic and there exists an affine multitwist
12
12

12

See Definition
1.2.6
.

f
in
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
that acts as a product
of Dehn twists in each cylinder
C_{i}
,
i\in I
.

3.

A
parabolic direction
if there exists
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
parabolic such that
D(f)
fixes the direction
\theta
.

Remark 3.3.32

For finite-type translation surface
M
, Lemma
1.2.6
shows parabolic directions and affine multitwist directions are the same thing. Moreover, the existence of an affine multitwist for a given direction is equivalent to the commensurability of the moduli of the corresponding cylinders.

As shown in Example
3.3.3
below, a parabolic direction in a translation surface is not necessarily an affine multitwist direction. However, as we see in Section
3.4
, the condition for a completely periodic direction to be stabilized by an affine multitwist can still be formulated in terms of moduli of the cylinders.

As a consequence of Lemma
3.3.3
we have the
following general statement for lifting parabolic affine automorphisms.

Corollary 3.3.33

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M

be a
G
-translation covering branched over
\Sigma
defined by
a morphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)\to G
.
Let
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
be a parabolic
element for which
C_{1}
, …,
C_{k}
are the maximal cylinders in the corresponding decomposition of
M
. Let
\gamma_{i}
be the
core curve of
C_{i}
considered as a conjugacy class in

\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)
. If all the elements
\rho(\gamma_{1})
, …,
\rho(\gamma_{k})

have finite order
m_{1}
, …,
m_{k}
in
G
, then
f^{\operatorname{lcm}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})}
lifts to
\widetilde{M}
.

of Corollary
3.3.3
.

Let us assume that
\rho(\gamma_{1})
, …,
\rho(\gamma_{k})
have finite order
m_{1}
, …,
m_{k}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.3.3
, the preimage of the cylinder
C_{i}
in

\widetilde{M}
is a disjoint union of cylinders with same height and whose circumference is

m_{i}
times the one of
C_{i}
. In particular, the modulus of any of these cylinders in
\widetilde{M}

is
\frac{\mu(C_{i})}{m_{i}}
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
be the multitwist whose derivative stabilizes the parabolic
direction. Let
m=\operatorname{lcm}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})
. Then
f^{m}
lifts as a multitwist in
\widetilde{M}
.
∎

We now turn to the case of Abelian coverings. As we already saw in
Theorem
3.3.2
from
Section
3.3.2
the lifting
of elements of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
to the covering
\widetilde{M}

can be expressed in terms of the action of this element on
the relative homology group
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
.

Definition 3.3.34

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
be a completely
periodic direction. Let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{k}
be the core curves
of the maximal cylinders in the corresponding decomposition of
M
. The
homological dimension
of the completely
periodic direction
\theta
in
M
is the dimension of the subspace
spanned by
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{k}
in absolute homology
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Q})
.

Note that the homological dimension is always smaller than or equal to the
number of cylinders.

Theorem 3.3.35
(one-cylinder trick)

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
p:\widetilde{M}\to M

be a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-translation covering defined by a subspace of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
and branched over
\Sigma
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
be a multitwist
whose derivative stabilizes a completely periodic direction
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
.

1.

If there is
only one
cylinder
C
in the cylinder decomposition of
M

in direction
\theta
, then the preimages of
this cylinder in
\widetilde{M}
are disjoint isometric copies of
C
.

2.

The direction
\theta
has homological dimension 1 in
M
if and only if
f_{*}
acts as the
identity on
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
.

In both situations above
f
lifts to an element
\widetilde{f}\in\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
.

Example 3.3.36

First, let’s consider the situation of the infinite staircase from
Section
1.2.3
to which Theorem
3.3.3
applies.
Recall that the infinite staircase is a
\mathbb{Z}
-translation covering
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
,
where
the base
M
is a torus and the covering map
p
is branched
over two points (see Example
2.2
).
In the horizontal
and vertical directions, the cylinder decompositions consist of a single cylinder
and hence the first item of Theorem
3.3.3
applies. In the
diagonal direction
\theta=\pi/4
there are two cylinders but their core
curves are homologous and the second item of Theorem
3.3.3

applies. Note that in this diagonal direction, the staircase decomposes
into two strips. The parabolic element
\widetilde{f}
that stabilizes
this strip decomposition is not a multitwist in the sense of Definition
1.2.6
.

We will actually prove results stronger than Theorem
3.3.3
. In particular, we will describe explicitly the action of multitwists on homology.
The algebraic intersection form

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\times H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}

will play an important role.

Lemma 3.3.37

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface and
V
a subspace of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
defining a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering

p:\widetilde{M}\to M
branched over
\Sigma\subset M
. Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
be an affine multitwist direction in
M\setminus\Sigma
with corresponding multitwist
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
. Let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{k}
in
H_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
be the core curves of the cylinders
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
in the and
\mu_{1}
, …,
\mu_{k}
their moduli.
Let us define

\phi:\begin{array}[]{ccc}H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})&\to&H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})\\
v&\mapsto&\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}\ \langle\gamma_{i},v\rangle\ \overline{\gamma_{i}}\end{array}

where
\overline{\gamma_{i}}
denotes the image of
\gamma_{i}
in
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
.
Then

1.

\langle\gamma_{i},V\rangle=0
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
if and
only if the preimage of each
C_{i}
in
\widetilde{M}
is a disjoint union
of cylinders isometric to
C_{i}
.

2.

\phi(V)=0
if and only if
f_{*}
acts trivially on
V
,

3.

\phi(V)\subset V
if and only if
f
lifts to the cover defined by
V
.

The main ingredient for the proof of Lemma
3.3.3
is the description of the action of a multitwist on homology given by the following exercise.

Exercise 3.3.38

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
be an affine multitwist direction in
M\setminus\Sigma
with corresponding multitwist
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
. Let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{k}
in
H_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})
be the core curves of the corresponding cylinders
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. The multitwist
f
acts as a power of a Dehn twist in each
cylinder. Let
m_{i}
be the multiplicity of the twist
in the cylinder
C_{i}
. Show that the action of
f
on

H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
is given by:

f_{*}(v)=v+\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}\langle\gamma_{i},v\rangle\ \overline{\gamma_{i}}

(3.18)

The last ingredient in the proof of Theorem
3.3.3
is the explicit expression of
\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
for
horizontal completely periodic directions.

Lemma 3.3.39

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface whose horizontal direction is
completely periodic. Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a finite set of points
and let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{k}
in
H_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{R})

be the core curves of maximal cylinders in
M\setminus\Sigma
and

h_{1}
,
h_{2}
, …,
h_{k}
their heights. Then the cycle

\gamma:=h_{1}\gamma_{1}+h_{2}\gamma_{2}+\ldots+h_{k}\gamma_{k}
is the
Poincaré dual of
\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
. That is to say

\forall\eta\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R}),\quad\langle\gamma,\eta\rangle=\int_{\eta}\operatorname{Im}(\omega).

We first proove Theorem
3.3.3
as a consequence
of Lemma
3.3.3
and Lemma
3.3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3.3
.

Let us assume that the surface
M
decomposes as a single cylinder
C
with core curve
\gamma
in direction
\theta
. By Lemma
3.3.3
the core curve
is the Poincaré dual of an element
\eta
in the tautological plane

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)\oplus\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
, that is
\eta=a\operatorname{Re}(\omega)+b\operatorname{Im}(\omega)
, for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence for any
\delta\in\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
we have that

\langle\gamma,\delta\rangle=a\int_{\delta}\operatorname{Re}(\omega)+b\int_{\delta}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)=0
. By the first item in Lemma
3.3.3
we conclude that the preimages of
C
in
\widetilde{M}
are disjoint isometric copies of
C
.

Now let us prove the second item. We assume that the horizontal direction
has homological dimension one. That is to say for each
i=1,\ldots,k

there exists
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
so that

\overline{\gamma_{i}}=\alpha_{i}\overline{\gamma_{1}}
.
Since the core curves are primitive elements of
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
we have

\alpha_{i}=1
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
. Now recall that the modulus of the cylinder
C_{i}
is
\mu_{i}=h_{i}/w_{i}
where

h_{i}
and
w_{i}
are the heights and circumferences of
C_{i}
respectively. But
since the
\gamma_{i}
are homologous, the
w_{i}
are equal and
\mu_{i}=h_{i}/w_{1}
.
In particular, we have for any
v\in H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Q})

\phi(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}\langle\gamma_{i},v\rangle\overline{\gamma_{i}}=\frac{1}{w_{1}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\gamma_{i},v\right\rangle\overline{\gamma_{1}}.

Now, similarly to the one-cylinder case we just apply
Lemma
3.3.3
to conclude that

\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\gamma_{i},v\right\rangle
vanishes
for vectors
v\in\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
. The conclusion follows from the second item in Lemma
3.3.3
and the Abelian lifting criterion in Theorem
3.3.2
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3.3
.

Let us consider the following generating system of
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n}
be the saddle connections on the boundary
of the cylinders appearing in the horizontal direction. We orient them in such way that
\int_{\eta_{i}}\omega>0
, for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Next, for each cylinder choose a saddle connection that goes from the top
to the botton. We denote
\xi_{i}
the transverse saddle connection in the
i
-th cylinder.
The set
\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\}
generates
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
and

•

for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,k
we have
\langle\eta_{i},\gamma_{j}\rangle=0

•

for all
i=1,\ldots,k
and
j=1,\ldots,k
we have
\langle\xi_{i},\gamma_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol.

As
\int_{\eta_{i}}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)=0
and
\int_{\xi_{i}}\operatorname{Im}(\omega)=h_{i}
we obtain the result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3.3
.

Suppose that the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
is defined by the kernel of
\rho:\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)\to G\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then
\langle\gamma_{i},V\rangle=0
if and only if
\rho(\gamma_{i})=0
for every
i=1,\ldots,k
. From Lemma
3.3.3
we conclude that this last condition happens if and only if for every
i=1,\ldots,k
the preimage of each
C_{i}
in
\widetilde{M}
is a disjoint union of cylinders isometric to
C_{i}
.

The multitwist
f
acts as a power of a Dehn twist in each
cylinder.

Let
m_{i}
be the multiplicity of the twist
in the cylinder
C_{i}
.
Now, recall that this multiplicity
m_{i}
is proportional to the moduli
\mu_{i}

of the cylinders. Namely if the parabolic stabilizes the horizontal direction and is hence written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

then
\lambda\mu_{i}=m_{i}
. By formula (
3.18
) from Exercise
3.3.3
we have that
f_{*}(v)=v+\frac{1}{\lambda}\phi(v)
where

\phi
was defined in the statement of Lemma
3.3.3
.
Hence
f_{*}
acts trivially on
V
if and only
if
\phi(V)=0
. From here the third item follows trivially and the last item is deduced
from the Abelian lifting criterion (Theorem
3.3.2
).
∎

Recall from Definition
3.3.2
and the discussion after that
a periodic point in a square tiled surface
p:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
is a point that
projects to
\mathbb{Q}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
under
p
.

Corollary 3.3.40
(one-cylinder trick for square-tiled surfaces)

Let
M
be a square-tiled surface and let
\Sigma\subset M
be a finite set of periodic points. Assume that
M
admits a completely periodic direction
of homological dimension one. Let
\widetilde{M}\to M
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering
determined by a subspace
V
of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
. Then the relative Veech group
13
13

13

See Definition
3.3.1

\Gamma(\widetilde{M}\to M)
contains a non-trivial normal subgroup of
\Gamma(M)

generated by parabolic elements.

A non-trivial normal subgroup of the lattice
\Gamma(M)
is
”big” in the following sense: its limit set is the whole circle.
This is a direct consequence of Lemma
A.1
, see also Appendix
A
for details. A particular case of
Corollary
3.3.3
appeared in
[
HS10
]
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
generated by the matrices
Df
where
f
is a multitwist that stabilizes a completely periodic direction with homological dimension one in
M
. Then by assumption
G
is non-trivial. It also follows from the one-cylinder trick (Theorem
3.3.3
) that any element in
G
lifts to
\widetilde{M}
. Let us prove that
G
is a normal subgroup. If
f
is a multitwist in
G
stabilizing a direction
\theta
and
h\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
, then
hfh^{-1}
is the multitwist stabilizing the direction
h\theta
. Moreover, the direction
\theta
and
h\theta
are affinely equivalent. In particular the completely periodic direction
\theta
and
h\theta
in
M
have the same homological dimensions and
hfh^{-1}
lifts to
\widetilde{M}
.
∎

Example 3.3.41

The eierlegende Wollmilchsau
14
14

14

The literal translation for this German word could be
egg-laying woolly milk sow
. This of course does not capture its meaning, and for this reason we added the following explanation, which is taken from die Deutsche Welle (a German public state-owned international broadcaster funded by the German federal tax budget, for more details see
here
): Germans have, apparently, never subscribed to the premise that you can’t make all the people happy all the time. That’s the idea behind the word
Eierlegende Wollmilchsau
. Who wouldn’t be happy to have a pig that lays eggs, gives milk and produces wool before it’s turned into a tasty Sunday roast? These days, the term has come to mean any person or device that’s able to be used in a wide variety of ways – like an alarm clock that makes coffee and toast or a cell phone that reminds you to pickup the dry cleaning.

p:W\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
is the square-tiled surface depicted in figure
3.4
. It was discovered independently by Herrlich and Weitze-Schmithüsen
[
HS08
]
and Forni
[
For06
]
.
W
is a genus 3 translation surface with
4
conical singularities of total angle
4\pi
(
i
.
e
., it belongs to the stratum
\mathcal{H}(1,1,1,1)
).

Figure 3.4
:
The eierlegende Wollmichsau square-tiled surface
W
. The integers on the outside of the polygons
correspond to the edge pairing. The bold labels in the squares show that
W
has Deck group
transformation the quaternion group.

Exercise 3.3.42

Let
W
be the eierlegende Wollmilchsau square-tiled surface and
\Sigma
be its set of four conical singularities.

1.

Show that the Deck group of
p:W\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
can be identified with the quaternion group

Q:=\{e,i,j,k,\overline{e},\overline{i},\overline{j},\overline{k}\}

with neutral element
e
and multiplication rules

•

i^{2}=j^{2}=k^{2}=\overline{e}

•

ij=k
,
ki=j
and
jk=i

•

\forall g\in Q
,
g^{-1}=\overline{g}
where
\overline{\overline{g}}=g
.

2.

Show that the subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\})
that defines
W

is characteristic. Conclude that the Veech group of
W
is
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})

(see Corollary
3.3.1
).

3.

Use the one-cylinder trick (Theorem
3.3.3
) to prove that the horizontal multitwist
in
W
acts trivially on
\operatorname{kerhol}(W,\Sigma)
.

4.

Using the fact that the Veech group of
W
is
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, prove that any multitwist in

\operatorname{Aff}(W,\Sigma)
acts trivially on
\operatorname{kerhol}(W,\Sigma)
.
Hint
: there is only one conjugacy class of parabolic elements in
\Gamma(W)=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

5.

Show that the derivative map induces an isomorphism between the subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(W,\Sigma)
generated
by the multitwists of the previous question and
\Gamma(4)

(
hint:
for surjectivity, use the fact that
\Gamma(4)
is generated by its parabolic elements)

6.

Conclude that the Veech group of any
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering
\widetilde{W}
of
W
at most
branched over
\Sigma
contains the congruence group
\Gamma(4)

(
hint:
use Theorem
3.3.2
).

In the above exercise we explicited the action of a finite index subgroup of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
on
\operatorname{kerhol}(W,\Sigma)
.
The complete description of the action of
\operatorname{Aff}(W,\Sigma)
on
\operatorname{kerhol}(W,\Sigma)
is given
in
[
MY10
]
. Other properties of the eierlegende Wollmilchsau are
discussed in
[
For06
]
and
[
HS08
]
.

Let us also mention that the eierlegende Wollmilchsau
W
is not the only known example of
finite-type square-tiled surface such that a finite index subgroup of its affine group acts trivially
in homology. There is a cousin of the eierlegende Wollmilchsau: a genus 4 square-tiled surface called
the
ornithorynque
(the french for platypus). Similarly to what is proposed
in Exercise
3.3.3
, one can prove that a subgroup of its affine
group isomorphic to
\Gamma(3)
acts trivially on homology. We refer to
[
MY10
]

and
[
FMZ11
]
,
[
FM14
, Chapter 7.2]
for more details on this example.

3.3.4
The Hooper-Weiss theorem

Let us recall from Definition
3.3.2
and the remark that follows
that a periodic point in a square-tiled surface
p:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
is a point that projects
under
p
to a point in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The aim of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 3.3.43
(
[
HW12
]
)

Let
M
be a square-tiled surface of genus
2
and let
\Sigma\subset M
be a finite set of periodic points. Let
\widetilde{M}\to M
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-covering determined by a subspace
V
of
\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma)
. Then the relative Veech group
15
15

15

See Definition
3.3.1

\Gamma(\widetilde{M}\to M)
contains a non-trivial normal subgroup of
\Gamma(M,\Sigma)
16
16

16

See Definition
1.1.5
.

The reader should notice the similarity of Theorem
[
HW12
]
with Corollary
3.3.3
of the one-cylinder trick. In both situations, the morphism
\operatorname{Aff}(M)\to\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma))
has an infinite kernel and all elements in this kernel lift to
\operatorname{Aff}(\widetilde{M})
. In particular, Theorem
[
HW12
]
implies that
\Gamma(\widetilde{M}\to M)
is a Fuchsian group of the first kind (see Appendix
A
for more details on Fuchsian groups).

Proof of Theorem
3.3.4
.

Since
\Sigma
are periodic points, the relative Veech group
\Gamma(M,\Sigma)

is a lattice of finite index in
\Gamma(M)
. Let

\psi:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{kerhol}(M,\Sigma))
and

\psi_{0}:\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)\to GL(\operatorname{kerhol}(M))
be the morphisms induced
by the action of the affine group on respectively
H_{1}(M,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
.
By Theorem
3.3.2
, it suffices
to show that the image of
\ker(\psi_{0})
in
\Gamma(M)
is non-trivial. We
suppose that
D\ker(\psi_{0})
is trivial and derive a contradiction.

Because
M
has genus 2,
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
has dimension 2. We know from
Theorem
3.3.2
that
\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
is
symplectic. Up to passing to a finite index subgroup
A
of
\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
we
can further suppose that:

1.

the image
\psi_{0}(A)
is torsion-free
(
e
.
g
., consider
\psi_{0}^{-1}(\Gamma(2))
where
\Gamma(2)
denote the principal
congruence subgroup)

2.

that both
D
and
\psi_{0}
are faithful representations of

A
into
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
(because in this case
\ker(\operatorname{Aff}(M)\to\Gamma(M))
is finite since it is a subgroup of the group of conformal automorphisms of a compact Riemann surface).

From now on we fix such finite index subgroup
A\subset\operatorname{Aff}(M,\Sigma)
.

Now, let
f\in A
. Then

1.

if
D(f)
is parabolic then
\psi_{0}(f)
is parabolic. Indeed, by
Exercise
3.3.3
, the action of
f
on homology has
only eigenvalues which are roots of unity. Furthemore,
\psi_{0}(f)
can not be
identity or elliptic since by assumption
\psi|_{A}
is injective.

2.

If
D(f)
is hyperbolic, then
2\leq|\operatorname{tr}(\psi_{0}(f))|<|\operatorname{tr}(D(f))|

(the dominant eigenvalue of
D(f)
is the stretch-factor of the pseudo-Anosov
and dominates any other eigenvalue of the action of
f
on
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
,
see Theorem
3.3.2
).

Now consider the hyperbolic surfaces

S=\mathbb{H}^{2}/D(A)
and
S_{0}=\mathbb{H}^{2}/\psi_{0}(A)
. Because both
\psi_{0}

and
D
are faithful, there exists an homotopy equivalence
\phi:S\to S_{0}

induced by the isomorphism at the level of fundamental groups. Because
parabolic elements in
A
are mapped to parabolic elements by

\psi_{0}
we know that the number of cusps
n
of
S
is smaller than or equal
to the one
n_{0}
of
S_{0}
. We claim that there are actually equal.
Indeed, let us denote by
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{n}
in
H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})

the generators of peripheral curves in
S
. If we had
n_{0}>n
then these linearly
dependent elements would be mapped to linearly independent elements
of
H_{1}(S_{0};\mathbb{Z})
contradicting the homotopy equivalence. Hence,
S
and
S_{0}

have the same number of cusps and, because of the homotopy equivalence, the same
genus. Thus
\psi_{0}(A)
is a lattice.

Now for each non-peripherial
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
let
\ell(\gamma)

denote the length of the geodesic representative on
S
, and let
\ell_{0}(\gamma)

denote the length of the geodesic representative of
\phi_{*}(\gamma)
. It is a deep
result by Thurston (see
[
Thu
, Theorem 3.1]
) that

\sup_{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)}\frac{\ell_{0}(\phi_{*}(\gamma))}{\ell(\gamma)}\geq 1,

(3.19)

with equality only if
\phi
is homotopic to an isometry of the hyperbolic
surfaces
S
and
S_{0}
.

Now a closed geodesic
\gamma
in
S
corresponds to the
conjugacy class
D(f)
of a hyperbolic affine element
f
in
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
.
Its image
\gamma_{0}=\phi(\gamma)
corresponds to the conjugacy class
of
\psi_{0}(f)
. The hyperbolic lengths of the loops
\gamma
and

\gamma_{0}
are respectively the logarithms of the dominant
eigenvalues of
D(f)
and
\psi_{0}(f)
.
Thus, equality in (
3.19
) implies that
\phi
is isotopic to an isometry, thus the logarithms of the dominant
eigenvalues of
D(f)
and
\psi_{0}(f)
are equal and this contradicts the
fact that
|\operatorname{tr}(\psi_{0}(f))|<|\operatorname{tr}(D(f))|
.
∎

Remark 3.3.44

The proof of Theorem
3.3.4
is actually more general. Along the same line one can prove the following generalization.

Theorem 3.3.45

Let
M
be a Veech surface. Assume that there exists a rank 2 subspace

E\subset\operatorname{kerhol}(M)
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and preserved by a subgroup
A
of
finite index of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
. Then the kernel of the induced action
A\to\operatorname{GL}(E)

is infinite or, equivalently, the image of this kernel in
\Gamma(M)
is non-trivial.

We will even see a more general version in Lemma
3.3.5
and
Remark
3.3.5
.

Exercise 3.3.46

Let
M=(X,q)
be a meromorphic quadratic differential with at most simple poles on a torus
such that its orientation cover is a
Veech surface. Let
\widetilde{M}\to(X,q)
be the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-half-translation covering
obtained by taking the universal cover of the torus (
i
.
e
., a Panov plane as in
Section
1.2.5
).Show that Theorem
3.3.4
can be applied to

\operatorname{Aff}(M)\to\operatorname{GL}(H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}))
.
Hint
: use Exercise
3.3.2
.

Example 3.3.47

We now come back to the square-tiled surface
Z_{3,1}
of Example
3.3.2
.
This example also appears as
[
HW12
, Example 7.2]
.
As can be seen on Figure
3.3
any cylinder decomposition of
the square-tiled surface
Z_{3,1}
has homological dimension 2. As a consequence, the
kernel of the action
\operatorname{Aff}(Z_{3,1})\to\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{kerhol}(Z_{3,1})

does not contain any parabolic element
(see Theorem
3.3.3
).
However, the Hooper-Weiss theorem does apply: this kernel is infinite.

The square-tiled surface
Z_{3,1}
belongs to the stratum
\mathcal{H}(1,1)
. Let us mention that
in the other stratum of genus
2
,
\mathcal{H}(2)
any square-tiled surface admits a completely
periodic direction made of a single maximal cylinder
[
HL06b
]
.

The following is a delicate open question.

Question 3.3.48

Does there exist a square-tiled surface
p:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
of genus 3 such that the kernel of
the induced action
\operatorname{Aff}(M)\to\operatorname{GL}(\operatorname{kerhol}(M))
is finite?

Note that Theorem
3.3.3
discards any square-tiled surface that admits a completely periodic of homological dimension one. The reader interested by this question should consult
[
HMS20
]
.

3.3.5
Affine symmetries of wind-tree models

In this section we study the affine symmetries of the wind-tree models
W_{a,b}
, which were defined in Section
1.2.4
. Recall that
W_{a,b}

is a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-covering of a finite-type translation surface
X_{a,b}
that belongs to the
stratum
\mathcal{H}(2^{4})
. Moreover,
X_{a,b}
is a
K
-covering of
L_{a,b}
in
\mathcal{H}(2)
where

K=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
is the Klein group (see Figure
3.5
).

Figure 3.5
:
Two views of
L_{1/2,1/2}
. The left picture is inspired from
Figure
1.15
for
X_{a,b}
. The right picture
is obtained from the left one by cutting along the dotted lines and gluing
along the sides
Y
and
Z
.

By important work of K. Calta
[
Cal04
]
and
C. McMullen
[
McM03
]
, we know the list of
(a,b)
for which
L_{a,b}

are Veech surfaces.

Theorem 3.3.49
(
[
Cal04
]
,
[
McM03
]
)

Let
a,b\in(0,1)
, then the following are equivalent:

1.

The surface
L_{a,b}
is a Veech surface.

2.

The surface
L_{a,b}
comes from a Thurston-Veech construction. In particular, it admits
horizontal and vertical affine multitwists.

3.

The surface
L_{a,b}
admits an affine pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism.

4.

The parameters
a
and
b
are rational, or there exists a square-free positive integer
D

and rational numbers
x,y
such that
1/(1-a)=x+y\sqrt{D}
and
1/(1-b)=(1-x)+y\sqrt{D}
.

Some of the implications in Theorem
3.3.5
are straightforward:

1
implies
3
,

4
is equivalent to
2

and
2
implies
3
. The
reader non-familiar with this topic might want to prove them. All the other implications are non trivial
and we refer to the original articles.

The following subset of parameters plays an important role

\mathcal{E}:=\left\{\left(\frac{p}{q},\frac{r}{s}\right)\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}:\ 0<p<q,\ 0<r<s,\ \text{$p$, $q$ odd and $r$, $s$ even}\right\}.

(3.20)

Theorem 3.3.50

For the wind-tree model parameters
(a,b)
, such that
L_{a,b}
is a Veech surface, the following holds:

1.

\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})
has
17
17

17

See Definition
3.3.1
.
infinite index in
\Gamma(L_{a,b})
,

2.

\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})
contains a non-trivial normal subgroup,
in particular its limit set is the whole circle, and

3.

if furthermore
(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is defined
in (
3.20
), then
\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})

contains a parabolic element.

The rest of the section is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
.
We will prove the first item about the index of

\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})
in
\Gamma(L_{a,b})
. Then, we consider
parameters
(a,b)
in
\mathcal{E}
and use the one-cylinder trick
(Theorem
3.3.3
) to prove the third item.
Finally, we apply a variant of the Hooper-Weiss theorem
(Theorem
3.3.4
) for all
parameters
(a,b)
which provides a proof of the second item.

\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})
has infinite index in
\Gamma(L_{a,b})

We begin the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
by proving that

\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})
is not a lattice. We use an argument
that appears in
[
Cab12
]
.

Let
a,b
be parameters so that
L_{a,b}
is a Veech surface. Let

f\in\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})
be the multitwist stabilizing the horizontal direction.
Since
X_{a,b}\to L_{a,b}
is a finite covering (the deck transformation group is the Klein group), it is sufficient to show that no power of
f
lifts to
W_{a,b}
.
Let
V
be the subspace of
\operatorname{kerhol}(X_{a,b})
generated by the elements

Y
and
Z
as in (
3.9
). We want to show that no power of

f_{*}
preserves
V
. By a direct computation, one can see that
f_{*}
fixes

Y
but for any
n\geq 1
, we have
(f_{*})^{n}Z\not\in V
. As a consequence
no power of
f
lifts to
W_{a,b}
and the index of
\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to L_{a,b})

in
\Gamma(L_{a,b})
is infinite.

Parabolic elements for parameters in
\mathcal{E}

Let us recall that in (
3.20
) we defined
a subset of rational parameters
\mathcal{E}
for the windtree-model. For

(a,b)
in
\mathcal{E}
we consider parabolic elements in
L_{a,b}
and show that some of
them can be lifted to
W_{a,b}
.

Lemma 3.3.51

Let
(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}
. Then
L_{a,b}
admits a completely periodic direction made of
a single cylinder. For any such direction, if the surface
L_{a,b}
decomposes
into a cylinder of modulus
\mu
then the surface
X_{a,b}
decomposes into two
cylinders of modulus
\mu/2
whose core curves are homologous.

As a consequence of the above Lemma and Theorem
3.3.3

we have.

Corollary 3.3.52

Let
(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}
. The square of any multitwist stabilizing a one
cylinder direction in
L_{a,b}
lifts to
X_{a,b}
and
W_{a,b}
.
In particular,
\Gamma(W_{a,b}\to X_{a,b})
contains a parabolic
element.

Proof of Lemma
3.3.5
.

We consider the quotient
\overline{L_{a,b}}
of
L_{a,b}
by the hyperelliptic
involution (the unique affine element in
\operatorname{Aff}(L_{a,b})
whose derivative is minus identity). The surface

\overline{L_{a,b}}
is depicted in Figure
3.6
. The advantage of
this surface is that it has genus 0, and describing a covering of a genus 0 surface
is very convenient (the fundamental group is generated by loops around the punctures).

(a)

The quotient
\overline{L_{a,b}}
of
L_{a,b}
is a quadratic differential
on the sphere (stratum
\mathcal{Q}(1,-1^{5})
). Each side of the pentagon is glued to itself
via a 180° rotation and contains a pole in its center. The five poles are labeled
p_{A}
,

p_{B}
,
p_{C}
,
p_{D}
,
p_{E}
in counterclockwise order.

(b)

The surface
L_{a,b}
as two copies of a pentagon. The labelling of the
sides is compatible with Figure
3.5
: the preimage of the pole
p_{A}
belongs
to side
A
, etc.

Figure 3.6
:
\overline{L_{a,b}}
and
L_{a,b}
.

The first step, is to describe the coverings
L_{a,b}\to\overline{L_{a,b}}

and
X_{a,b}\to\overline{L_{a,b}}
. Both involve homology with
coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
relative to the set of singularities

\overline{\Sigma}
of
\overline{L_{a,b}}
that consists of the five poles
p_{A}
, …,
p_{E}
and
a conical singularity of angle
3\pi
.
We denote by

\overline{A}
,
\overline{B}
,
\overline{C}
,
\overline{D}
and

\overline{E}
the elements in

H_{1}(\overline{L_{a,b}},\overline{\Sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
that
corresponds to the quotients of the sides
A
,
B
,
C
,
D
and
E

of the figure
L_{a,b}
(see Figure
3.6
). Since
we consider
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
coefficients, the orientation of these
curves does not matter. These 5 elements form a basis of

H_{1}(\overline{L_{a,b}},\overline{\Sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

In this basis the cycle
c_{L}\in H_{1}(\overline{L_{a,b}},\overline{\Sigma};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
that corresponds to the degree 2
covering
L_{a,b}\to\overline{L_{a,b}}
and
c_{X}\in H_{1}(\overline{L_{a,b}},\overline{\Sigma};(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{3})
that
corresponds to the degree 8 covering
X_{a,b}\to\overline{L_{a,b}}
are

c_{L}=\overline{A}+\overline{B}+\overline{C}+\overline{D}+\overline{E}\qquad c_{X}=(1,1,0)\overline{D}+(1,0,1)\overline{C}+(1,0,0)(\overline{A}+\overline{B}+\overline{E}).

(3.21)

The formula for
c_{L}
and
c_{X}
can be deduced by analyzing the action of the Deck group of

X_{a,b}\to L_{a,b}
on
H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{Z})
.

(a)

A one cylinder direction in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
when
(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}

has one of its sides with the poles
\{p_{C},p_{D}\}
while the other three
are on the other side.

(b)

The preimage of the one cylinder direction of Figure
3.7

in
L_{a,b}
.

(c)

The preimage of the one cylinder direction of Figure
3.7

in
X_{a,b}
.

Figure 3.7
:
A one cylinder direction in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
and its preimages in

L_{a,b}
and
X_{a,b}
.

A direction is completely periodic in
L_{a,b}
if and only if it is completely periodic
in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
. Furthermore, if the direction in
L_{a,b}
is made of a single
cylinder then it is also the case in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
which is a quotient.

A single cylinder direction in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
has two poles on one boundary
and the three other together and the
3\pi
singularity on the other.
By
[
HLT11
]
for any completely periodic direction in

\overline{L_{a,b}}
made of a single cylinder, one side of this cylinder
contains the two poles
p_{C}
and
p_{D}
while the three other poles and the
conical singularity of angle
3\pi
are on the other side
This fact holds exactly
for the parameters
(a,b)
in
\mathcal{E}
and this is where this hypothesis is crucial.

From now on, we analyze a single cylinder direction in
\overline{L_{a,b}}

where one boundary is made of a saddle connection between
p_{C}
and
p_{D}
.
Such one cylinder direction has the form shown in
Figure
3.7
(up to permutation of the poles
p_{A}
,
p_{B}
,
p_{E}

located on the top of the cylinder). The core curve

\gamma
seen as an element of

H_{1}(\overline{L_{a,b}}\setminus\{p_{A},p_{B},p_{C},p_{D},p_{E},q\};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
intersects the cycles
\overline{C}
and
\overline{D}
but not the other three.
From formulas (
3.21
) we compute

\langle c_{X},\gamma\rangle=1+1=0\quad\text{and}\quad\langle c_{L},\gamma\rangle=(1,1,0)+(1,0,1)=(0,1,1).

Hence, the preimage in
L_{a,b}
and
X_{a,b}
of this cylinder
consists respectively of a single cylinder of modulus
2\overline{\mu}

(see Figure
3.7
) and two cylinders of modulus
\overline{\mu}

with homologous core curves (see Figure
3.7
). Hence the
conclusion of Lemma
3.3.5
holds for any
one-cylinder direction in
\overline{L_{a,b}}
one of its boundary
consisting of a saddle connection between
p_{C}
and
p_{D}
.

As already mentioned, for
(a,b)\in\mathcal{E}
all one-cylinder directions
are of this form. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Homology action of
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})

We now prove the second item in Theorem
3.3.5
by using a
variant of the Hooper-Weiss Theorem
3.3.4
.

First of all the surface
X_{a,b}
admits a decomposition of
H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{Q})

which is finer than the discussed for a general surface in
Theorem
3.3.2
. This is a very general phenomenon
for normal covers: the deck group of the cover acts on homology, and the affine group
preserves the isotypical components of this action
18
18

18

The isotypical components
of a finite group
are defined via the representation theory of finite groups, see
[
Ser77
]
. For a definition in
the context of translation surfaces see
[
MYZ14
]
.

Let
\tau_{h}
be the translation of
X_{a,b}
that exchanges
the polygon
00
with
10
and
01
with
11
of
Figure
1.15
. In other words,

\tau_{h}
exchanges the two copies drawn on the same horizontal.
Similarly, let
\tau_{v}

be the one exchanging the copies
00
with
01
and
10
with
11
.

Lemma 3.3.53
(
[
DHL14
]
)

Let
(a,b)
be any parameter for the surface
L_{a,b}
. Let

\tau_{h}
and
\tau_{v}
be the generators of the Deck transformations of
the covering
X_{a,b}\mapsto L_{a,b}
. Let us define for each

s,t\in\{+1,-1\}
the following subspaces:

E^{st}:=\{v\in H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{R}):\ \tau_{h}v=sv\quad\tau_{v}v=tv\}.

Then

1.

\dim E^{++}=4
and
\dim E^{+-}=\dim E^{-+}=\dim E^{--}=2
,

2.

there is a symplectic orthogonal splitting
H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{R})=E^{++}\oplus E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--}

and
E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--}\subset\operatorname{kerhol}(X_{a,b})
,

3.

each element of
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})
preserves
E^{++}
and
E^{--}
,

4.

each element of
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})
either preserves or exchanges
E^{+-}
and
E^{-+}
.

Proof.

We refer to
[
DHL14
]
for details. All these results
follow from the computation of the topology of the various quotients. First of
all
L_{a,b}=X_{a,b}/\langle\tau_{h},\tau_{v}\rangle
hence
E^{++}\simeq H_{1}(L_{a,b};\mathbb{Q})
which has rank 4. Next
X_{a,b}/\langle\tau_{h}\rangle
,

X_{a,b}/\langle\tau_{v}\rangle
and
X_{a,b}/\langle\tau_{h},\tau_{v}\rangle
have all genus 3.
∎

Lemma 3.3.54
(
[
Par
]
)

Let
(a,b)
be parameters so that
L_{a,b}
is a Veech surface. Let

H_{1}(X_{a,b};\mathbb{R})=E^{++}\oplus E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--}

be as in Lemma
3.3.5
. Then the kernel of
the group action
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})\to\operatorname{GL}(E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--})

is non-trivial.

Proof.

Up to passing to a subgroup
A
of index 2 of
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})
, we can assume that
each individual component
E^{st}
for
(s,t)\in\{+-,-+,--\}

is preserved. Since each component
E^{st}
has rank 2 and is contained
in
\operatorname{kerhol}(X_{a,b})
the general Hooper-Weiss theorem
(Theorem
3.3.4
)
implies that the kernels of
A\to\operatorname{GL}(E^{st})
are infinite.
Their images in the lattice
\Gamma(X_{a,b})
are non-trivial normal subgroups and
hence non-elementary of the first kind (see Lemma
A.1
). In particular
there exist
\phi_{+-}
,
\phi_{-+}
and
\phi_{--}
in
A
so that
\phi_{st}
acts
trivially on
E^{st}
for each
st\in\{+-,-+,--\}
.

Let us first show that one can choose
\phi_{+-}
and
\phi_{-+}
so
that
g_{1}:=D(\phi_{+-})
and
g_{2}:=D(\phi_{-+})
do not have
common fixed points on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Assume they have a common
fixed points. Because the group is non-elementary, there exists an
element
\psi
that does not have any common fixed point with

\phi_{+-}
. The elements
\psi^{n}\phi_{+-}\psi^{-n}
for
n\geq 1

are all pseudo-Anosovs which act trivially on
E_{+-}
. Moreover,
the fixed points of their derivatives
D(\psi^{n}\phi_{+-}\psi^{-n})

are pairwise disjoint. Hence, there is one, say for
n=n_{0}
, whose
derivative has disjoint fixed points from
\phi_{-+}
and we can
replace
\phi_{+-}
by its conjugate
\psi^{n_{0}}\phi_{+-}\psi^{-n_{0}}
.

From now on, we assume that
g_{+-}:=D(\phi_{+-})
and

g_{-+}:=D(\phi_{-+})
have disjoint fixed points. By the
ping-pong lemma (see Theorem
A.1

from Appendix
A
) there exists an integer
m

so that
(g_{+-})^{m}
and
g_{-+}
generate a free-group whose
non-trivial elements are hyperbolic. In particular the commutator

[(\phi_{+-})^{m},\phi_{-+}]
whose derivative
is
D([(\phi_{+-})^{m},\phi_{-+}])=[(g_{+-})^{n},g_{-+}]

is a hyperbolic affine element. Moreover it acts trivially
on both
E^{+-}
and
E^{-+}
.

In order to construct a hyperbolic element that acts trivially
on all of
E^{+-}
,
E^{-+}
and
E^{--}
one can repeat the same
argument with the two affine elements
[(\phi_{+-})^{m},\phi_{-+}]

and
\phi_{--}
.

Since
L_{a,b}
is a Veech surface, so is
X_{a,b}
and hence the limit of any non-trivial normal subgroup of

\Gamma(X_{a,b})
is the whole circle, see Lemma
A.1
. To finish the proof recall that
the subspace
V<H_{1}(X_{a,b},\mathbb{Q})
defining the non-ramified covering
W_{a,b}\to X_{a,b}
is generated by the cycles
Y

and
Z
described in equation (
3.9
). Both
X
and
Y
lie in
E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--}
, hence by the Abelian lifting criterion every element in the kernel of
\operatorname{Aff}(X_{a,b})\to\operatorname{GL}(E^{+-}\oplus E^{-+}\oplus E^{--})

lifts to
\operatorname{Aff}(W_{a,b})
.

∎

Remark 3.3.55

The proof of Lemma
3.3.5
does actually provide
an extension of the general Hooper-Weiss theorem. If
M
is a Veech surface
and there exist subspaces
E_{1}
, …,
E_{k}
of kerhol in
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
such that

1.

each
E_{i}
has rank 2 and they are in direct sum

2.

a subgroup of finite index
A
of
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
preserves each
E_{i}

Then the kernel of the induced action
A\to\operatorname{GL}(E_{1})\times\ldots\times\operatorname{GL}(E_{k})

is infinite and its image in
\Gamma(A)
is a non-trivial normal subgroup.

3.4
Hooper-Thurston-Veech construction

In Section
1.2.6
we introduced the
Thurston-Veech construction, which given two multicurves filling a finite-type
topological surface
S
produces a half-translation surface
M
on
S
admiting
a horizontal and a vertical cylinder decomposition where all cylinders involved
have the same modulus. As a consequence the surface
M
has a lot of affine
pseudo-Anosov homeomorphisms. In the following paragraphs we detail an
extension of the Thurston-Veech construction based on the ideas of P.
Hooper
[
Hoo15
]
and illustrate it with several
examples. Infinite-type quadratic differentials constructed this way have
non-elementary Veech groups and their translation flows exhibit rich dynamics,
which we study in detail in a (second) forthcoming volume
[
DHV
]

dedicated mostly to dynamical aspects of infinite-type translation surfaces.

Remark 3.4.1

In this Chapter we construct surfaces where changes of coordinates are translations or half-translations. For the sake of a simple nomenclature, we will henceforth use the equivalent term quadratic differential (see Chapter
1
).

The whole construction is based in the following statement, which is the extension of Lemma
1.2.6
to infinite-type surfaces.

Lemma 3.4.2

Let
M
be a half-translation surface for which the horizontal direction is completely periodic and suppose that every maximal cylinder in the corresponding cylinder decomposition has modulus equal to
\frac{1}{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
. Then there exists a unique affine automorphism
\varphi_{h}
which fixes the boundaries of the cylinders and has derivative
D\varphi_{h}=h_{\lambda}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Moreover, the automorphism
\varphi_{h}
acts as a Dehn twist along the core curve of each cylinder.

Remark 3.4.3

If
M
is a half-translation surface and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
is a completely periodic direction for which every cylinder has modulus equal to
\frac{1}{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, then one can apply to
M
the rotation
R_{\theta}\in\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})
that takes
\theta
to the horizontal direction and apply the preceding lemma. For example, if
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, then there exists a unique affine automorphism
\varphi_{v}
which fixes the boundaries of the vertical cylinders, acting on each cylinder as a Dehn twists and with derivative
D\varphi_{v}=v_{-\lambda}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Exercise 3.4.4

Let
M
be a half-translation surface and suppose that
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
is a direction in which the translation flow decomposes
M
into maximal cylinders
\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of modulus
\{\mu_{i}\}
. Moreover, suppose that for every
i\in I
there exists
s\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
such that
m_{i}:=s\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Show that there exist a unique
f\in\operatorname{Aff}(M)
fixing the boundary of each cylinder
C_{i}
whose derivative is, up to conjugation in
\operatorname{SO}(2,\mathbb{R})
, equal to
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&s\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

The proof of Lemma
3.4
can be deduced from the preceding exercise and we leave it to the reader.

Let us remark that Lemma
1.2.6
gives an equivalence
between parabolic directions and affine multitwist directions for finite-type
surfaces. Though for infinite-type surfaces, only the implication stated in
Lemma
3.4
is valid. Indeed, we already
encountered many counterexamples to the converse. First of all, in
Example
3.3.3
we showed that the Veech group of
the infinite staircase has a parabolic element fixing the direction

\theta=\frac{\pi}{4}
and in this direction the staircase decomposes into two strips.
Secondly, if one considers the plane or any cyclic cover ramified over the origin,
its Veech group is
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. In this case, any direction
\theta
is parabolic but
none of them contains a cylinder. Finally, more exotic examples can be constructed from
Theorem
3.2.1
and Exercise
3.2.1
.
Note that all these examples have infinite area.

Question 3.4.5

Let
M
be an infinite-type translation surface of finite area.
Are parabolic directions in
M
affine multitwist directions?

Definition 3.4.6

A quadratic differential
M
is called a
Hooper-Thurston-Veech
surface if it admits horizontal and vertical cylinder decompositions where all cylinders have the same modulus. The common modulus of the cylinders is called the modulus of the Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface.

Remark 3.4.7

If
M
is a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface of modulus
\frac{1}{\lambda}
then
\operatorname{Aff}(M)
has two affine multitwists
\phi_{h}
and
\phi_{v}
with
D\phi_{h}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
D\phi_{v}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Moreover, if
\lambda\geq 2
then

G_{\lambda}=\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
-\lambda&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle

(3.22)

is a free subgroup of the Veech group
\Gamma(M)
(see Theorem
A.2
from Appendix
A
).

Note that finite-type Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces are those given by the
classical Thurston-Veech construction discussed in
Section
1.2.6
. Translation coverings
provide an elementary way of obtaining Hooper-Thuston-Veech surfaces. More
precisely the lifting criterion for cylinders stated in
Lemma
3.3.3
implies the following:

Lemma 3.4.8

Let
M
be a finite-type translation surface obtained by the Thurston-Veech construction
for the multicurves
\alpha=\cup_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}
and
\beta=\cup_{j\in J}\beta_{j}
and
G
any group.
Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
be the
G
-covering branched at most over the singularities
\Sigma

of
M
defined by a morphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M\setminus\Sigma)\to G
such that for all
i\in I

we have
\rho(\alpha_{i})=1
and for all
j\in J
we have
\rho(\beta_{j})=1
.
Then
\widetilde{M}
is a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface.

The rest of the section is dedicated to the general construction of
Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces starting from a pair of transverse
multicurves in an infinite-type surface.

3.4.1
From cylinder decompositions to bipartite graphs

Let
S
be a
topological surface. Recall from Definition
1.2.6
that a
multicurve in
S
is a locally finite family of essential simple
closed curves in
S
, pairwise non-intersecting and pairwise non-homotopic.
A pair of simple closed curves
c
and
c^{\prime}
are in
minimal position
if

|c\cap c^{\prime}|=\iota(c,c^{\prime})
, where
\iota(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the
geometric intersection between isotopy classes of curves.

Definition 3.4.9

We say that a pair of multicurves
\alpha=\cup\alpha_{i}
,
\beta=\cup\beta_{j}

•

is in
minimal position
if every pair
\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}
of
components of
\alpha
and
\beta
are in minimal position,

•

fills

S
if every connected component of
the complement of the union
\alpha\cup\beta
is a topological disc with at most
one puncture.

Exercise 3.4.10

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
two multicurves in a topological surface
S
. Show
that each component
\alpha_{i}
of
\alpha
intersects finitely
many components of
\beta
.

hint:
use the fact that a multicurve is by definition locally finite.

Definition 3.4.11

Let
\alpha=\cup_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}
and
\beta=\cup_{j\in J}\beta_{j}
be two multicurves in a topological surface

S
in minimal position (not necessarily filling).
The
configuration graph
of the pair
(\alpha,\beta)
is the bipartite graph
19
19

19

We allow multiple edges since
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{j}
can intersect more than once. Strictly speaking
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
is a multigraph, but we keep the term graph to facilitate the reading of this section.

\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)

whose vertex set is
I\sqcup J
and where there is an edge between two vertices
i\in I
and
j\in J
for every
intersection between the corresponding curves
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{j}
.

Before elaborating the construction for infinite-type surfaces, we explain how
the configuration graph already appeared in disguise form in the
Thurston-Veech contruction from
Section
1.2.6
(more precisely
Exercises
1.2.6
and
1.2.6
). Let
S
be a
finite-type surface and
\alpha=\cup\alpha_{i}
,
\beta=\cup\beta_{j}
a pair of
filling multicurves in minimal position. As we have seen there is a
half-translation structure on
S
which admits an horizontal
and a vertical affine multitwist along the multicurves
\alpha
and
\beta

respectively. To construct such half-translation structure, we considered the
intersection matrix
E=(\iota(\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}))_{i\in I,j\in J}
and shown that the heights of
the horizontal cylinders
\textbf{h}_{h}
and the
heights of the vertical cylinders
\textbf{h}_{v}
must satisfy
E\textbf{h}_{v}=\lambda\textbf{h}_{h}
and
E^{t}\textbf{h}_{h}=\lambda\textbf{h}_{v}
. Let us
consider the following matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&E\\
E^{t}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then
A
is the adjacency matrix of the configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)

and the vector
\textbf{h}=(\textbf{h}_{h},\textbf{h}_{v})
is an eigenvector of
A
.

Now, let us consider a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface with
modulus
1/\lambda
. Let
H=\{H_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
V=\{V_{j}\}_{j\in J}

be respectively the horizontal and vertical cylinders of
M
.
For every
i\in I
let
\alpha_{i}
be the
core curve of
H_{i}
and for every
j\in J
let
\beta_{j}
be
the core curve of
V_{j}
.
Then
\alpha=\cup\alpha_{i}
and
\beta=\cup\beta_{j}
are multicurves
in
M
. Let
\textbf{h}:I\cup J\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be the function which to
an index associates the width of the corresponding cylinder.
Then it satisfies the equation
A\textbf{h}=\lambda\textbf{h}
where
A
is the
adjacency operator of the graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
defined by

(A\textbf{h})(v):=\sum_{w\sim v}\textbf{h}(w)

(3.23)

where the sum above is taken over the
vertices
w
adjacent to
v
in
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)

(with the multiplicity given by the number of edges
between
v
and
w
).

Definition 3.4.12

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be a graph with vertices of finite degree.
Let
A:V^{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
its adjacency operator.
A function
\textbf{h}:V\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfies
A\textbf{h}=\lambda\textbf{h}

is called a
\lambda
-harmonic function
.

Exercise 3.4.13

Let
H=\{H_{i}\}
and
V=\{V_{j}\}
be horizontal and vertical cylinder decompositions
of a quadratic differential
M
, and let
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
be the configuration
graph of the corresponding core curves.
Suppose that each horizontal cylinder intersects finitely many vertical cylinders and
vice versa
. Show that the union of the core curves of the cylinders in
H\cup V
fills
M
,
i
.
e
., their complement in
M
is a union of discs with at most one puncture. On the other hand, show that the core curves of the horizontal and vertical cylinder decompositions of the infinite-type translation surface
M
in Example
2.4
, Chapter
2
, do not fill.

We now present the main construction that extends to infinite-type surfaces
the Thurston-Veech construction of Exercise
1.2.6
.

Theorem 3.4.14

Let
S
be topological surface,
\alpha=\cup_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}
and
\beta=\cup_{j\in J}\beta_{j}
two multicurves in minimal position whose union fills
S
.
Suppose that:

•

C1
. The configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
has finite degree (i.e., there is an upper bound on the degree of the vertices in
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
),

•

C2
. For every component
D
of the complement of
\alpha\cup\beta
in
S
, its boundary
\partial D
in
S
is connected.

•

C3
. For every component
D
of the complement of
\alpha\cup\beta
for which
\partial D
intersects infinitely many curves in
\alpha\cup\beta
, then
D
is a disk
without
punctures.

Let
\textbf{h}:\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function, where
\lambda>1
.

Then, there exist a half-translation structure
M=M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})

on
S
which is a Hooper-Thurson-Veech surface of modulus
1/\lambda
whose
horizontal cylinders (respectively vertical cylinders) have core curves

\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(respectively
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j\in J}
).

Remark 3.4.15

Any connected infinite graph of finite degree has a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function if
\lambda
is large enough. Moreover, we always have that
\lambda\geq 2
. A more detailed discussion of this fact can be found in Appendix
B
.
For the configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
connectedness and finite degree are guaranteed by the fact
that
\alpha\cup\beta
fills the surface
S
and contidion

C1
above respectively.

Remark 3.4.16

Note that if
S
is of finite-type then the conditions
C1
,
C2
and
C3
above are satisfied automatically and the result follows from the Thurston-Veech construction as explained in Section
1.2.6
.

Proof.

The union
\alpha\cup\beta
of the multicurves
\alpha
and
\beta
define a graph embedded in
S
: the vertices are points in
\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\alpha_{i}\cap\beta_{j}
and edges are the connected components of
\alpha\cup\beta
minus its vertices. It is important not to confuse the (geometric) graph
\alpha\cup\beta
with the (abstract) configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
.

To define the quadratic differential we consider a dual embedded graph
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
in
S
. When all complementary components of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
have a compact boundary
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
is just the dual graph of
\alpha\cup\beta
. In the case when there are complementary components of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
whose boundary is formed by infinitely many vertices and edges we need to define
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
more carefully. The vertices of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
are defined as follows:

•

A1
. For every connected component
D
of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
such that
\partial D
is formed by finitely many edges of
\alpha\cup\beta
choose a unique point
v_{D}
inside the connected component. If
D
is a punctured disc, then choose
v_{D}
to be the puncture.

•

A2
. If
D
is a connected component of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
such that
\partial D
is formed by infinitely many edges of
\alpha\cup\beta
, then by hypothesis
C3
above
D
is homeomorphic to a disc. Moreover,
D\cup\partial D
has only one end to which we associate an ideal point
v_{D}
. We think of
v_{D}
as a vertex “at infinity” of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
, see Figure
3.8
.

(a)

\partial D
has finitely many sides

(b)

\partial D
has infinitely many sides

Figure 3.8
:
Edges of the graph
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
.

The points
v_{D}
, as chosen above, are the vertices of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
. Two vertices in this graph are joined by an edge in
S
if the closures of the corresponding connected components of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
share an edge of
\alpha\cup\beta
. Edges are chosen to be pairwise disjoint. For edges with vertices at infinity, edges are chosen to be proper rays defining the end of
D\cup\partial D
associated to
v_{D}
, see Figure
3.8
.

Given that
\alpha\cup\beta
fills and the boundary
\partial D
of each complementary component
D
of
\alpha\cup\beta
is connected (hypothesis
C2
), every connected component
S\setminus(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
is a topological quadrilateral which contains a unique vertex of
\alpha\cup\beta
. Hence there is a well defined bijection between edges in the abstract graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
and the set of these quadrilaterals. This way, for every edge
e\in E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))
we denote by
R_{e}
the closure in
S
of the corresponding topological quadrilateral with the convention to add to
R_{e}
vertices
v_{D}
corresponding to punctures
in
S
and points at infinity (as chosen above) so that
R_{e}
has four vertices.

Note that there are only two sides of
R_{e}
intersecting the multicurve
\alpha
, which henceforth are called
vertical sides
. The other two sides are in consequence called
horizontal
, see Figure
3.9
.

We now build a half-translation atlas on
S
by mapping the topological
quadrilaterals
R_{e}
of the dual graph
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
into Euclidean
rectangles. As in the finite-type case,
any positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
\textbf{h}:\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
gives
us compatible heights of horizontal and vertical cylinders. As noted in Remark
3.4.1
, given that the union of the multicurves
\alpha
and
\beta
fill the surface and hypothesis
C1
, we can always count on the existence of such a function. Let us define the maps

p_{\alpha}:E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\to V(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{\beta}:E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\to V(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))

(3.24)

which associates to an edge
e
of the configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)

its endpoints
p_{\alpha}(e)
in
I
and
p_{\beta}(e)
in
J
. Our aim is to
construct a homeomorphism

R_{e}\longrightarrow[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\beta}(e)]\times[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\alpha}(e)]

(3.25)

that will determine a unique half-translation atlas for
S
with the required
properties (see Figure
3.9
).

Figure 3.9
:
Transforming topological Rectangles into Euclidean rectangles.

In order to ensure compatibility of the atlas we first consider mapping
of the edges. For each oriented edge
c
of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
crossing
a component
\alpha_{i}
of
\alpha
we choose an homeomorphism

\theta_{c}:c\to[0,\textbf{h}(i)]
such that

•

the start point of
c
is mapped to
0
and its endpoint to
\textbf{h}(i)
,

•

c^{-1}(\textbf{h}(i)/2)
is the intersection of
c
with
\alpha_{i}
,

•

if
c^{\prime}
is the same edge with opposite orientation
\theta_{c^{\prime}}(x)=\textbf{h}(i)-\theta_{c}(x)
.

Then for each rectangle
R_{e}
(corresponding to an
edge
e
of
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
) and one of its horizontal
sides
c_{1}
we construct a map
\psi_{e,c_{1}}
as follows.
Choose a cyclic ordering of the sides
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
following
the orientation of the surface. Then define on the boundary
\partial R_{e}

\psi_{e,c_{1}}:x\mapsto\begin{cases}(\theta_{c_{1}}(x),0)&\text{if $x\in c_{1}$}\\
(\textbf{h}(j),\theta_{c_{2}}(x))&\text{if $x\in c_{2}$}\\
(\textbf{h}(j)-\theta_{c_{3}}(x),\textbf{h}(i))&\text{if $x\in c_{3}$}\\
(0,\textbf{h}(i)-\theta_{c_{4}}(x))&\text{if $x\in c_{4}$}\end{cases}

Note that by our choice of compatibilities of the maps
\theta_{c}
we have that

\psi_{e,c_{1}}|_{\partial R_{e}}=-\psi_{e,c^{\prime}_{3}}|_{\partial R_{e}}
where

c^{\prime}_{3}
is the edge
c_{3}
with reversed orientation.

Now, we extend each
\psi_{e,c_{1}}
into a homeomorphism such that

•

\alpha_{i}\cap R_{e}
is mapped to
[0,\textbf{h}(j)]\times\{\textbf{h}(i)/2\}
,

•

\beta_{j}\cap R_{e}
is mapped to
\{\textbf{h}(j)/2\}\times[0,\textbf{h}(i)]
,

•

\psi_{e,c_{1}}=-\psi_{e,c^{\prime}_{3}}
.

We claim that the family
\{\psi_{e,c}\}
we constructed above extends to a unique
half-translation atlas on
S
. First of all, the coordinate change between

\psi_{e,c_{1}}
and
\psi_{e,c^{\prime}_{3}}
is a point reflection. Notice also that each
point in the interior of a rectangle is covered by two charts. Now, for each
edge
c
in the intersection of two neighbouring rectangles
R_{e}
and
R_{e^{\prime}}
, the
compatibility of the maps
\psi_{e}
on the boundaries via the maps
\theta_{c}
can be
used to provide charts compatible with the atlas.

Note that the argument is similar to the one we used in the introduction to show that a
constructive translation surface (obtained from gluing polygons) is also a geometric
translation surface (endowed with a translation atlas). See
item
1
of Theorem
1.1.3
.

We denote the resulting half-translation surface
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
.

Now, for every
i\in I
, the curve
\alpha_{i}
is the core curve of the horizontal
cylinder
H_{i}:=\cup_{e\in p_{\alpha}^{-1}(i)}R_{e}
. Because
h
is harmonic we have

\sum_{e\in p_{\alpha}^{-1}(i)}\textbf{h}(p_{\beta}(e))=\sum_{j\sim i}\textbf{h}(j)=\lambda\textbf{h}(i).

In other words, the circumference
\sum_{e\in p_{\alpha}^{-1}(i)}\textbf{h}(p_{\beta}(e))
of

H_{i}
is
\lambda
times its height
\textbf{h}(i)
. That is, the modulus
\mu(H_{i})
of
H_{i}

is equal to
\frac{1}{\lambda}
. The same computation with
\beta_{j}
shows that the vertical cylinders

V_{j}:=\cup_{e\in p_{\beta}^{-1}(j)}R_{e}
have core curve
\beta_{j}

and modulus
\frac{1}{\lambda}
.

∎

Remark 3.4.17

Let
M=M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
be a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface as in Theorem
3.4.1
. For every connected component
D
of
S\setminus\alpha\cup\beta
we have that:

1.

If
\partial D
is formed by
2k
edges of
\alpha\cup\beta
then the vertex
v_{D}
of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
is a conical singularity of total angle
k\pi
in
20
20

20

For
k=2
, the vertex
v_{D}
is a regular point of
M
.

\operatorname{Sing}(M)
and all other points of
D
are regular.

2.

If
\partial D
is formed by infinitely many edges of
\alpha\cup\beta
then the vertex
v_{D}
of
(\alpha\cup\beta)^{*}
is either an infinite angle singularity or a wild singularity.

This follows from the fact that in the proof of Theorem
3.4.1
we are glueing Euclidean rectangles with a common vertex at
v_{D}
. When
v_{D}
is an infinite angle or wild singularity, it has a bi-infinite rotational component
21
21

21

i
.
e
., the map
\widetilde{\rm dir}
restricted to each rotational component defines a bijection with
\mathbb{R}
, see (
2.9
), in Section
2.4.2
.
. Moreover, the existence of such bi-infinite rotational component among the rotational components of
v_{D}
does not depend on the positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h
. However, not all rotational components of linear approaches to
v_{D}
are bi-infinite. In the next section we see that baker’s surfaces are examples of Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces. All surfaces in this family have a single wild singularity which has bi-infinite rotational components but also bounded rotational components if
\lambda>2
.

In the rest of this Chapter we illustrate with several examples how to determine explicitly the quadratic differential
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
.

Example 3.4.18

In the following paragraphs we show that the infinite staircase and all
\lambda
-staircases are Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces homeomorphic to the Loch Ness monster. Figure
3.10
shows a Loch Ness monster with two multicurves
\alpha
and
\beta
in minimal position satisfying contidions
C1
–
C4
of Theorem
3.4.1
and the corresponding configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
, to which we refer hereafter as the
\mathbb{Z}
-graph because its vertices are indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
.

(a)

Two oriented multicurves
\alpha
(in blue) and
\beta
(in red) in the Loch Ness monster for which the Hooper-Thurston-Veech construction produces the infinite staircase.

(b)

The
\mathbb{Z}
-graph

Figure 3.10
:
The infinite staircase as a Hooper-Thurson-Veech surface.

A
\lambda
-harmonic function on the
\mathbb{Z}
-graph satisfies

\textbf{h}(n+1)=\lambda\textbf{h}(n)-\textbf{h}(n-1)

(3.26)

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is a linear recurrence relation whose characteristic polynomial is
x^{2}-\lambda x+1
. By Remark
3.4.1
(see also Appendix
B
) we know that
\lambda\geq 2
. Hence let
r_{+}:=\frac{\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}
and
r_{-}:=\frac{1}{r_{+}}
be the corresponding roots. There are two cases to consider:

•

\lambda=2
. Since
r_{+}=r_{-}=1
the general solution of (
3.26
) is of the form
\textbf{h}(n)=C+Dn
. Positive solutions only exist if
D=0
and hence the only
positive
\lambda
-harmonic function in this case (up to scaling) is
\textbf{h}(n)=1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•

\lambda>2
. For each fixed value of
\lambda
, the general solution of (
3.26
) is a linear combination of the positive eigenfunctions
\textbf{h}_{1}(n)=r_{+}^{n}
and
\textbf{h}_{2}(n)=r_{+}^{-n}
.

We now explain a systematic way to picture
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
. In general it can be difficult to visualize the graph
\alpha\cup\beta
and how the rectangles lie in
S
. The following steps are intented to overcome this difficulty.

1.

Orientations
. Fix an orientation on each of the curves in
\alpha\cup\beta
and the standard orientation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
\psi_{e,c_{1}}
be an orientation preserving homeomorphism as defined in the proof of Theorem
3.4.1
.
The orientation of each component of
\alpha
and
\beta
,
induces an orientation of the arcs
\psi_{e,c1}(\alpha\cap R_{e})
and
\psi_{e,c1}(\beta\cap R_{e})
.
We say that
R_{e}
is
positive
(respectively
negative
) if the algebraic intersection (w.r.t. the orientations just described) of
\psi_{e,c_{1}}(\alpha\cap R_{e})
with
\psi_{e,c_{1}}(\beta\cap R_{e})
is positive (respect. negative). In Figures
3.11
and
3.12
we illustrate positive and negative rectangles.

2.

Gluing rectangles
. The orientation of elements in
\alpha\cup\beta
defines two permutations

r:E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\to E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054ptu:E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))\to E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))

(3.27)

as follows. Let
e\in E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))
be determined by a point
p_{ij}\in\alpha_{i}\cap\beta_{j}
and let
p_{ij^{\prime}}\in\alpha_{i}\cap\beta_{j}
be the point of
\alpha_{i}\cap\beta
that follows from
p_{ij}
when considering the orientation of
\alpha_{i}
. If
e^{\prime}\in E(\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta))
denotes the edge determined by
p_{ij^{\prime}}
then
r(e)=e^{\prime}
. The permutation
u
is defined in an analogous way by considering points in
\alpha\cap\beta_{j}
. Note that for every vertex
i\in I
(respectively
j\in J
) of
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
,
r
defines a cyclic permutation of edges adjacent to
i
(respect.
u
for edges adjacent to
j
).

As discussed in the proof of Theorem
3.4.1
, the rectangles
[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\beta}(e)]\times[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\alpha}(e)]
are glued along parallel edges following the configuration of the multicirves
\alpha\cup\beta
. More precisely: for each
e\in E
is glued to the right side of the Euclidean rectangle
[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\beta}(e)]\times[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\alpha}(e)]
to the left side of
R_{r(e)}
using a translation if both rectangles
R_{e}
and
R_{r(e)}
are positive or using a half-translation if both rectangles are negative. On the other the upper side of
[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\beta}(e)]\times[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{\alpha}(e)]
is glued to the lower side of
R_{u(e)}
always using a translation.

3.

Singularities
. Remove all corners of rectangles glued as above which correspond to marked points in
S
or which produce singularities which are not conical.

Figure 3.11
:
Orientation of the multicurves
\alpha\cup\beta
inducing an orientation on the rectangles
R_{e}
in
S
.

Figure 3.12
:
Orientation of the Euclidean rectangles
R_{e}
induced by the homeomorphism
\psi_{e}
.

To visualize concretely the steps explained above consider the oriented multicurves depicted in Figure
3.10
and the
\lambda
-harmonic function
\textbf{h}(n)=1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
of the adjacency operator of the
\mathbb{Z}
-graph (
i
.
e
., case
\lambda=2
). For each edge
e
in the
\mathbb{Z}
-graph the rectangle
R_{e}
is positive and identified with a unit square, therefore all gluings of these are done using translations. Therefore
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
is a translation surface. Moreover, it is one of our favorites examples in this book: the infinite staircase. See Figure
3.10
.

h
of the

-graph

and

in Chapter

.

-graph. Construct the quadratic differential
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
following the steps explained in Example
3.4.1
