., there are changes of coordinates in the corresponding atlas that are half-translations.

Constructing Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces from bipartite graphs

In the preceding section, we have shown that a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface is encoded by a pair of multicurves
\alpha
and
\beta
(the core curves of the horizontal and vertical cylinders) and a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
\textbf{h}:\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
of the configuration graph (which encodes the heights of cylinders). As explained in Example
3.4.1
, an orientation of the components of
\alpha
and
\beta
defines a pair of permutations
r,u
on the edges of
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
(see (
3.27
)) which encode how cylinders intersect. In this section we explain how to construct a Hooper-Thurston-Veech from an abstract bipartite graph of finite degree, a pair of permutations and a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function. This is the way the construction is presented in
[
Hoo15
]
.

Definition 3.4.22

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a bipartite graph with vertices
I\sqcup J
. A
ribbon structure
on
\mathcal{G}
is a choice
for each vertex
v
of a cyclic ordering to the edges adjacent to
v
. In other words, it is a pair of
permutations
r:E(\mathcal{G})\to E(\mathcal{G})
and
u:E(\mathcal{G})\to E(\mathcal{G})
such that for each edge adjacent to

i\in I
(respectively
j\in J
) the orbit of
i
by
r
(respectively of
j
by
u
) are the edges adjacent
to
i
(respectively
j
).

Theorem 3.4.23
(
[
Hoo15
]
)

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a connected bipartite graph of finite degree endowed with a ribbon
structure. Then for every positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h

of
\mathcal{G}
there is an Abelian differential
M=M(\mathcal{G},h)
which is a
Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface of modulus
\frac{1}{\lambda}
. Let
H=\{H_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and

V=\{V_{j}\}_{j\in J}
be the corresponding horizontal and vertical cylinder
decompositions; and
\alpha
and
\beta
be the multicurves given by their core curves. Then
\mathcal{G}
is the configuration graph
\mathcal{G}(\alpha\cup\beta)
.

Proof.

We sketch the main ingredients of the proof. Let
V(\mathcal{G})=I\sqcup J
be the set of vertices and
E(\mathcal{G})
the set of edges of the bipartite graph
\mathcal{G}
. We have natural projections:

p_{I}:E(\mathcal{G})\to I\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptp_{J}:E(\mathcal{G})\to J

which assign to an edge
e=ij
the vertices
p_{I}(e)=i
and
p_{j}(e)=j
. Consider a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h
of
\mathcal{G}
and for each
e\in E(\mathcal{G})
define
R_{e}=[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{J}(e)]\times[0,\textbf{h}\circ p_{I}(e)]
. The ribbon structure of
\mathcal{G}
defines a pair of permutations

r:E(\mathcal{G})\to E(\mathcal{G})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptu:E(\mathcal{G})\to E(\mathcal{G})

as follows:
r(e)=\sigma_{p_{I}(e)}(e)
and
u(e)=\sigma_{p_{J}(e)}(e)
, where
\sigma_{p_{I}(e)}
and
\sigma_{p_{J}(e)}
are the permutations given by the ribbon structure on edges adjacent to
p_{I}(e)
and
p_{J}(e)
respectively. Thus, the
r
-orbit (respectively
u
-orbit) of
e
is formed by the all edges adjacent to
I(e)
(respect.
J(e)
). To obtained the desired translation surface glue using a translation the right side of the Euclidean rectangle
R_{e}
to the left side of
R_{r(e)}
, and the upper side of
R_{e}
to the bottom side of
R_{u(e)}
. The rest of the proof follows from arguments analogous to the ones used in the proof of Theorem
3.4.1
.
∎

Remark 3.4.24

Note that the steps of the construction of
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
are very similar to those explained in Example
3.4.1
, except that we do not have to care about orientations for there is no surface involved in the input data. On the other hand, if we consider
\alpha
and
\beta
the multicurves formed by the core curves of the cylinder decompositions
H
and
V
of
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
respectively, then
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
is equal to
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
, the Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface given by Theorem
3.4.1
.

Exercise 3.4.25

Let
h
be a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function used to construct a Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface
M
of the form
M(\alpha,\beta,\textbf{h})
or
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
. Show that the area of
M
is given by

\operatorname{Area}(M)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{v\in V(\mathcal{G})}\textbf{h}_{v}^{2}.

Deduce that
h
is a sequence in
\ell^{2}(V)
if and only the surface
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
has finite area.

Example 3.4.26:
The
\mathbb{N}
-graph

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the graph depicted in Figure
3.13
. Henceforth we call
\mathcal{G}
the
\mathbb{N}
-graph and as indicated in the Figure vertices are indexed by the non-negative integers.
Given that every vertex except one has degree 2, there is a unique ribbon structure on this graph. As in Example
3.4.1
, a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h
on
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the (linear recurrence) relation
\textbf{h}(n)=\lambda\textbf{h}(n-1)-\textbf{h}(n-2)
, for
n\geq 2
with initial condition
\lambda\textbf{h}(0)=\textbf{h}(1)
. The corresponding characteristic polynomial is
x^{2}-\lambda x+1
. For
\lambda=2
there is only one root and the general solution is of the form
\textbf{h}(n)=A+Bn
,
A,B\in\mathbb{R}
.

Figure 3.13
:
The
\mathbb{N}
-graph.

Let’s now consider the case of simple roots
r_{+}>r_{-}
corresponding to
\lambda>2
. The general solution in this case is of the form
\textbf{h}(n)=Ar_{+}^{n}+Br_{-}^{n}
.

Exercise 3.4.27

Draw the infinite area surface
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
for
\mathcal{G}
equal to
\mathbb{N}
-graph and
h
is the
2
-harmonic function
\textbf{h}(n)=n+1
. For
\lambda>2
and
\textbf{h}(0)=r_{+}-r_{-}
show that
\textbf{h}(n)=(r_{+}^{n+1}-r_{-}^{n+1})
is a
\lambda
-harmonic function and draw the corresponding Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface
M(\mathcal{G},\textbf{h})
. You should get in both cases something that resembles a staircase going upwards. One can turn the
\mathbb{N}
-graph into a metric graph by declaring that each edge has length 1. Consider then the subset of each surface corresponding to the ball of radius
n
in the
\mathbb{N}
-graph centered at the origin. Denote this subset
Y_{n}(\lambda)
. Compare the growth of the area of
Y_{n}(\lambda)
for parameters
\lambda=2
and
\lambda>2
.

Example 3.4.28

Modified
\mathbb{N}
-graph
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{k}
be the graph obtained by adding
k\geq 1
vertices of valence 1 to the vertex
0
in the
\mathbb{N}
-graph, see Figure
3.14
.

Figure 3.14
:
Modified
\mathbb{N}-
graph
\mathcal{G}_{k}
.

Henceforth we call
\mathcal{G}_{k}
the modified
\mathbb{N}
-graph. Label by
\{(-1,1),\ldots,(-1,k)\}
the
k
vertices of valence 1 adjacent to
0
the only vertex of valence
n+1
. Any positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h
satisfies
\lambda\textbf{h}((-1,j))=\textbf{h}(0)
for all
j=1,\ldots,k
and
\textbf{h}(1)=\textbf{h}(0)(\lambda-\frac{k}{\lambda})
. For every
n\geq 1
we have the same linear recurrence relations as in Example
3.4.1
, namely
\textbf{h}(n)=\lambda\textbf{h}(n-1)-\textbf{h}(n-2)
. Its characteristic polynomial is
x^{2}-\lambda x+1
. There are hence two cases to consider:

1.

\lambda=2
. Here the general solution of the linear recurrence relation that a
2
-harmonic function must satisfy is of the form
\textbf{h}(n)=A+Bn
. By evaluating in
n=0
and
n=1
, and substituing the value for
\textbf{h}(1)
calculated above we obtain that
\textbf{h}(n)=\textbf{h}(0)+(\lambda\textbf{h}(0)-\frac{k}{\lambda})n
. The only possible values that yield a positive
2
-harmonic function are
k=1
(which is just the case of the
\mathbb{N}
-graph treated before) and
k=2
.

Exercise 3.4.29

Show that for
k=2
the corresponding Hooper-Thurston-Veech surface is tiled by infinitely many unit squares but is not isometric to the infinite staircase.

2.

\lambda>2
. In this case the general solution of the linear recurrence relation
that
h
satifies must satisfy of the form

\textbf{h}(n)=Ar_{+}^{n}+Br_{-}^{n}

(3.28)

(where
r_{+}>r_{-}
are the roots of the characteristic polynomial). Therefore:

\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}=\frac{\textbf{h}(0)}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\begin{pmatrix}-r_{-}&1\\
r_{+}&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\lambda-\frac{k}{\lambda}\end{pmatrix}

Hence:

\textbf{h}(n)=\frac{\textbf{h}(0)}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\left((\lambda-\frac{k}{\lambda}-r_{-})r_{+}^{n}-(r_{+}-\lambda+\frac{k}{\lambda})r_{-}^{n}\right)

(3.29)

Exercise 3.4.30

k\geq 2
fixed, the condition
