, each singularity is part of a connection. We now perturb
f_{1}
as follows.
Let
(\delta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a decreasing sequence of positive real numbers so that
\delta_{i}+\delta_{i+1}<\frac{1}{3^{i}}
.
Let us define the following operation on intervals
B([x,y],a,b):=[x-a,y+b]
.
We define a new interval exchange transformation
f_{2}
by setting

I^{\prime top}_{a_{1}}:=I^{top}_{a_{1}}=\left[0,1/3\right]\hskip 14.22636ptI^{\prime top}_{c}:=\left[1/3,1/3+\delta_{1}\right]

I^{\prime top}_{a_{i}}:=B(I^{top}_{a_{i}},\delta_{i},\delta_{i})\hskip 14.22636ptI^{\prime top}_{b_{i}}:=B(I^{top}_{b_{i}},-\delta_{i},-\delta_{i+1})

I^{\prime bot}_{a_{1}}:=I^{bot}_{a_{1}}=\left[2/3,1\right]\hskip 14.22636ptI^{\prime bot}_{c}:=\left[2/3-\delta_{1},2/3\right]

I^{\prime bot}_{a_{i}}:=B(I^{bot}_{a_{i}},\delta_{i},\delta_{i})\hskip 14.22636ptI^{\prime bot}_{b_{i}}:=B(I^{bot}_{b_{i}},-\delta_{i+1},-\delta_{i}).

These new intervals define an interval exchange transformation
f_{2}
on
[0,1]
(see Figure
4.11(c)
).
By construction,
f_{1}=f_{2}
on each of
I^{top}_{a_{i}}
. In particular,
f_{2}
preserves the triadic Cantor set.

Now we claim that if the
\delta_{i}

•

are rationally independent,

•

\delta_{i+1}<\delta_{i}
, and

•

\lambda_{b_{i}}=\frac{1}{3^{i}}-\delta_{i}-\delta_{i+1}<2(\delta_{i}-\delta_{i+1})

then
f_{2}
has no saddle connection.

On each interval
I^{\prime top}_{x}
we denote by
\tau_{x}
the corresponding translation modulo
\mathbb{Q}
. We have

\tau_{a_{i}}=0\qquad\tau_{c}=-\delta_{1}\qquad\tau_{b_{i}}=\delta_{i+1}-\delta_{i}

Let us denote by
I^{top}_{a_{i},\ell}
(respectively
I^{top}_{a_{i},r}
) the left
and right part added to the interval
I^{top}_{a_{i}}
to become
I^{\prime top}_{a_{i}}

and let us consider the induced interval exchange transformation on the unions
of the
a_{i,\ell}
,
a_{i,r}
for
i\geq 2
.

1.

all points in
I^{\prime top}_{a_{i},r}
go into
I^{\prime top}_{a_{i-1},r}
except for
i=2
for which
T(I^{\prime top}_{a_{2,r}})\subset I^{\prime top}_{c}
.

2.

the points in
I^{\prime top}_{a_{i},\ell}
either go in one step in
I^{top}_{a_{i+1},\ell}
or in many steps through
b_{i}
and
a_{i}
in
I^{top}_{a_{i},r}
.

Figure 4.12
:
The translations modulo
\mathbb{Q}
of the induced map on the added extremities of
I^{top}_{a_{i}}
. The value of
n_{i}
is always a number larger than
2
.

We now prove that
f_{2}
has no connection. Recall that a connection is a triple

(x,y,m)
where
x
is a backward singularity,
y
is a forward singularity and

(f_{2})^{m}(x)=y
. The backward or forward singularity modulo
\mathbb{Q}
are

-\delta_{i}
and
+\delta_{i}
for respectively the left and right side of the
interval labelled
a_{i}
. We denote
\delta(a_{i,\ell})=-\delta_{i}
and

\delta(a_{i,r})=\delta_{i}
.

Recall that a connection is a finite orbit starting at a backward singularity
x

and ending at a forward singularity
y
. This could be written
x+\tau=y

where
\tau
is the sum of the translations realized along the orbit of
x
. For our
purpose we consider this equation modulo
\mathbb{Q}
. As we already mentioned the values of

x
and
y
are
\pm\delta_{i}
depending whether they belong to
a_{i,\ell}
or

a_{i,r}
. The value of
\tau
is obtained by following the orbit along the graph
of
4.12
and summing the weights on the edges. By the assumption on the linear independence of the
\delta_{i}
no path exists that would lead to
an equation of the form
\delta(u)+\tau(\gamma)=\delta(v)
where

\gamma
is an oriented path in the graph from
u
to
v
and
\tau(\gamma)

is the sum of the weights along
\gamma
.
∎

4.4
Entropy

The entropy of a dynamical system is a non-negative real number that quantifies the degree of disorder of a transformation. We recall its definition in order to fix notations and refer the reader to
[
CFS82a
]
,
[
Dow11
]
,
[
VO16
]
or
[
DK21
]
for more details.

Let
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
be a standard Borel probability space. Given a (measurable) countable partition
\mathcal{P}
of
X
we define its
entropy
relative to
\mu
as

H(\mu,\mathcal{P})=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{P}}-\mu(A)\log(\mu(A)).

A finite partition of cardinality
n
has entropy less than
\log(n)
, because the function
x\to-x\log(x)
is concave on
(0,1)
. On the other hand, an infinite partition might have infinite entropy (
e.g.
Example 9.1.4 in
[
VO16
]
). For any countable family of partitions
\mathcal{P}_{n}
define:

\bigvee_{n}\mathcal{P}_{n}:=\{\cap_{n}P_{n}\hskip 2.84526pt|\hskip 2.84526ptP_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\hskip 2.84526pt\text{for each $n$}\}.

Given a measurable transformation
T:X\rightarrow X
preserving the measure
\mu
and a partition
\mathcal{P}
with finite entropy
, the (metric)
entropy of
f
relative to
\mathcal{P}
is given by

h(T,\mu,\mathcal{P})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{H\left(\mu,\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}T^{-k}\mathcal{P}\right)}{n}.

The above limit is equal to
\inf\frac{H\left(\mu,\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}T^{-k}\mathcal{P}\right)}{n}
as the sequence
n\mapsto H(\mu,\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}^{(n)})
is subadditive. The
entropy
of the transformation
T
is

h(T,\mu):=\sup_{\mathcal{P}}h(T,\mu,\mathcal{P})

(4.7)

where
\mathcal{P}
ranges over all partitions with finite entropy
5
5

5

As a matter of fact this definition is not affected if the supremum is taken over only over finite partitions.
. When the measure is implicit in the discussion we write just
h(T)
for the entropy of the transformation
T
,
e.g.
for IETs Lebesgue is the implicit measure.

Exercise 4.4.1

1.

Let
T
be a measure preserving transformation on
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and suppose that
X=A\sqcup B
with
A
and
B
measurable and
T
-invariant. Show that
h(T,\mu)=\mu(A)h(T|_{A},\nu_{A})+\mu(B)h(T|_{B},\nu_{B})
where

\nu_{A}
and
\nu_{B}
are the probability measures on
A
and
B
obtained by restricting
\mu
and renormalizing, respectively.

2.

Let
T
be a measure preserving transformation on
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
such that for every
x\in X
the orbit
\{T^{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is finite. Show that
h(T,\mu)
is zero.

3.

Let
T_{i}:(X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i},\mu_{i})
,
i=1,2
, be measurable-equivalent measure preserving transformations on two probability spaces
6
6

6

That is to say there exist measurable sets of full measure
X_{i}^{\prime}\subset X_{i}
and a measurable bijection
\phi:X_{1}^{\prime}\to X_{2}^{\prime}
with measurable inverse such that
\phi_{*}\mu=\nu
and
\phi\circ T_{1}=T_{2}\circ\phi

. Prove that
h(T_{1},\mu_{1})=h(T_{2},\mu_{2})
.

As we show later (see Corollary
4.4
), finite interval exchange transformations have zero entropy. The main aim of this section is to give an upper bound estimate for the measured entropy of an infinite interval exchange. More precisely:

Theorem 4.4.2

Let
f=f_{\pi,\lambda}
be an interval exchange transformation on
[0,1]
.
Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots
be the lengths of the subintervals (ordered by decreasing sizes) and
\Lambda_{m}:=\lambda_{m+1}+\lambda_{m+2}+\ldots
. Then

h(f)\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\log(m)\Lambda_{m}.

We also recall the construction (see Example
4.4
) of an infinite IET having infinite entropy.

Theorem
4.4
is a slightly modified version of a result that appear in
[
Blu12
]
. For its proof we use some corollaries of Kolmogorov-Sinai’s theorem (see
[
VO16
]
for details) and an estimate on the combinatorial complexity of interval exchange transformations. We address these tools in what follows.

Theorem 4.4.3
(Kolmogorov-Sinai)

Let
T
be a measurable, measure-preserving transformation of a probability space
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and

\mathcal{P}_{1}\prec\ldots\prec\mathcal{P}_{n}\prec\ldots
a non-decreasing sequence of partitions with finite entropy such that
\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{n}
generates the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
, up to measure zero. Then

h(T,\mu)=\lim_{n\to\infty}h(T,\mu,\mathcal{P}_{n})

Recall that given two partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
we say that
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is finer than
\mathcal{P}_{2}
(and denote it by
\mathcal{P}_{1}\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}
) if every element of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is contained in some element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. On the other hand, a subset
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}
generates the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
up to measure zero if the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is equal to
\mathcal{B}
up to measure zero
7
7

7

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be any two subsets of a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
is a probability space
(X,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}
up to measure zero if for every
B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
there exists
B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
such that
\mu(B_{1}\Delta B_{2})=0
, where
B_{1}\Delta B_{2}=(B_{1}\cup B_{2})\setminus(B_{1}\cap B_{2})
.

Corollary 4.4.4

Suppose that
X
is a metric space with a Borel probability measure
\mu
. Let
T:X\to X
be a measurable, measure-preserving transformation and
\mathcal{P}_{1}\prec\ldots\prec\mathcal{P}_{n}\prec\ldots
a non-decreasing sequence of partitions with finite entropy. Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}(x)
denote the element of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
containing
x\in X
. Suppose that for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
:

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm diam}(\mathcal{P}_{n}(x))=0.

Then
h(T,\mu)=\lim_{n\to\infty}h(T,\mu,\mathcal{P}_{n})

Combinatorial complexity
. Let
f:D\to R
be a partial interval exchange transformation on
I
and
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{a}\}_{a\in A}
a finite partition of
I
. For every
n\geq 1
one can consider the map
D_{n}\to A^{n}
which associates to an orbit of length
n
,
x
,
fx
,
f^{2}x
, …,
f^{n-1}x
its coding
w_{0}w_{1}w_{2}\ldots w_{n-1}
with respect to the partition
\mathcal{P}
. That is,
w_{i}
is the unique element of
A
so that
f^{i}x\in P_{w_{i}}
. The
combinatorial complexity
of a partial interval exchange transformation
f:D\to R
is the function
p:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
where
p(n)
is the number of different codings of orbits of length
n
of
f
. In other words,
p(n)
the cardinality of the image of the “coding map”
D_{n}\to A^{n}
.

Proposition 4.4.5

Let
f:D\to R
be a partial interval exchange transformation with finitely many intervals, let say
d
. Then its combinatorial complexity with respect to the set of connected components of
D
satisfies
p(n)\leq(d-1)n+1
. Moreover, if it is an interval exchange transformation without connections then
p(n)=(d-1)n+1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{d}\}
be the set of connected components of
D
. We consider the coding of
f
with respect to this natural partition. Let
f^{n}:D_{n}\to D_{-n}
denote the
n
-th iterate of
f
. Now to each coding
w=w_{0}w_{1}\ldots w_{n-1}
of length
n
with respect to
\mathcal{I}
, one can associate the interval
I_{w}\subset D
formed by all points whose orbit of length
n
has exactly the coding
w
. Namely

I_{w}=I_{w_{0}}\cap f^{-1}(D_{-1}\cap I_{w_{1}})\cap f^{-2}(D_{-2}\cap I_{w_{2}})\cap\ldots f^{-n-1}(D_{-n-1}\cap I_{w_{n-1}}).

I_{w}
is a maximal interval of the domain
D_{n-1}
of
f^{n-1}
. Therefore
p(n)
is equal to the number of connected components of
D_{n-1}
(or equivalently of
D_{-n+1}
). Note that
D_{n}=f^{-n+1}(D_{-n+1}\cap D)
. Hence, as
f^{-n+1}
is continuous on
D_{-n+1}
, the number of connected components of
D_{n}
is exactly the number of connected components of
D_{-n+1}\cap D
. As
D
is made of
d
intervals, this number increases at most by
d-1
as we iterate
f
. Therefore
p(n)
is bounded above by
d+(n-1)(d-1)=(d-1)n+1
.
If
f
is an interval exchange transformation without connections, each time we iterate
f
exactly
d-1
new separation points appear in the partition of
D_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.4.6

Finite interval exchange have zero entropy.

Proof.

A classical result by Keane
[
Kea75
]
(that can be traced back to the work of Mayer
[
May43
]
, see also
[
ZK75
]
Section 2) implies that the domain of any IET on a finite number of intervals is decomposed into subsets where the IET is either periodic or minimal. By Exercise
4.4
it is sufficient to show that the restriction of
f
to a minimal componet has entropy zero. Hence let
f:D\to R
be a minimal interval exchange transformation and
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{d}\}
be the corresponding intervals. Since there are no periodic components, Corollary
4.1.1
implies that for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that every interval
J\subset\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}f^{-k}(\mathcal{I})
has length
|J|<\varepsilon
. Hence
\{\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}f^{-k}(\mathcal{I})\}_{n\geq 1}
generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and therefore
h(f,\mu)=h(f,\mu,\mathcal{I})
. By Proposition
4.4
,
\displaystyle\#\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}f^{-k}(\mathcal{I})\leq(d-1)n+1
and thus

\frac{H(\mu,\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{I})}{n}\leq\frac{\log((d-1)n+1)}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0

∎

Example 4.4.7

We present a cutting and stacking construction for a map of positive entropy that appears in
[
Shi73
]
. On the first step as usual we consider the stack
\mathbf{S}(1,0)=(0,1)
. In the second step we cut the unit interval in two pieces of equal length but we do not stack them. This produces two stacks
\mathbf{S}_{1}(1,\frac{1}{2})=(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\mathbf{S}_{2}(1,\frac{1}{2})=(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Now suppose that at the
k^{th}
step,
k\geq 3
, one has stacks
\{\mathbf{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{S}_{q_{k}}\}
having all the same width
w_{k}
. For each
1\leq i\leq q_{k}
cut
\mathbf{S}_{i}
into stacks
\mathbf{S}_{i}^{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 2q_{k}
, according to the parameters
p_{ij}=\frac{w_{k}}{2q_{k}}
. Then stack them into
q_{k}^{2}
new stacks according to the rule:

\mathbf{S}_{i,j}:=\mathbf{S}_{j}^{i}\mathbf{S}_{q_{k}+i}^{j},\hskip 8.53581pt1\leq i\leq q_{k},\hskip 8.53581pt1\leq j\leq q_{k}.

(4.8)

Since each of the new
q_{k}^{2}
stacks has width
\frac{w_{k}}{2q_{k}}
the term defining the limit in
4.6
satisfies

\sum_{i=1,2,\ldots,q_{k}^{2}}w^{(k+1)}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1,2,\ldots,q_{k}}w^{(k)}_{i}

and therefore the construction defines an infinite IET
f
on
I=(0,1)
.

Figure 4.13
:
The first four levels of a Bratteli-Vershik diagram of a map of positive entropy.

The corresponding Bratteli-Vershik diagram is depicted in Figure
4.13
. Here
V_{0}=\{0\}
, for
k\geq 1
we set
V_{k}=\{0,1,\ldots,2^{2^{k-1}}-1\}
and arrows are defined by (
4.8
). There are several ways to calculate que entropy of
f
. For example, from
[
Shi73
]
explains that
h(f)
is given by
\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{n}q_{k}
, where
W_{k}
is the sum of the widths of the
q_{k}
stacks at the
k^{th}
step of the construction. Therefore

h(f)=\lim_{k\to\infty}(q_{k}w_{k})\log(q_{k})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\log(2^{2^{k}})=\log(2)=0.69314...

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

We assume that

C:=\liminf_{m\to\infty}\log(m)\Lambda_{m}<+\infty

(4.9)

otherwise there is nothing to prove.

Let us denote by
I_{i}
the subinterval of
[0,1]
of size
\lambda_{i}
.
We consider the finite partitions induced by the domain of the IET of the form
\alpha_{m}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{m},J_{m}\}
where

J_{m}:=\bigcup_{m^{\prime}>m}I_{m^{\prime}}.

Note that
\mu(J_{m})=\Lambda_{m}
.

Let
\alpha_{m}^{(n)}:=\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}f^{-k}\alpha_{m}
. We claim that

\displaystyle h(f)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(\mu,\alpha_{m}^{(n)})}{n}=\sup_{m\to\infty}\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(\mu,\alpha_{m}^{(n)})}{n}.

Indeed, since
H(\mu,\alpha_{m}^{(n)})
is subadditive we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(\mu,\alpha_{m}^{(n)})}{n}=\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(\mu,\alpha_{m}^{(n)})}{n}
. The sequence of partitions
\alpha_{m}
is nested, that is
\alpha_{m+1}
is a refinement of
\alpha_{m}
. Hence
h(\mu,\alpha_{m})\leq h(\mu,\alpha_{m+1})
and the limit in
m
is a sup. This justifies the right hand side equality above.

We now justify the left hand side equality above. First remark that the entropy of
f
is equal to the entropy of
f
restricted to the complement of periodic components. Now consider for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
the partition
\alpha_{k}^{(k)}
. Corollary
4.1.1
implies that for
\mu
-almost every
x
not in a periodic component
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\rm diam}\alpha_{k}^{(k)}(x)=0
. The claim then follows from Corollary
4.4
.

As a consequence, if
(m_{k})
is a diverging sequence and
(n_{k})
is any sequence, then

h(f)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{H(f,\alpha_{m_{k}}^{n_{k}})}{n_{k}}.

(simply because
H(f,\alpha_{m_{k}}^{n_{k}})/n_{k}\geq h(f,\mu,\alpha_{m_{k}})
).

We code the orbits of the interval with respect to the partition
\alpha_{m}
by using the letter
i
for the interval
I_{i}
and
*
for the interval
J_{m}
. Let
\Delta_{m,n,k}
be the set of finite codings of length
n
that contains exactly
k
times the letter
*
.
Let also
\Omega_{m,n,k}
be the subset of
[0,1]
that consists of points whose codings of length
n
belongs to
\Delta_{m,n,k}
. For example,
\Omega_{m,n,0}
is the set of points whose first
n
-th iterates never enter
J_{m}
.
Note that
\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{\Omega_{m,n,k}\}_{k=0}^{n}
is a partition of
[0,1]
that is coarser than
\alpha_{m}^{(n)}
.

It follows from Proposition
4.4
that
\#\Delta_{m,n,0}\leq(m-1)n+1\leq mn
. As a consequence, we deduce a naive estimate for
\#\Delta_{m,n,k}
. By decomposing over all possible positions for the symbol
*
we have

\#\Delta_{m,n,k}\leq\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(m\ell_{i})

where the sum runs over all non-negative integer vectors
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{k+1})
such that
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ldots+\ell_{k+1}=n-k
. The number of terms in the right hand side is
\binom{n}{k}
for which an upper bound is given by
\binom{n}{k}=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{n-i}{k-i}\leq n^{k}
. Using the trivial bound
\ell_{i}\leq n
, we obtain

\#\Delta_{m,n,k}\leq n^{k}\left(mn\right)^{k+1}\leq(n^{2}m)^{k+1}.

(4.10)

Now we claim that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}k\,\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k})=n\Lambda_{m}.

(4.11)

Indeed,
\mathcal{A}_{n}=\{\Omega_{m,n,k}\}_{k=0}^{n}
is a partition of
[0,1]
and the left
hand side in (
4.11
) is the expectation of the number of
letter
*
in a coding of length
n
. This is equal to
n
times the
expectation of a letter
*
which is
\Lambda_{m}=\mu(J_{m})
. More formally, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}k\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{J_{m}}+\mathbbm{1}_{J_{m}}\circ f+\ldots+\mathbbm{1}_{J_{m}}\circ f^{n-1}\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{J_{m}}\circ f^{k}d\mu

\displaystyle=n\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{J_{m}}d\mu=n\,\mu(J_{m})

where in the second line we used the linearity of the integral and in the third line the
f
-invariance of
\mu
. This concludes the proof of the claim.

Let us denote
g(x)=-x\log(x)
. In order to reduce entropy estimates to counting, we use the following inequality that follows from the concavity of the function
g
: if
A_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
are disjoint measurable sets whose union is
A
then
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}g(\mu(A_{i}))\leq\mu(A)\log(|\mathcal{I}|)+g(\mu(A))

8
8

8

this can also be shown as a consequence of the following formula for conditional entropy: if
\mathcal{P}=\{A_{1},A_{2},\ldots\}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots\}
are (measurable) partitions then
H(\mathcal{P}|\mathcal{Q})=\sum_{B\in\mathcal{Q}}\mu(B)H_{B}(\mathcal{P})
where
H_{B}(\mathcal{P})
is the entropy of
\mathcal{P}
for the measure
\mu_{B}
induced on
B
.
.
Now, using (
4.10
), we obtain:

\displaystyle H(\alpha_{m}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{w\in\Delta_{m,n,k}}g(\mu(I_{w}))

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k})\log\#\Delta_{m,n,k}+g(\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k}))\right)

(4.13)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k})\log\#\Delta_{m,n,k}\right)+H(\{\Omega_{m,n,k}\}_{k=0}^{n})

(4.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n}(k+1)\mu(\Omega_{m,n,k})\log(n^{2}m)+\log(n+1)

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leq\log(n^{2}m)(n\Lambda_{m}+1)+\log(n+1)

(4.16)

Indeed, (
4.12
) and (
4.14
) follow by definition, (
4.13
) uses the the counting bound with
A_{i}=I_{w}
and
A=\Omega_{m,n,k}
, (
4.15
) uses (
4.10
) and
H(\mathcal{P})\leq\log\#\mathcal{P}
, and (
4.16
) follows from (
4.11
).

Now, let
m_{k}
so that
\log(m_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}
tends to
C
defined in (
4.9
).
Pick
n_{k}
so that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log(n_{k})}{\log(m_{k})}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log(m_{k})}{n_{k}}=0.

(For example, one can choose
n_{k}=\lfloor\log(m_{k})\rfloor^{2}
). Then

\displaystyle h(f,\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{H\left(\alpha_{m_{k}}^{(n_{k})}\right)}{n_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\log(n_{k}^{2}m_{k})(\Lambda_{m_{k}}+1/n_{k})+\frac{\log(n_{k}+1)}{n_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}2\log(n_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}+\log(m_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}+2\log(n_{k})/n_{k}

\displaystyle+\log(m_{k})/n_{k}+\log(n_{k}+1)/n_{k}.

By our choice of
m_{k}
and
n_{k}
we have
\log(m_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}\to C
and all other terms tend to 0:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\log(n_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log(n_{k})}{\log(m_{k})}\log(m_{k})\Lambda_{m_{k}}=\left(\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log(n_{k})}{\log(m_{k})}\right)C=0,\quad

and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\log(m_{k})}{n_{k}}=0
. Hence
h(f,\alpha)\leq C
.
∎

Exercise 4.4.8

Find an example of an infinite interval exchange transformation such that the estimate of Theorem
4.4
is positive but the entropy is null.

Example 4.4.9

We apply the estimates given in Theorem
4.4
to baker’s surfaces. Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be fixed and
I\subset B_{\alpha}
be a horizontal segment of length
\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}
with no singularities in its interior. Consider
f:I\to I
the infinite IET defined by the first return map to
I
of the (vertical) translation flow on
B_{\alpha}
. The lengths of the corresponding subintervals are
\{\alpha^{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
, thus
\Lambda_{m}=\frac{\alpha^{m+1}}{1-\alpha}
. Therefore

h(f)\leq\liminf\frac{\alpha^{m+1}}{1-\alpha}\log(m)=0.

Exercise 4.4.10

Show that the infinite interval exchange transformation
f_{\alpha,\theta}
arising from in Example
4.1.1
by the translation flow in direction
\theta
on baker’s surface
B_{\alpha}
has entropy zero.

Example 4.4.11

In Section
4.1.2
we discussed first return maps on a translation surface
M(P)
associated to an infinite step billiard table
P
. Recall from Example
2.4
that
P=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[n-1,n]\times[0,h_{n}]
where
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a monotonically decreasing sequence of positive numbers. Hence, if
\sigma_{x}
and
\sigma_{y}
are the reflections of the plane w.r.t. the horizontal and vertical lines passing through the origin respectively, then
M(P)
is tiled by
\{P,\sigma_{x}P,\sigma_{y}\sigma_{x}P,\sigma_{y}P\}
. Moreover, if the sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is summable then
M(P)
has finite area and
\gamma:=(\cup_{n\geq 1}\{n\}\times[-h_{n+1},h_{n+1}])\sqcup(\cup_{n\geq 1}\{-n\}\times[-h_{n+1},h_{n+1}])
is a finite length disconnected smooth curve which is transversal to the translation flow on
M(P)
in any direction different from the vertical direction. By the formulae following equation (
4.2
), if
\theta\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, the first return map
f_{\gamma,\theta}:\gamma\to\gamma
of the translation flow
F_{\theta}^{t}
to
\gamma
defines a generalized infinite IET. We use the following general result and Theorem
4.4
to show that many of these infinite IETs have zero entropy.

Lemma 4.4.12

Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq\ldots
be summable positive numbers and

\Lambda_{m}=\lambda_{m+1}+\lambda_{m+2}+\ldots
.
Let
\{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3},\ldots\}
be a partition of
\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
and
K>0
a constant such that

|J_{i}|\leq K
for all
i
.
Denote

\lambda^{\prime}_{i}:=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}\lambda_{j}
and assume that
\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\geq\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\geq\ldots
.
Denote also
\Lambda^{\prime}_{m}:=\lambda^{\prime}_{m+1}+\lambda^{\prime}_{m+2}+\ldots

and
d_{i}:=\min\{j\in\mathbb{N}:K\lambda^{\prime}_{j}<\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\}
. Then

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\log(m)\Lambda_{m}\leq\liminf_{i\to\infty}\log(d_{i})(K\ i\ \lambda^{\prime}_{i}+\Lambda^{\prime}_{i}).

(4.17)

Proof.

For each
i
let us define
m_{i}
as the minimum number
m
such that
for all
j\leq i
,
J_{j}\cap\{1,\ldots,m\}\not=\emptyset
.

We claim that we have the two following inequalities

1.

m_{i}\leq Kd_{i}
,

2.

\Lambda_{m_{i}}\leq K\ i\ \lambda^{\prime}_{i}+\Lambda^{\prime}_{i}
.

The conclusion of the lemma follows directly from the above two inequalities.

Let us prove the first inequality.
Because each
J_{i}
contains at most
K
elements,
m_{i}
is bounded by the
number of elements
j
such that
\lambda_{j}\geq\lambda^{\prime}_{i}/K
.
Now, among the
Kd_{i}
first numbers
j
there exists
necessarily one that belongs to a partition
J_{n}
with
n\geq d_{i}
.
Hence
\lambda_{Kd_{i}}\leq\lambda^{\prime}_{d_{i}}<\lambda^{\prime}_{i}/K
(where the last
inequality holds by definition of
d_{i}
). This shows the first inequality.

We now prove the second inequality.
We consider the intervals

\lambda_{m}
with
m>m_{i}
and decompose them into two subsets:
the one for which
m
belongs to
J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup\ldots\cup J_{i}
and
the rest. On the first hand, each subset
J_{j}
contains at most
K
elements.
Hence the sum of the elements of the first family is bounded by

K\cdot i\cdot\lambda_{i}
. On the other hand, the other sum is bounded
by
\Lambda^{\prime}_{i}
. This terminates the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the case of the step billiard we can take
\{\lambda_{i}\}
as the sequence
\{\lambda_{A_{i}},\lambda_{D_{i}}\}\cup\{\lambda_{B_{i}},\lambda_{C_{i}}\}
defined by the formulae following equation (
4.2
) and
J_{i}
is just the partition induced by considering all elements in a pair of transversal segments in
\gamma
of the form
\{\pm n\}\cup[-h_{n},h_{n}]
. Hence we have that
K=8
. Moreover, if the size of the steps
h_{n}
defining the infinite step billiard table
P
decays exponentially fast then
d_{i}=i+1
. From
(\ref{EQ:EstimateEntropy})
we obtain that
\liminf_{m\to\infty}\log(m)\Lambda_{m}=0
and from Theorem
4.4
that the first return map
f_{\gamma_{\theta}}
has entropy zero. Remark that this fact depends only on the exponential decay of the sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and not on the direction
\theta
.

Abramov’s formula

Let
M
be a finite area translation surface and suppose that for a fixed direction
\theta
the translation flow
F_{\theta}^{t}
is defined almost everywhere on
M
. The entropy of this flow is by definition (see
[
CFS82b
]
) the (metric) entropy of the time-1 map
F_{\theta}^{1}
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure on
M
. For example, if
M
is obtained by suspending the IET from Example
4.4
, then the entropy of the (vertical) translation flow on
M
is
log(2)
. On the other hand, the entropy of the translation flow on baker’s surface is equal to zero.

Abramov’s formula (see
[
Abr66a
]
and
[
Abr66b
]
) which we describe below, relates the entropy of the translation flow to the entropy of the first return map to an interval (globally) transverse to it. More precisely, let
\gamma:I\to M
be a smooth curve transverse to
F_{\theta}^{t}
. For simplicity let
\gamma
also denote the image of this map and
dx
the push-forward of Lebesgue measure of the interval
I
to
\gamma
. We suppose that
\gamma
intersects every orbit of
F_{\theta}^{t}
. Since the area of
M
is finite the first return time
r:\gamma\to(0,+\infty]
of
F_{\theta}^{t}
to
\gamma
is defined almost everywhere on
\gamma
as well as the first-return map
f:\gamma\to\gamma
. Abramov’s formula states that:

h(F_{\theta}^{1},Leb_{M})=\frac{h(f,dx)}{\int_{\gamma}rdx}

(4.18)

Since
M
is of finite area and the transversal curve
\gamma
is global we have that
\int_{\gamma}rdx=\operatorname{Area}(M)
.

Appendix A
The hyperbolic plane and Fuchsian groups

Let us recall from Section
1.1.5
that translation surfaces admit a
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
-action. The quotient
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})/\{\pm 1\}
identifies to the isometry group of the hyperbolic plane. This relation with geometry provides a very efficient tool to study
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and its discrete subgroups. In this appendix we recall classical definitions and results about hyperbolic geometry, discrete subgroups of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and Rosen continued fractions. For most of the proofs we refer to the literature such as
[
Kat92
]
or
[
Bea83
]
.

A.1
Fuchsian groups

Let
\hat{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
denote the Riemann sphere. It admits an action of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
by
homographies
also called
Möbius transformations

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot z=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}.

(A.1)

It is a standard result that the Möbius transformations are the only analytic automorphisms of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
, see for example Theorem 1.8.2 in
[
Hub06
]
. The Möbius transformations stabilizing respectively the affine plane
\mathbb{C}
and the upper half space
\mathbb{H}^{2}=\{z=x+iy\in\mathbb{C}:y>0\}
are respectively the subgroups
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathbb{C})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}
and
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. These two groups are all analytic automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{H}^{2}
respectively, see also Theorem 1.8.2 in
[
Hub06
]
. The Poincaré-Koebe’s uniformization theorem states that these are the only three simply connected Riemann surfaces up to analytic automorphisms.

In this section we focus on the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. It is somehow the richest of the three simply connected Riemann surfaces as it is the universal cover of any Riemann surface which is not homeomorphic to a sphere, a torus, a plane or a cylinder. It can be endowed with the
Poincaré metric
or
hyperbolic metric
:

ds^{2}=\frac{dx^{2}+dy^{2}}{y^{2}}.

The geodesics in the metric space
(\mathbb{H}^{2},ds)
are the half circles and lines perpendicular to the boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}:=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, see Theorem 1.2.1 in
[
Kat92
]
. This metric space is uniquely geodesic: by every pair of points of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
passes a unique geodesic. This property also extends to
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
and a geodesic is entirely determined by its two endpoints in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The group
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
acts by isometries with respect to this metric, see Theorem 1.1.2 in
[
Kat92
]
.

Elements of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
are classified with respect to their trace as follows. We say that a matrix

A\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
different from
\pm 1
is

•

elliptic
if
|\operatorname{tr}(A)|<2
.

•

parabolic
if
|\operatorname{tr}(A)|=2
.

•

hyperbolic
if
|\operatorname{tr}(A)|>2
.

As explained in Chapter 2 in
[
Kat92
]
, every elliptic element fixes exactly one point in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, every parabolic fixes exactly one point in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
and every hyperbolic fixes exactly two points
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The
axis
of a hyperbolic element is the unique geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
joining the corresponding fixed points.

A
Fuchsian group
is a discrete subgroup of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Fuchsian groups can be characterized in more geometric terms using the following notion.

Definition A.1.1

Let
X
be a locally compact topological space and
\Gamma
a discrete group acting on
X
.
The action is said to be
properly discontinuous
if for every compact
K\subset X
the set

\{g\in\Gamma:gK\cap K\not=\emptyset\}

is finite.

Theorem A.1.2
(
[
Kat92
, Theorem 2.2.6 and Corollary 2.2.7]
)

Let
\Gamma
be a subgroup of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Then the following are equivalent

•

\Gamma
is Fuchsian

•

\Gamma
acts properly discontinuously on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
,

•

for all
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
, the orbit
\Gamma\cdot z
is discrete.

Let
\Gamma
be a Fuchsian group. By the discretness of item 3 in Theorem
A.1
, for a point
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
the set of accumulation points of its orbit
\Gamma\cdot z
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
is contained in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
. The latter is independent of the point
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}\cup\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
and is called
the limit set of
\Gamma
and denoted
\Lambda(\Gamma)
.

As explained in
[
Kat92
, Chapter 3.4]
, there is the following trichotomy for the limit set of a Fuchsian group
\Gamma

1.

either
\Lambda(\Gamma)
contains at most two points,

2.

or it is homeomorphic to the Cantor set,

3.

or it is equal to
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

In the first case we say that
\Gamma
is
elementary
. An elementary Fuchsian group either stabilizes a point in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
(in which case
\Gamma
consists of elliptic elements) or a single point in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
(in which case
\Gamma
consists of parabolic elements) or a pair of points in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
(in which case
\Gamma
consists of hyperbolic elements and possibly an elliptic involution). Fuchsian groups whose limit set is nowhere dense in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,
i
.
e
., cases (1) and (2), are said to be of the
second kind
and those satifying (3) are of the
first kind
.

A Fuchsian group for which the quotient
\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
has finite area is called a
lattice
and, if in addition this su is compact the lattice is
uniform
. A Fuchsian group is a lattice if and only if it is finitely generated and of the first kind. The following Theorem gives several characterizations for elementary Fuchsian groups.

Theorem A.1.3

Let
\Gamma
be a Fuchsian group. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\Lambda(\Gamma)
is infinite,

2.

\Gamma
contains a free group on two generators,

3.

there is a pair of hyperbolic elements in
\Gamma
whose sets of fixed points in
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
are disjoint,

4.

there is a pair of hyperbolic elements in
\Gamma
whose axes are disjoint.

A proof of this Theorem can be deduced, for example, from the discussion on non-elementary Fuchsian groups presented in
[
Bea83
]
.

The following result is used in Sections
3.3.4
and
3.3.5
to show that Fuchsian groups of the first kind appear as Veech groups of wind-tree models.

Lemma A.1.4

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary Fuchsian group and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a non-trivial normal subgroup of
\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is non-elementary and the limit sets of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
coincide.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is non-elementary. Since it is non-trivial it
contains an element
h
which is not the identity. Then, for an appropriate
g\in\Gamma

the group
\langle h,ghg^{-1}\rangle
is non-elementary. In particular
\Gamma^{\prime}

is non-elementary.

For the rest of the proof we use the following two facts, which can be found in
[
Kat92
]
: every non-elementary Fuchsian group contains infinitely many hyperbolic elements, no two of which have a common fixed point, and every limit set
\Lambda(\Gamma)

containing more than one element is the closure of the set of fixed points of the hyperbolic elements in
\Gamma
.
Let then
g\in\Gamma
be hyperbolic. It is sufficient to show that the attractive fixed
point of
g
is accumulated by fixed points of elements in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let us consider any hyperbolic element
h\in\Gamma^{\prime}
. If its attractive fixed point
x

is fixed by
g
, then there is nothing to do. Otherwise, the attractive fixed
point of the hyperbolic element
g^{n}hg^{-n}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
is
g^{n}x
which converges to the attractive fixed point of
g
.
∎

We conclude this section with a particular version of the ping-pong lemma (
a.k.a.
the table-tennis lemma), adapted to the context of hyperbolic elements acting on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We refer the reader to II.B in
[
de 00
]
for more details about the proof.

Theorem A.1.5
(Ping-pong lemma)

Let
G
be a group acting on a topological space
X
.
Let
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
, …,
g_{k}
be elements of
G
and
assume that there exist disjoint non-empty open subsets

X^{+}_{1},X^{-}_{1},X^{+}_{2},X^{-}_{2},\ldots,X^{+}_{k},X^{-}_{k}\subset X
such that for all

i
in
\{1,\ldots,k\}
we have

h_{i}(X\setminus X_{i}^{-})\subset X^{+}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{i}^{-1}(X\setminus X_{i}^{+})\subset X^{-}_{i}

Then

•

all the
g_{i}
have infinite order

•

the subgroup of
G
generated by the
g_{i}
is canonically isomorphic to the free product
\langle g_{1}\rangle*\langle g_{2}\rangle*\cdots*\langle g_{k}\rangle
.

•

the action of
\langle g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{k}\rangle
on
X
is free and properly discontinuous.

As a consequence of the preceding discussion and the ping-pong lemma we have the following:

Theorem A.1.6

Let
g,h
be two hyperbolic elements in
\operatorname{Isom}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
such that
\operatorname{Fix}(g)\cap\operatorname{Fix}(h)=\emptyset
. Then, for large enough integer
m
the group generated by
g^{m}
and
h
is free and all its elements different from the identity are hyperbolic.

A.2
The group
G_{\lambda}

In this section we investigate a family of Fuchsian group that we denote
G_{\lambda}
where
\lambda>2
is a parameter. These groups play a central role in finite and infinite Thurston-Veech constructions, for example in the proofs of Section
3.5.1
.

Our presentation mainly follows the original article of D. Rosen
[
Ros54
]
.
The latter is a continuation of earlier work of Hecke
[
Hec36
]
.

For
\lambda>2
let

h_{\lambda}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{\lambda}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\lambda&1\end{pmatrix}.

We define
G_{\lambda}:=\left\langle h_{\lambda},v_{\lambda}\right\rangle
. The main two results about the groups
G_{\lambda}
are Theorem
A.2
and Theorem
A.2
that we state now.

Theorem A.2.1

Let
\lambda>2
and
G_{\lambda}
be as above.
Then
G_{\lambda}
is a free group generated by
h_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda}
which is discrete and of infinite covolume in
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The quotient
SO(2)\backslash\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})/G_{\lambda}
is a hyperbolic pair of pants with boundary lengths
0
,
0
and
2\operatorname{acosh}\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}-2}{2}\right)
. In other words, the quotient is a sphere with two cusps and a funnel.

Theorem
A.2
is proven in Section
A.2.1
below. See also Figure A.1 for a picture of the fundamental domain of the action of
G_{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Theorem A.2.2

Let
\lambda>2
and
G_{\lambda}
as above. Let
I=(r,-r+\lambda)

where
r:=\frac{\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}
.
Then the intervals
\{g\cdot I\}_{g\in G_{\lambda}}
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\mathbb{R}
are disjoint and
the limit set
\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
of
G_{\lambda}
is a Cantor set whose complement
is the union
\bigcup_{g\in G_{\lambda}}g\cdot I
.

For
\lambda=2
, we have
\Lambda(G_{2})=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem
A.2
is proven in Section
A.2.2
. See also Figure A.2 for a picture illustrating the gap
(r,-r+\lambda)
in the limit set of
G_{\lambda}
.

Remark A.2.3

In the limit situation
\lambda=2
,
G_{2}<\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
is still a free group generated
by
h_{2}
and
v_{2}
which is discrete. However it has finite index in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})

and hence finite covolume. The quotient is a hyperbolic pair of pants with three cusps
(or rather ”the” hyperbolic pair of pants with three cusps since it is unique up
to biholomorphism).

It was proven by Hecke that for
\lambda<2
the group
G_{\lambda}
is discrete if and only if

\lambda=2\cos(\pi/n)
for some integer
n\geq 3
. The group
G_{2\cos(\pi/n)}

has finite covolume in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and has for unique relation

\left(h_{2\cos(\pi/n)}(v_{2\cos(pi/n)})^{-1}\right)^{n}=(-1)^{n}.

The quotient is a pair of pants with two cusps and an orbifold point with angle
2\pi/n
.

A.2.1
A fundamental domain for
G_{\lambda}

In this section we prove Theorem
A.2
by finding an explicit fundamental domain for the action of
G_{\lambda}
. A useful ingredient in the proof is the ping-pong lemma (Theorem
A.1
).

Figure A.1
:
A fundamental domain for
G_{\lambda}
(in gray with bold boundaries)
in the upper half plane model. To form the quotient
surface, the vertical blue geodesics and red geodesics on the boundary of the
fundamental domain are identified with respectively
h_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda}
.
According to Theorem
A.2
this quotient is a pair of pants
(a three punctured sphere).
The thinner lines are translates by
G_{\lambda}
of the boundary of the fundamental domain.

Theorem A.2.4

Let
\lambda>2
. Then
G_{\lambda}
is a free group generated by
h_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda}
.
Let us define

\displaystyle X_{1}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{Re}(z)>\lambda/2\}

\displaystyle X_{1}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{Re}(z)<-\lambda/2\}

\displaystyle X_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{|z-1/\lambda|<1/\lambda\}

\displaystyle X_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{|z+1/\lambda|<1/\lambda\}

Then
D_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{H}^{2}\setminus(X_{1}^{+}\cup X_{1}^{-}\cup X_{2}^{+}\cup X_{2}^{-})
is a fundamental region for the action of
G_{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that
h_{\lambda}\cdot z=z+\lambda
and
v_{\lambda}\cdot z=\frac{1}{\lambda+\frac{1}{z}}
.
These are two parabolic elements that stabilize respectively
\infty
and
0
.

The subsets
X_{1}^{+},X_{1}^{-},X_{2}^{+},X_{2}^{-}
do not exactly satisfy the assumption of Theorem
A.1
for
g_{1}=h_{\lambda}
and
g_{2}=v_{\lambda}
but almost. The only problem comes from the geodesic boundaries
\{\operatorname{Re}(z)=\lambda/2\}
,
\{\operatorname{Re}(z)=-\lambda/2\}
,
\{|z-1/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}
and
\{|z+1\/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}
. More precisely

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}\cdot\{\operatorname{Re}(z)=-\lambda/2\}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{Re}(z)=\lambda/2\}

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}\cdot\{|z+1/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}

\displaystyle=\{|z-1/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}.

Indeed,
h_{\lambda}\cdot\frac{-\lambda}{2}=\frac{\lambda}{2}
and
v_{\lambda}\cdot\frac{-2}{\lambda}=\frac{2}{\lambda}
. For that purpose, let us extend a bit the domains

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{Re}(z)>\lambda/2-\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{Re}(z)<-\lambda/2+\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle Y_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle=\{|z-(1/\lambda+\varepsilon)|<1/\lambda+\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle Y_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{|z+(1/\lambda+\varepsilon)|<1/\lambda+\varepsilon\}

where
\varepsilon
is a positive real such that
2\varepsilon<|\lambda/2-2/\lambda|
.
The sets
Y_{1}^{+}
,
Y_{1}^{-}
,
Y_{2}^{+}
and
Y_{2}^{-}
do satisfy the assumption of Theorem
A.1
and this shows that
G_{\lambda}
is a free group generated by
h_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda}
.

We now show that
D_{\lambda}
is a fundamental domain for
G_{\lambda}
.

Let us first show that no two elements of the interior
\mathring{D}_{\lambda}
of
D_{\lambda}
are congruent. This follows
from the ping-pong property : for any
g\in G_{\lambda}\setminus\{1\}
, we have
g\cdot\mathring{D}_{\lambda}\subset(X_{1}^{+}\cup X_{1}^{-}\cup X_{2}^{+}\cup X_{2}^{-})
.

Now, let
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We construct an element
g\in G_{\lambda}
such that
gz\in D_{\lambda}
.
For that purpose, we consider the function
f(z)=\operatorname{d}(z,D_{\lambda})
(where the distance is with respect to the hyperbolic metric).
Our first aim is to show that for all
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
there exists
g\in G_{\lambda}
such that
f(g\cdot z)=0
.
Let us first notice that the band
\mathbb{H}^{2}\setminus(X_{1}\cup X_{1}^{-})
is a fundamental domain for
\langle h_{\lambda}\rangle
.
In other words, if
z\in X_{1}^{+}\cup X_{1}^{-}
there exists
n\not=0
such that
h^{n}_{\lambda}\cdot z\not\in(X_{1}^{+}\cup X_{1}^{-})
.
For this particular
n
we have
f(h^{n}_{\lambda}\cdot z)<f(z)
.
Similarly, if
z\in X_{2}^{+}\cup X_{2}^{-}
, we can play the same game with
v_{\lambda}
.
This shows that whenever
z\not\in D_{\lambda}
one can find an element in
\langle h_{\lambda}\rangle\cup\langle v_{\lambda}\rangle

that makes the distance to
D_{\lambda}
decrease. Since
G_{\lambda}
acts freely and properly discontinuously this process
must stop after finitely many steps. But when this stops, the point must be in
G_{\lambda}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

By Theorem
A.2.1
, the quotient
G_{\lambda}\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
is biholomorphic
to the domain
D_{\lambda}
quotiented by the two identifications of the boundaries
\{\operatorname{Re}(z)=-\lambda/2\}

and
\{Re(z)=\lambda/2\}
by
h_{\lambda}
and the boundaries
\{|z+1/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}
and

\{|z-1/\lambda|=1/\lambda\}
by
v_{\lambda}
.

The quotient is indeed a pair of pants with two cusps (corresponding to the points at infinity
0
and
\infty
) and a funnel (corresponding to the
boundary segments
[-\lambda/2,-2/\lambda]
and
[2/\lambda,\lambda/2]
. The boundary length of the funnel is the hyperbolic length of the simple geodesic
turning once around the funnel which corresponds to the conjugacy class of
g=h_{\lambda}(v_{\lambda})^{-1}
. The hyperbolic
length
\ell
of this curve is equal to half the leading eigenvalue. This can be deduced from the trace of the matrix
g
via the relation

\operatorname{tr}(g)/2=\cosh(\ell/2)
. Indeed if
z
is on the axis of a hyperbolic element
g\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})

then the distance between
z
and
g\cdot z
is
\ell
such that
\operatorname{tr}(g)/2=\cosh(\ell/2)
.
∎

A.2.2
Rosen continued fraction

We now describe the Rosen continued fraction which is the main ingredient
in our proof of Theorem
A.2
. The Rosen map
is a map defined on an interval which corresponds to the action of certain elements
of
G_{\lambda}
on the line at infinity
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
in the upper half plane. Passing
from the Rosen continued
fraction to
G_{\lambda}
will be done in the next section. The construction depends on

\lambda>2
but we omit this dependency in our notations.

Figure A.2
:
A zoom in the region
(r,-r+\lambda)
that does not contain
any limit point of
G_{\lambda}
, see Theorem
A.2
.
The geodesic from
r
to
-r+\lambda
(in green) is a periodic geodesic on the
quotient which corresponds to the simple curve around the funnel.

Let
T:\left[-\frac{2}{\lambda},\frac{2}{\lambda}\right]\to\left[-\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{\lambda}{2}\right]
be the map defined
by

T(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\varepsilon(x)\cdot x}-\lambda\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon(x)\cdot x}+\frac{1}{2}\right\rfloor&\text{if $x\not=0$}\\
0&\text{if $x=0$}.\end{array}\right.

where
\varepsilon(x)
is the sign of
x
.
For
x\not=0
, we also note
\displaystyle a(x)=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{\lambda\varepsilon(x)x}+\frac{1}{2}\right\rfloor\in\{1,2,\ldots,+\infty\}
.

Note that
T
has a codomain larger than its domain as
2/\lambda<1<\lambda/2
. We aim to study orbits under the map
T
that is the list
(x,T(x),T^{2}(x),\ldots)
. Because the codomain is smaller than the domain, some orbits are finite and can not be extended further.
Let
\Lambda(T)\subset\left[-\frac{2}{\lambda};\frac{2}{\lambda}\right]
be the subsset of
points with infinite orbit, that is

\Lambda(T):=\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\Lambda_{n}(T)\quad\text{where}\quad\Lambda_{n}(T):=T^{-n}\left(\left[-\frac{2}{\lambda},\frac{2}{\lambda}\right]\right).

Below, we give a description of
\Lambda(T)
and explore the link with the limit set of
G_{\lambda}
.

For
n\geq 1
and
x\in\Lambda_{n-1}(T)
, we define
a_{n}(x)=a(T^{n-1}x)
and
\varepsilon_{n}(x)=\varepsilon(T^{n-1}x)
.
The
Rosen continued fraction expansion
of
x\in\Lambda(T)
is the infinite sequence of pairs
(\varepsilon_{n}(x),a_{n}(x))_{n\geq 1}
.

Figure A.3
:
Graph of the Rosen map for
\lambda=2.27
. The map is not defined
on
[\frac{-\lambda}{2},\frac{-2}{\lambda}]\cup[\frac{2}{\lambda},\frac{\lambda}{2}]
(yellow region).

Recall that (invertible)
2\times 2
real matrices act by homography on the
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and its
boundary
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
via

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot z=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}.

Matrix multiplication corresponds to function composition. The only matrices that
act trivially are
\pm Id
and this action of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}

corresponds to the action of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
the boundary of the Poincaré half-plane. With
that notation in mind, we have for
x\in[-2/\lambda;2/\lambda]\setminus\{0\}
the expression

T(x)=\begin{pmatrix}-\lambda r(x)&\varepsilon\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\cdot x=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon(x)\\
1&\lambda a(x)\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\cdot x.

In the above formula, there is a slight abuse since the determinant of the matrix is
-\varepsilon(x)
and hence not an element of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
if
x>0
. In order to make sense
we consider the action of the larger group
\operatorname{SL}^{\pm 1}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

We see that the inverse branches of
T
are parametrized by the pairs
(\varepsilon_{1},a_{1})\in\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Given such a pair, we define the corresponding inverse branch
H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}:\left[-\frac{\lambda}{2};\frac{\lambda}{2}\right)\to\left[-\frac{\lambda}{2};\frac{\lambda}{2}\right)
by

H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
1&\lambda a_{1}\end{pmatrix}\cdot x=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\lambda a_{1}+x}.

(A.2)

Then a direct computation gives the following lemma.

Lemma A.2.5

For any pair
(\varepsilon_{1},a_{1})\in\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the map
H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}
is a bijection on its image

I_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}:=H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}\left(\left(-\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{\lambda}{2}\right]\right)=\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\left(\frac{2}{(2a_{1}+1)\lambda};\frac{2}{(2a_{1}-1)\lambda}\right]

Furthermore, for
x\in\Lambda(T)
we have
(\varepsilon_{1}(x),a_{1}(x))=(\varepsilon_{1},a_{1})
if and only if
x\in I_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}
.

By iterating the inverse branches, we obtain a complete characterization of
\Lambda(T)
.
Before stating the main result we introduce further convenient notation.
We denote
\mathcal{S}
the set of finite sequences of elements in
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
For
s=((\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(\varepsilon_{n},a_{n}))\in\mathcal{S}
we denote

\displaystyle H_{s}(x)

\displaystyle:=H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}\circ H_{\varepsilon_{2}:a_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ H_{\varepsilon_{n}:a_{n}}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
1&\lambda a_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{2}\\
1&\lambda a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{n}\\
1&\lambda a_{n}\end{pmatrix}\cdot x

\displaystyle=\cfrac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\lambda a_{1}+\cfrac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\ldots+\cfrac{\varepsilon_{n}}{\lambda a_{n}+x}}}.

We also denote
I_{s}:=H_{s}([-\lambda/2,\lambda/2))
the image of
H_{s}
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
the set of finite and infinite sequences of elements in
\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Lemma A.2.6

The continued fraction expansion

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Lambda(T)&\to&\overline{\mathcal{S}}\\
x&\mapsto&((\varepsilon_{n}(x),a_{n}(x)))_{n\geq 1}\end{array}

is a bijection.

In order to prove the above lemma, we compute the diameters
of the intervals
I_{s}=H_{s}([-\lambda/2,\lambda/2))
. Given

s=((\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(\varepsilon_{n},a_{n}))\in\mathcal{S}
we
define

\begin{pmatrix}p_{n-1}(s)&p_{n}(s)\\
q_{n-1}(s)&q_{n}(s)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
1&\lambda a_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{2}\\
1&\lambda a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdots\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{n}\\
1&\lambda a_{n}\end{pmatrix}

or equivalently

H_{s}(x)=\frac{p_{n-1}(s)x+p_{n}(s)}{q_{n-1}(s)x+q_{n}(s)}.

The numbers
p_{n}(s)
and
q_{n}(s)
can be computed inductively by the following formulas

\begin{array}[]{l@{;\ }l@{;\ }l}p_{-1}(s)=1&p_{0}(s)=0&p_{n}(s)=\lambda a_{n}p_{n-1}(s)+\varepsilon_{n}p_{n-2}(s)\\
q_{-1}(s)=0&q_{0}(s)=1&q_{n}(s)=\lambda a_{n}q_{n-1}(s)+\varepsilon_{n}q_{n-2}(s)\end{array}.

Lemma A.2.7

Let
s=((\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(\varepsilon_{n},a_{n}))\in\mathcal{S}
.
For all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have

|q_{k}(s)|>r\cdot|q_{k-1}(s)|

(A.3)

where
r=\frac{\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}

and

\operatorname{sign}(q_{k}(s))=\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}a_{k-1}\cdots a_{1}).

(A.4)

If furthermore,
\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}a_{k-1})=\varepsilon_{k}
or
|a_{k}|\geq 2
, then

|q_{k}(s)|>\lambda\cdot|q_{k-1}(s)|.

Corollary A.2.8

Let
((\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(\varepsilon_{n},a_{n}))\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
m
be the number of indices

k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that either
\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}a_{k-1})=\varepsilon_{k}
or
|a_{k}|\geq 2
. Then we have

q_{n}(s)>\lambda^{m}\cdot r^{n-m}

where
r=\frac{\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}
.

Proof.

We prove the first inequality by induction on
k
.
Since
q_{0}=1
,
q_{1}=\lambda a_{1}
and
\lambda>r
, the
inequality holds for
k=1
. Assume that it holds for
k-1
.
Then

\displaystyle q_{k}

\displaystyle=\lambda a_{k}q_{k-1}+q_{k-2}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda|q_{k-1}|-|q_{k-2}|

\displaystyle>|q_{k-1}|(\lambda-1/r)

where in the last step we used the induction hypothesis.
Since
\lambda-1/r=r
this finishes the proof of the first inequality.

We now prove the formula for
\operatorname{sign}(q_{k})
. By the previous inequality, we
got that
|\lambda a_{k}q_{k-1}|>|q_{k-2}|
. Hence

\operatorname{sign}(q_{k})=\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}q_{k-1})
from which

\operatorname{sign}(q_{k})=\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}a_{k-1}\cdots a_{1})
follows.

Next, if
\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}a_{k-1})=\varepsilon_{k}
then by (
A.4
) we have

\operatorname{sign}(a_{k}q_{k-1})=\operatorname{sign}(\varepsilon_{k}q_{k-2})
and hence

|q_{k}|=\lambda|a_{k}||q_{k-1}|+|q_{k-2}|
.
In particular, it follows that

|q_{k}|\geq\lambda|q_{k-1}|+|q_{k-2}|\geq(\lambda+1/r)|q_{k-1}|

where we applied the inequality (
A.3
) to
q_{k-2}
.
Finally, if
|a_{k}|\geq 2
then

|q_{k}|\geq 2\lambda|q_{k-1}|-|q_{k-2}|\geq(2\lambda-1/r)|q_{k-1}|.

Since
2\lambda-1/r>\lambda
we also deduce that
|q_{k}|\geq\lambda|q_{k-1}|

in that case.
∎

Lemma A.2.9

Let
s=((\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(\varepsilon_{n},a_{n}))\in\mathcal{S}
and
y\in[-\lambda/2;\lambda/2)
.
Set
x=H_{s}(y)
. Then

x-\frac{p_{n}(s)}{q_{n}(s)}=\frac{(-1)^{n}\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\cdot y}{q_{n}(s)(q_{n-1}(s)y+q_{n})}.

(A.5)

In particular

\left|x-\frac{p_{n}(s)}{q_{n}(s)}\right|\leq\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}\cdot\frac{1}{|q_{n}\cdot q_{n-1}|}.

(A.6)

Proof.

Let us use
p_{n},p_{n-1},q_{n},q_{n-1}
for
p_{n}(s),p_{n-1}(s),q_{n}(s),q_{n-1}(s)
respectively.
From

x=\begin{pmatrix}p_{n-1}&p_{n}\\
q_{n-1}&q_{n}\end{pmatrix}\cdot y

we can write

x-\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}=\frac{p_{n-1}y+p_{n}}{q_{n-1}y+q_{n}}-\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}=\frac{(q_{n}p_{n-1}-p_{n}q_{n-1})y}{q_{n}(q_{n-1}y+q_{n})}.

Now,
q_{n}p_{n-1}-p_{n}q_{n-1}
is the determinant of the matrix representation
of the homography
H_{s}
which is a product of
n
matrices with determinant respectively

-\varepsilon_{1}
,
-\varepsilon_{2}
, …,
-\varepsilon_{n}
. Hence

q_{n}p_{n-1}-p_{n}q_{n-1}=(-1)^{n}\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}

from which we deduce the equality.

Let us now derive the inequality. By (
A.3
) from Lemma
A.2.2
, we obtain that

|q_{n-1}y+q_{n}|>(r-\lambda/2)|q_{n-1}|=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}|q_{n-1}|
.
The inequality (
A.6
) follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.2.2
.

For
x\in\Lambda\setminus\{0\}T
, we have
x=H_{\varepsilon_{1}(x):a_{1}(x)}(T(x))
. Hence

\Lambda(T)=\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{(\varepsilon_{1},a_{1})\in\{\pm 1\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}H_{\varepsilon_{1}:a_{1}}(\Lambda(T)).

By iterating this equality we see that, for
x\in\Lambda(T)
, either its orbit reaches zero after
finitely many steps and we have
x=H_{\varepsilon_{1}(x):a_{1}(x)}\circ\cdots\circ H_{\varepsilon_{n}(x):a_{n}(x)}(0)

which corresponds to a finite continued fraction expansion. Otherwise, the orbit of
x
never reaches

0
and we obtain an infinite continued fraction expansion
(\varepsilon_{n}(x),a_{n}(x))_{n}
. From lemma
A.2.2
,
the diameter of
I_{\varepsilon_{1}(x):a_{1}(x),\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}(x):a_{n}(x)}
tends to zero as
n\to\infty
.
Hence

\{x\}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}I_{\varepsilon_{1}(x):a_{1}(x),\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}(x):a_{n}(x)}.

In other words
x\mapsto((\varepsilon_{n}(x),a_{n}(x)))_{n\geq 1}
is injective.
∎

Corollary A.2.10

Let
I=[-\lambda/2,-2/\lambda)\cup(2/\lambda,\lambda/2]
.
Then the sets
\{h_{s}(I)\}_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
are disjoint and their union is
the complement of
\Lambda(T)
in
[-\lambda/2,\lambda/2]
.

In particular

\min\Lambda(T)=-r
and
\max\Lambda(T)=r
where

r:=\frac{\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4}}{2}
.

A.2.3
The limit set of
G_{\lambda}

We now prove Theorem
A.2
. To do so, we show that the limit set
\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
and the set
\Lambda(T)
of the Rosen map are intimately related. The Rosen continued fraction expansion of points in
\Lambda(T)
allows to determine the nature of points in the limit set.

Theorem A.2.11

Let
\lambda>2
and
T_{\lambda}:\left[-\frac{2}{\lambda},\frac{2}{\lambda}\right]\to\left[-\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{\lambda}{2}\right]
be the associated Rosen map as in Section
A.2.2
.
We have
\Lambda(T_{\lambda})=\Lambda(G_{\lambda})\cap[-\lambda/2;\lambda/2]
.
Furthermore, a point
x\in\Lambda(T_{\lambda})
is

•

stabilized by a parabolic element in
G_{\lambda}
if and only if its continued fraction expansion is finite,

•

stabilized by an hyperbolic element in
G_{\lambda}
if and only if its continued fraction expansion is periodic.

In order to tighten the link between the group
G_{\lambda}
and Rosen continued fraction we introduce a slightly
larger group
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
in
\operatorname{PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
where
\operatorname{PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
denotes
2\times 2
matrices with
determinant
\pm 1
. The group
\operatorname{PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
is identified with isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
that do not necessarily
preserves the orientation as follows. If
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{PSL}^{\pm}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
determinant
-1
we set

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot z:=\frac{-a\overline{z}+b}{-c\overline{z}+d}.

The group
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
is defined as the group generated by the three reflections

s=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad t=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad u=\begin{pmatrix}-1&\lambda\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Figure A.4
:
The fundamental domain of
G_{\lambda}
together with the reflection axis of
s
and
t
. A fundamental domain for

\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
is obtained by considering a quarter of the one of
G_{\lambda}
.

Lemma A.2.12

The group
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
contains
G_{\lambda}
and more precisely
h_{\lambda}=ut
and
v_{\lambda}=sh_{\lambda}s
. It can be defined by generators and relations as
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}=\langle s,t,u|s^{2},t^{2},u^{2},(st)^{2}\rangle
1
1

1

Such presentation is called a
(2,\infty,\infty)
-triangle group.
. Moreover
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
is a semi-direct product of
G_{\lambda}
with the Klein four group
K:=\langle s,t\rangle
and we have

s\,h_{\lambda}\,s=v_{\lambda}
and
t\,h_{\lambda}\,t=(h_{\lambda})^{-1}

The fundamental domain of
D_{\lambda}
of
G_{\lambda}
from Theorem
A.2.1
is preserved by the group
\langle s,t\rangle
.

Proof.

The relations
h_{\lambda}=u
and
v_{\lambda}=sh_{\lambda}s
and
(h_{\lambda})^{-1}=th_{\lambda}t
are straightforwrd matrix multiplications.
One can check that the elements
s
and
t
generate a group isomorphic to the Klein 4 group
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
that correspond to
a dihedral group of isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
fixing
\sqrt{-1}
. The group
K
stabilizes the fundamental region
D_{\lambda}
from Theorem
A.2.1
and permutes the 4 sub-regions delimited by the axes of
s
and
t
as can be seen on Figure
A.4
.
∎

The last ingredient needed for the proof of Theorem
A.2.3
is the relation between
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
and Rosen continued fractions. The homographies that define the branches of the Rosen map are elements of
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
. Indeed, the matrices
H_{\varepsilon_{1},a_{1}}
defined in (
A.2
) satisfy

H_{+1:a_{1}}=s\,(h_{\lambda})^{a_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{-1:a_{1}}=s\,t\,(h_{\lambda})^{a_{1}}.

(A.7)

The following lemma, whose proof is left to the reader, shows that the group
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
decomposes as finitely many translates of continued fraction matrices.

Lemma A.2.13

For
x,y\in K
let
\Delta_{x,y}:=\{x(h_{\lambda})^{a_{1}}x_{2}(h_{\lambda})^{a_{2}}x_{3}\cdots x_{n}(h_{\lambda})^{a_{n}}y:x_{i}\in\{s,st\},a_{i}>0\}
. Then

\widehat{G_{\lambda}}=K\sqcup\bigsqcup_{x,y\in K}\Delta_{x,y}.

Proof of Theorem
A.2.3
.

Let
x\in\Lambda(G_{\lambda})\subset\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, that is there exists a sequence
g_{n}\in G_{\lambda}
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
,
g_{n}z\to x
as
n\to\infty
.

Since
K=\langle s,t\rangle
stabilizes
\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
the limit set coincide :
\Lambda(\widehat{G_{\lambda}})=\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
. This shows in particular that
\Lambda(T)\subset\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
. From Lemma
A.2.3
, one deduces that
\Lambda(G_{\lambda})=\cup_{g\in K}g\Lambda(T)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.2
.

The limit set
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
has a single cusp which is the orbit of
0
. A point
x\in\Lambda(G_{\lambda})
with a finite continued fraction expansion
s
can be written as

x=H_{s}(0)
. But
H_{s}
is an homography in
\widehat{G_{\lambda}}
, so
x
is stabilized by
H_{s}pH_{s}^{-1}
where
p(z)=z/(z+1)
is the parabolic element fixing
0
.

Conversely, if
x
is stabilized by
gpg^{-1}
then one can write
g=H_{s}
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

Next, if
x
has a periodic continued fraction expansion then
x=H_{s}(x)
for some
H_{s}
. Since all
H_{s}
are hyperbolic, we are done.

Conversely, if
gx=x
then we can write down
g=H_{s}
.
∎

Appendix B
Eigenvalues of infinite graphs. Geometric Structures

In the first section of this Appendix we review some results about the spectrum of infinite graphs, more precisely about
positive
\lambda
-harmonic functions. These considerations are crucial for the
Hooper-Thurston-Veech construction considered in
Section
3.4
. Most of the material is well known and can be found in the
survey by B. Mohar and W. Woess
[
MW89
]
. Our discussion follows Appendix C in P. Hooper
[
Hoo15
]
. In the second and last section, we briefly review the general notion of
(G,X)
-structures, developing map and holonomy.

B.1
Spectral radius and Martin boundary

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be a connected graph (or multigraph) consisting of possibly infinitely many vertices
but such that each vertex
v
has finitely many neighbors. We consider
the space of functions
\mathbb{R}^{V}
from the vertices
V
of the graph to the real numbers.
For each
p\in[1,\infty]
we denote
\ell^{p}(V)
the subspace of power
p

integrable functions. Recall that we have the norm:

\|f\|_{p}=\left(\sum_{v\in V}f(v)^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

Because
V
is discrete we have
\ell^{p}(V)\subset\ell^{p^{\prime}}(V)
for

p\leq p^{\prime}
. In particular
\ell^{1}(V)\subset\ell^{2}(V)\subset\ell^{\infty}(V)
.

We denote by
\{e_{v}\}_{v\in V}
the functions

e_{v}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&\text{if $u=v$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

This collection
\{e_{v}\}_{v\in V}
is a Hilbert basis of
\ell^{2}(V)
and for
any
f\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
we have
f=\sum_{v\in V}f(v)e_{v}
.

The
adjacency operator

A=A_{\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{G}
is the operator on
\mathbb{R}^{V}
defined by

(Af)(u)=\sum_{uv\in E}f(v).

It is important to recall that in the case that
\mathcal{G}
is a multigraph, the set
E
is a multiset, hence the same term can appear several times in the sum above.

Exercise B.1.1

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph and
A
its adjacency operator.

1.

For
p\in[1,\infty]
and
f\in\ell_{p}(V)
show that

\|Af\|_{p}\leq\left(\max_{v\in V}\deg_{\mathcal{G}}(v)\right)\cdot\|f\|_{p}.

2.

For each
v\in V
show that

\|Ae_{v}\|_{p}\geq\deg_{\mathcal{G}}(v)^{1/p}

3.

For each
v
let
e^{\prime}_{v}=\sum_{w\in N_{\mathcal{G}}(v)}e_{w}
where
N_{\mathcal{G}}(v)
are the neighbors
of
v
in
\mathcal{G}
. Show that

\|Ae^{\prime}_{v}\|_{p}\geq\deg_{\mathcal{G}}(v)^{1-1/p}\cdot\|e^{\prime}_{v}\|_{p}.

4.

Deduce that the following are equivalent

(a)

\mathcal{G}
has bounded degree,

(b)

A
preserves
\ell^{p}(V)
for some
p\in[1,\infty]
,

(c)

A
preserves
\ell^{p}(V)
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
.

The
coefficients
of
A
are the numbers

\{\langle Ae_{u},e_{v}\rangle\}_{u,v}
1
1

1

Recall that
\langle(x_{i}),(y_{i})\rangle=\sum x_{i}y_{i}
. Note that the coefficient

u,v
is nothing else than the multiplicity of edges from
u
to
v
.
The
spectral radius

\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})
of the graph is the growth rate of coefficients of the powers of

\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G}):=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\langle A^{n}e_{u},e_{v}\rangle\right)^{1/n}

(B.1)

where
u
and
v
are any two vertices of
\mathcal{G}
. When the context is clear, we abbreviate
\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})
by
\lambda_{0}
.

Exercise B.1.2

1.

Show that
\langle A^{n}e_{u},e_{v}\rangle
is the number of oriented paths from
u
to
v
of length
n
.

2.

Show that the quantity in (
B.1
) is indeed independent of the choice of
the vertices
u
and
v
(hint: use the connectedness of the graph).

Lemma B.1.3

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a connected infinite graph of bounded degree, that is
\deg(\mathcal{G}):=\max_{v\in V}\deg_{\mathcal{G}}(v)<\infty
. Then
2\sqrt{\deg(\mathcal{G})-1}\leq\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})\leq\deg(\mathcal{G})
. In particular
\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})\geq 2
,

Proof.

The upper bound can be shown in various manners, we use a classical result of Schur (see Theorem 6.12-A
[
Tay58
]
): if
(\alpha_{uv})
is an infinite matrix of real scalars such that:

\sum_{v}|\alpha_{uv}|\leq M_{1}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for all $u$}

and

\sum_{u}|\alpha_{uv}|\leq M_{2}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for all $v$}

then
(\alpha_{uv})
represents a bounded operator
A:\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}
such that
||A||\leq\sqrt{M_{1}M_{2}}
. For the lower bound let
T\to\mathcal{G}
be the universal cover and let
T_{min}\hookrightarrow T
a regular tree whose degree is
d=\min_{v\in V}\deg_{T}(v)
. Then in
T_{min}

for any
n\geq 1
and any
v\in V(T_{min})
we have:

\langle A^{2n}e_{v},e_{v}\rangle\geq C_{n}\cdot d\cdot(d-1)^{n-1}.

where
C_{n}
is the nth Catalan number. Since

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(C_{n}\cdot d\cdot(d-1)^{n-1}\right)^{1/(2n)}=2\sqrt{d-1}

we obtain the lower bound for
T_{min}
. Since we are working with a regular tree in the universal covering of
\mathcal{G}
, this lower bound also works for
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Now we distinguish more carefully the different possible asymptotic behavior. For any connected multigraph
\mathcal{G}
let:

R_{\lambda}:=\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}A\right)^{n}.

It is known that all entries of
R_{\lambda}
are real numbers if
\lambda>\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})
and every entry of
R_{\lambda}
is equal to
+\infty
if
\lambda<\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})
.

Definition B.1.4

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a connected multigraph of bounded degree.
We say that
\mathcal{G}
is transient if all entries of
R_{\lambda_{0}}
are in
\mathbb{R}
and recurrent otherwise.
When
\mathcal{G}
is recurrent, we define

\delta_{u,v}:=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{n}}\left(\langle A^{n}e_{u},e_{v}\rangle\right)

We say that
\mathcal{G}
is
null-recurrent
if
\delta_{u,v}=0
and
positive recurrent
otherwise.
These properties are independent of the choice of the pair
u,v
of vertices.

The matrix
R_{\lambda}
satisfies
\lambda R_{\lambda}=AR_{\lambda}+I
. Hence, when
R_{\lambda}
has entries in the real numbers, one can think of its columns
R_{\lambda}e_{v}
as being ’almost’ eigenfunctions of
A
.

For the reader familiar with random walk, the terminology in the above definitions is
explained in the exercise below.

Exercise B.1.5

Assume that
\mathcal{G}
is a
d
-regular graph,
i
.
e
., each vertex has degree
d
.
Let
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
be a random walk in
\mathcal{G}
starting from a root vertex
o
,
i
.
e
.,
Z_{0}=0
, and where at each step the walk jumps randomly one of its neighbor with equal
probability. Let

\tau:=\inf\{n>0:Z_{n}=o\}

1.

Show that
\operatorname{P}(\tau<+\infty)=1
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is recurrent.

2.

Show that
\mathbb{E}(\tau)<+\infty
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is positive recurrent.

Infinite graphs of bounded degree which are recurrent can always be used to produce Hooper-Thurston-Veech surfaces:

Theorem B.1.6

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph of bounded degree which is recurrent. Then
there exists a positive solution to the equation
Af=\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{G})f
which
is unique up to scaling. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}
is positive-recurrent if and only if
this solution belongs to
\ell^{2}(V)
.

We refer to Theorem 6.2 in
[
MW89
]
and references therein for a proof of the result above.
For transient graphs, one can parametrize the positive eigenfunctions by the so-called
Martin boundary. We briefly recall this notion in what follows.

For
o\in V
a root vertex we define the
\lambda
-Martin kernel
:

K_{\lambda,o}:\begin{array}[]{lll}V^{2}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
(u,v)&\mapsto&\frac{R_{\lambda}(e_{v})(u)}{R_{\lambda}(e_{v})(o)}.\end{array}

Now, to a vertex
v
in
V
, we associate the function

\phi_{v}:u\to K_{\lambda,o}(u,v)
. This defines an embedding
of
V\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{V}
.
The Martin compactification
V_{\lambda}
is the smallest compactification of
V

to which all
\phi_{u}
extend continuously, or, equivalently, for which the embedding
V\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{V}
has a continuous extension.
The
\lambda
-Martin boundary
is
M_{\lambda}:=V_{\lambda}\setminus V
.
By construction, given
\zeta\in M_{\lambda}
the quantity
\phi_{v}(\zeta)
is
well defined. To obtain the correspondence that interest us we need to introduce the notion of minimal elements: these are points
\zeta\in M_{\lambda}
such that, whenever
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}\in M_{\lambda}
satisfty
tk_{\eta_{1}}+(1-t)k_{\eta_{2}}=k_{\zeta}
for some
0<t<1
, one has that
\zeta=\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
.

Theorem B.1.7

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a transient graph of bounded degree and
\lambda
such
either
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
or
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
.
Then the function

\begin{array}[]{lll}M_{\lambda}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{V}\\
\zeta&\mapsto&(v\mapsto\phi_{v}(\zeta))\end{array}

(B.2)

provides a bijection between the minimal points of the Martin boundary and the extremal positive

\lambda
-harmonic functions on
\mathcal{G}
which take value
1
at the root vertex
o
.

Corollary B.1.8

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph of bounded degree and
\lambda
such
either
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
or
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}
is transient.
Then there exists a positive
\lambda
-harmonic function on
\mathcal{G}
.

B.1.1
Minimal
\lambda
-harmonic functions of regular trees

In the following paragraphs we illustrate the correspondence between positive
\lambda
-harmonic eigenfunctions and points in the minimal Martin boundary when
2
2

2

The description of all positive
\lambda
-harmonic functions for the regular tree
\mathbb{T}_{2}
was carried out in Example
3.4.1

\mathcal{G}=\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
is an regular tree of degree
d=q+1
,
q\geq 2
. The following general result (see Appendix F in
[
Hoo15
]
and references therein) stablishes the correspondence between extremal positive
\lambda
-harmonic functions on
\mathbb{T}_{d}
and its Gromov boundary.

Theorem B.1.9

If
\mathcal{G}
is a hyperbolic infinite graph and
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
, then every point in the Martin boundary

M_{\lambda}
is minimal and homeomorphic to the Gromov boundary of
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{G}
is a transient tree, then
M_{\lambda_{0}}
is homeomorphic to the Gromov boundary of
\mathcal{G}
.

The graph
\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
is transient, hyperbolic and its Gromov boundary is homeomorphic to its space of ends, which in turn is homeomorphic to a Cantor set. We refer the reader to
[
Löh17
]
and references therein for a discussion on Gromov hyperbolic spaces.

Let
o\in\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
be a fixed vertex and
\omega\in\partial\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
be a point in its Gromov boundary. We identify
\omega
with a (equivalence class of) path
[x_{0}=o,x_{-1},x_{-2}\ldots]
going to infinity. The end
\omega
defines a partition
V(\mathbb{T}_{q+1})=\sqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{n}
into horocycles, where
o\in H_{0}
. Every vertex in
H_{n}
has a set of adjacent vertices formed by
q
vertices in
H_{n+1}
(which are ’further away’ from
\omega
) and one vertex in
H_{n-1}
(which is closer). From the work of Cartier (see Section 9 in
[
Car73
]
) we deduce that every minimal positive
\lambda
-harmonic function
h
is constant along horocycles and grows exponentially along the ray
[x_{0}=0,x_{1},\ldots]
. These facts, together with the equation
A\textbf{h}=\lambda\textbf{h}
, lead to the linear recurrence relation:

\lambda\textbf{h}_{n}=q\textbf{h}_{n+1}+\textbf{h}_{n-1}

Here
\textbf{h}_{n}
is the value of
h
along the horocyle
H_{n}
. The general solution of the equation above is of the form
\textbf{h}_{n}=Ar_{+}^{n}+Br_{-}^{n}
where
r_{+}=\frac{1}{2q}(\lambda+\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4q})
and
r_{-}=\frac{1}{2q}(\lambda-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-4q})
. Hence, we must have that
\lambda\geq 2\sqrt{q}
. For the extreme value

\lambda=2\sqrt{q}
the normalization
\textbf{h}(o)=1
leads to
\textbf{h}_{n}=r_{+}^{n}
. For
\lambda>2\sqrt{q}
the same normalization leads to
\textbf{h}_{n}=r_{+}^{n}
being the solution that corresponds to
\omega
, because
r_{-}^{n}
decreases exponentially as we move along
[x_{0}=o,x_{-1},x_{-2}\ldots]
. We finish this Section with an exercise for a motivated reader.

Exercise B.1.10

In the preceding paragraph, the functions
\textbf{h}_{+}
and
\textbf{h}_{-}
depend on the choice of a basepoint
o\in\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
and an end
\omega\in\partial\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
. Denote this dependency by
\textbf{h}_{+,o,\omega}
and
\textbf{h}_{-,o,\omega}
, respectively. Show that , if we normalize
\textbf{h}_{+,o,\omega}(o)=\textbf{h}_{-,o,\omega}=1
,
then there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
\partial\mathbb{T}_{q+1}
such that:

\textbf{h}_{-,o,\omega}=\int_{\partial\mathbb{T}_{q+1}}\textbf{h}_{+,o,\omega^{\prime}}d\mu(\omega^{\prime}).

B.2
Geometric structures

We briefly review the general notion of
(G,X)
-structure, developing map and holonomy. For a more detailed discussion, we refer the reader to Chapter 43 in
[
Thu97
]
.
Let
X
be a manifold and
G
a group acting on
X
by homeomorphisms. A
(G,X)
-structure on a manifold
M
is an atlas
\mathcal{A}=\{\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\to X\}
whose transition functions are restrictions of elements in
G
. The ideas leading to this notion seem to be as old as Poincaré’s studies of second order holomorphic ODEs on Riemann surfaces. For a detailed discussion on the history of
(G,X)
-structures, we refer the reader to
this post in Mathoverflow
3
3

3

https://mathoverflow.net/questions/332012/history-of-the-notion-of-g-x-structure
.
The following are examples of
(G,X)
-strutures appering in this book.

1.

A translation surface
M
is a
\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-structure on the complement of the set of conical singularities (see Definition
1.1.2
).

2.

A half-translation surface (
a.k.a.
quadratic differential)
M
is a
\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rtimes\{\pm 1\}
-structure on the complement of the set of conical singularities (see Definition
1.1.4
).

3.

A flat conical metric is a
\operatorname{Isom}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rtimes\operatorname{SO}(2)
-structure (defined on the complement of the conical singularities). For example, consider the flat conical metric on the sphere given by the triangular pillowcase

\mathbb{S}^{2}(P)
used in Example
2.2
.

4.

A dilation surface is a
\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rtimes\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-structure.

5.

Every rotational component (see
2.4.2
) admits a
\operatorname{Trans}(\mathbb{R})
-structure.

Developing maps and holonomy
. To construct the developing map of a
(G,X)
-structure on a manifold
M
we need the following technical condition: if
g,h
are two elements of
G
which coincide on a non-emtpy open set, then
g=h
. This is true for all examples considered above. The developing map:

dev:\widetilde{M}\to X,

(B.3)

where
\pi:\widetilde{M}\to M
is the universal cover, plays the role of a global coordinate chart for the
(G,X)
-structure. To define it remark first that if
\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\to X
are coordinate charts of
M
,
i=1,2
, such that
U_{1}\cap U_{2}\neq\emptyset
then by the technical condition above, there exists a unique
g\in G
such that
g\circ\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{1}
on
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
. This allows us to extend
\varphi_{1}
to a map
\varphi:U_{1}\cup U_{2}\to X
satisfying
\varphi=g\circ\varphi_{2}
on
U_{2}
and
\varphi=\varphi_{1}
on
U_{1}
. Using this principle, it is easy to see that each lift
\widetilde{\varphi_{1}}
of a chart of
M
to the universal cover defines a map
dev:\widetilde{M}\to X
extending
\widetilde{\varphi_{1}}
. This map is well defined up to the choice of the chart and its lift, and is called the
developing map
relative to the chart
\phi_{1}
. Different choices of charts and lifts define developing maps which differ by composition by an element of
G
. This can be expresed in the following equivariance condition:

dev\circ\gamma=hol(\gamma)\circ dev

(B.4)

for each deck transformation of the universal cover
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
. Here

hol:\pi_{1}(M)\to G

is a homomorphism called the
holonomy
(representation) of the
(G,X)
-structure. Remark that
h
is also well defined, up to conjugation by an element of
G
.
